Control of McKean–Vlasov SDEs with Contagion Through Killing at a State-Dependent Intensity


Abstract
We consider a novel McKean–Vlasov control problem with contagion through killing of particles and common noise. Each particle is killed at an exponential rate according to an intensity process that increases whenever the particle is located in a specific region. The removal of a particle pushes others towards the removal region, which can trigger cascades that see particles exiting the system in rapid succession. We study the control of such a system by a central agent who intends to preserve particles at minimal cost. Our theoretical contribution is twofold. Firstly, we rigorously justify the McKean–Vlasov control problem as the limit of a corresponding controlled finite particle system. Our proof is based on a controlled martingale problem and tightness arguments. Secondly, we connect our framework with models in which particles are killed once they hit the boundary of the removal region. We show that these models appear in the limit as the exponential rate tends to infinity. As a corollary, we obtain new existence results for McKean–Vlasov SDEs with singular interaction through hitting times which extend those in the established literature. We conclude the paper with numerical investigations of our model applied to government control of systemic risk in financial systems.



Ben Hambly and Philipp Jettkant

Mathematical Institute, University of Oxford

August 8, 2026

1
Introduction

McKean–Vlasov control problems arise naturally as the infinite population limit of finite systems of controlled particles interacting through their empirical distribution. The dynamics of a representative particle in the infinite population limit are described by a McKean–Vlasov stochastic differential equation (SDE). Along with its close companion mean-field games, introduced independently by Lasry and Lions
[
32
]
and Caines, Huang, and Malhamé
[
4
]
, McKean–Vlasov control has received widespread attention in recent years
[
2
,
33
,
8
,
38
,
14
,
7
]
.

In this paper, we study the optimal control of a McKean–Vlasov SDE that features contagion through killing of particles in the presence of a common noise
W^{0}
. The state of the representative particle is described by a diffusion process
X=(X_{t})_{t\geq 0}
on the real line with McKean–Vlasov dynamics. The particle has a cumulative intensity process
\Lambda=(\Lambda_{t})_{t\leq 0}
that increases according to a rate
\lambda(X_{t})
whenever the state
X_{t}
drops below zero. Once the accumulated intensity
\Lambda_{t}
exceeds a critical threshold, modelled by a standard exponential random variable
\theta
, the particle is removed from the system. Hence, the underlying McKean–Vlasov SDE does not depend on the conditional law
\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|W^{0})
of all particles but rather on the conditional subprobability distribution
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
of the remaining particles. Furthermore, the removal of a particle feeds back into the system by pushing the remaining particles instantaneously closer to the removal region
(-\infty,0)
. We refer to this mechanism as contagion. The force of the contagion is proportional to the fraction of killed particles
L_{t}=\mathbb{P}(\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
, called the loss.

Our goal is to connect this McKean–Vlasov control problem with the control of a corresponding finite particle system. In the uncontrolled setting without killing or contagion, it is well-known that as the particle system’s population size
N
tends to infinity, the particles become independent and their empirical measure converges to the law of the solution of a McKean–Vlasov SDE – the so-called mean-field limit. This phenomenon is known as propagation of chaos
[
27
,
36
,
41
]
. For controlled systems, however, the optimisation step makes establishing the convergence to the McKean–Vlasov control problem more challenging. A general approach to resolve this issue uses a relaxed formulation for the control problem and an associated martingale problem. This technique was applied in
[
30
]
to study a broad class of McKean–Vlasov control problems and extended in
[
13
]
to systems with common noise.

The existing literature, however, does not cover our model because of the presence of the killing and contagion mechanisms. On the level of the particle system, killing leads to a sudden loss of mass in the empirical measure of the remaining particles, while the contagion mechanism introduces jumps into the particles’ trajectories. These innovations bring about technical challenges that we need to address in this work. The first is that the control
\gamma^{N,i}=(\gamma^{N,i}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
acting on particle
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
may depend on the associated critical threshold
\theta_{i}\sim\text{Exp}(1)
, because, naturally, the controller takes the killing of particles into account. However, to reap the benefits of the regularisation of the mean-field limit afforded by the exponential random variable
\theta
, we must show that the dependencies between the controls
(\gamma^{N,i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
and the thresholds
(\theta_{i})_{1\leq i\leq N}
vanish as
N\to\infty
. This requires a careful setup and analysis of the particle system, but ultimately allows us to conclude that the discontinuities present in the particle system vanish in the infinite population limit. The second challenge lies in showing the weak convergence of the particles’ killing times
\tau^{N}_{i}
. Using the continuous mapping theorem we can reduce this convergence to the continuity of the killing time
\tau
of the representative particle in the mean-field limit as a function of its state
X
. To establish this continuity we prove that the representative particle cannot be located on the boundary
\{0\}
of the removal region at the time of killing by studying the interplay between the set
\{t\in[0,T]\mathpunct{:}X_{t}=0\}
of zeros of
X
and the accumulated intensity
\Lambda
.

1.1
Related Literature

The removal mechanism in our work is inspired by a mean-field model of loss from default in large portfolios
[
17
,
18
]
. This framework follows a reduced-form approach and directly models the instantaneous default intensity
\lambda_{t}
of constituents in a portfolio, whereas in our model the intensity depends on the underlying state process
X
. In both cases, however, killing occurs when the cumulative intensity
\Lambda_{t}
exceeds an exponential random variable and feeds back into the remaining system.
[
17
]
proves propagation of chaos for the system of intensity processes by proving that their empirical measure converges to the solution of a nonlinear partial differential equation which describes the evolution of the mean-field limit.
[
18
]
extends the result to the common noise setting. Subsequent works study the central limit theorem
[
39
]
, large deviations
[
40
]
, and parameter estimation
[
16
]
for the mean-field model. The arguments in
[
17
,
18
]
no longer apply once controls enter the picture, so we cannot draw on their ideas in this paper.

The removal according to a state-dependent intensity
\lambda(X_{t})
can at least heuristically be viewed as a regularisation of models with absorption. In these models, a member of the population is killed (or absorbed) once its state
X_{t}
hits the negative half-line. Formally, this corresponds to the choice
\lambda(x)=\infty\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)
. McKean–Vlasov SDEs with absorption have been studied by various authors and we can distinguish between two strands within the literature. The first considers “smooth” interaction through hitting times, i.e. the feedback through the loss
L_{t}
, which measures the number of killed particles up to time
t
, may appear in the drift and possibly the diffusion coefficient of the state equation
[
19
,
21
,
5
,
6
,
3
,
22
]
. In the “singular” variant the loss
L_{t}
acts instantaneously, as it does in our model, and appears as a finite variation term in the dynamics of the representative particle
[
37
,
20
,
11
,
34
,
12
]
.

A complication that arises once controls are introduced in the system with absorption is that the population’s response as a function of the control is merely continuous if the interaction through the hitting times is smooth and can even exhibit discontinuities in the singular setting. Thus, classical methods of optimal control such as the stochastic maximum principle and the value function approach are not applicable. This shortcoming is our main motivation for studying the regularised model and explains why to date there is only a single publication
[
10
]
that studies the control of absorbing McKean-Vlasov systems.
Nonetheless, we are able to connect both frameworks by showing that the regularised model converges in a suitable sense to the absorption model with singular interaction as the intensity function
\lambda
approaches
\infty\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}
(cf. Theorem
2.14
). As a corollary, we obtain a novel existence result for McKean–Vlasov SDEs with singular interaction through hitting times in the presence of common noise which extends those in the established literature
[
37
,
34
,
22
]
.

The paper
[
10
]
, mentioned above, analyses the optimal control of absorbing dynamics with singular interaction, interpreted as a model for government bailouts (similar to the financial model we describe in Section
1.2
). Leveraging the specific structure of their chosen cost functional, they deduce the convergence of the corresponding controlled particle system to the McKean–Vlasov control problem through tightness arguments. Their proof technique does not extend to models with nonlinear dependence on the control, so is not applicable to our work. As a consequence their framework is less flexible than ours and, in particular, does not include common noise. For their numerical computations, they rely on an alternative regularisation procedure, which traps particles at zero with high probability instead of absorbing them. Their numerical results are broadly in line with ours and show that the optimal control is of bang-bang type, meaning that the central agent only intervenes if a particle’s state nears the killing region.

Yet another regularisation technique, first introduced in
[
21
]
, was recently analysed in
[
22
]
from the convergence angle. The regularisation proceeds by mollification of the instantaneous action of the loss
L_{t}
, which leads to a model with smooth but path-dependent interaction through hitting times.
[
22
]
shows that one recovers the singular model in the limit as the mollification tends to zero. That paper does not touch on controlled versions of the problem.

Finally, let us mention the “Up the River” problem formulated by Aldous
[
1
]
, where a unit drift is distributed among a finite number of Brownian particles on the positive half-line which are absorbed at zero. The goal is to keep as many particles as possible alive for all times.
[
42
]
shows that the asymptotically optimal strategy as
N\to\infty
is to simply push the laggard, i.e. to allocate the entire drift to the particle closest to the absorbing boundary.

1.2
A Model for Government Interventions in Financial Systems

One application of our framework is in the modelling of systemic risk and government interventions in financial markets. In this context, the members of the finite particle system, labelled
i=1
, …,
N
, represent financial institutions with mutual obligations or common exposures, for instance commercial banks in an interbank lending market or hedge funds investing in government bonds. In the case of commercial banks, the state
X^{i}_{t}
measures an institution’s level of equity at time
t
. Whenever the equity breaches a given threshold, the institution’s default intensity becomes positive. Default occurs once the accumulated intensity
\Lambda^{i}_{t}
exceeds the exponential random variable
\theta_{i}
. A defaulted entity does not pay back its obligations to its creditors in full, which diminishes the equity of the crediting institutions. This may trigger further defaults that wipe out more equity, and so on. In this way, a large number of financial institutions can go bankrupt in a short period of time with devastating consequences for the economy. To prevent the development of such default cascades a government or another central authority, such as a central bank, may intervene in the financial system, either by supporting market activity, e.g. through quantitative easing, or by bailing out institutions outright. We can formulate such an intervention as an optimal control problem, in which the central agent weighs the negative externalities of market interference, such as moral hazard or higher inflation, with the cost of a potential systemic crisis.

A simple model for government interventions in financial systems could read as follows: the processes
X^{i}=(X^{i}_{t})_{t\geq 0}
, called
distance-to-breach
, have the dynamics

X^{i}_{t}=(X^{i}_{0}-c)+\int_{0}^{t}\gamma^{i}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\sigma W^{i}_{t}+\sigma_{0}W^{0}_{t}-\alpha L^{N}_{t},\quad\Lambda^{i}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(X^{i}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

where
W^{0}
,
W^{1}
, …,
W^{N}
are independent Brownian motions,
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
are capital injections from the government, and
L^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{\theta\leq\Lambda^{i}_{t}}
denotes the fraction of banks that have defaulted up to time
t\geq 0
. The diffusion
\sigma W^{i}_{t}+\sigma_{0}W^{0}
represents fluctuations in the value of assets held by the bank, which can be correlated among institutions through the common noise
W^{0}
, and
\alpha
measures the size of the banks’ mutual obligations. The random variable
X^{i}_{0}
stands for a bank’s initial capital position and
c>0
is a capital buffer. If we choose the intensity function
\lambda
to be
\lambda(x)=\lambda_{0}x_{-}
for a positive constant
\lambda_{0}
, then a bank is at risk of default once the equity breaches the buffer
c
. We capture the government’s objective through the cost

J^{N}(\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T\land\tau_{i}}w\gamma^{i}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+L^{N}_{T}\biggr],

where
T>0
is some finite time horizon and
w
weighs the negative externalities of a bailout with the cost of a systemic crisis as measured by the fraction of defaulted entities at time
T
. We provide numerical illustrations for this model in Section
5
.

Let us briefly note that there are other applications of our framework, for instance it could also be used to model opinion dynamics in advertising or political campaigns. Here the particles are the target audience of the campaign and the state
X^{i}_{t}
represents an audience member’s level of satisfaction with a product or a political candidate. If people’s satisfaction wanes, for instance because of a damaging news report, they abandon the product or candidate, which negatively impacts other people’s views, who are in turn more likely to change their opinion.
In response, the product originator or political candidate may attempt to shore up confidence by increasing their spending on advertisements to maintain people’s satisfaction.

1.3
Main Contributions and Structure of the Paper

This paper makes three main contributions. Firstly, in Section
2
we introduce a new and flexible mean-field control model which features killing of particles and a contagion mechanism. Ours is the first work that considers these features in such a general setting and, in particular, in the presence of common noise.

Secondly, in Section
3
we prove the convergence of the controlled particle system for the regularised model to the corresponding McKean–Vlasov control problem. We achieve this by introducing a relaxed formulation of the McKean–Vlasov control problem (cf. Subsection
2.1
) and expressing the underlying McKean–Vlasov SDE for the state process as a controlled martingale problem. In Subsections
3.1
to
3.3
we show that any (approximately) optimally controlled sequence of particle systems converges subsequentially to a solution of this martingale problem. As such the limiting solution yields a control for the relaxed formulation, which is easily shown to be optimal. In other words, the controlled particle system converges to the optimum of the relaxed McKean–Vlasov control problem. To establish this convergence we carefully analyse the dependence of the particle system on the exponential random variables
\theta_{1}
, …,
\theta_{N}
and prove the continuity of the killing time
\tau
as a function of the state
X
in the mean-field limit. Finally, in Subsection
3.4
and
3.5
, using the theory from Djete, Possamaï, and Tan
[
13
]
, we show that the relaxed formulation is equivalent to the original control problem, which concludes the argument.

Our third contribution is to connect the regularised framework with the absorbing model in Section
4
. As for the regularised model, we adopt a relaxed formulation (cf. Subsection
2.2
) and introduce a suitable martingale problem. Then, in Subsections
4.1
to
4.3
we show that any sequence of regularised models with intensity functions
\lambda^{n}(x)
, that tend to
\infty\mathbf{1}_{(-\infty,0)}(x)
in a suitable sense, subsequentially converges to a solution of the martingale problem. This, in particular, implies a novel and very general existence theorem for McKean–Vlasov SDEs with singular interaction through hitting times in the presence of common noise, that extends existing results
[
37
,
34
,
22
]
. However, in contrast to the regularised model, we cannot in general conclude that the relaxed control for the absorbing model, induced by the solution to the martingale problem, is optimal. This is due to the lack of uniqueness for McKean–Vlasov SDEs with singular interaction through hitting times
[
37
,
20
,
34
]
. Only if we revert to a simpler setting, in which the underlying McKean–Vlasov SDE has constant coefficients and the cost functions satisfy certain monotonicity properties, can we deduce that the limiting control is indeed optimal, see Section
4.4
.

In Section
5
we provide numerical simulations for the McKean–Vlasov control problem arising from our model for government interventions in financial systems.

2
Main Results

In this section, we state and explain the main results of this paper.

2.1
Convergence of the Nearly Optimally Controlled Particle System

We introduce a general particle system for our contagion model. As before the state space of the particle will be one-dimensional (excluding the cumulative intensity). This is sufficient to highlight the novel features of the model and is in line with the majority of the literature on McKean–Vlasov SDEs with interaction through hitting times, which inspired our work. Most results that concern the convergence of the nearly optimally controlled particle system to the regularised model generalise straightforwardly to higher dimensions. The only step where we explicitly use the one-dimensional setup is when we prove the convergence of the particles’ killing times as
N\to\infty
. Nonetheless, under suitable assumptions on the intensity function
\lambda
, these arguments can be transferred to higher dimensions. We comment on this in Remark
3.8
. In what follows we assume that the processes
X^{i}
and
\Lambda^{i}
,
i=1
…,
N
, follow the dynamics

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X^{i}_{t}&=b(t,X^{i}_{t},\nu^{N}_{t},\gamma^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{i}_{t},\nu^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,X^{i}_{t},\nu^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}\\
&\ \ \ -\alpha(t,X^{i}_{t-},\nu^{N}_{t-})\,\mathrm{d}L^{N}_{t},\\
\mathrm{d}\Lambda^{i}_{t}&=\lambda(t,X^{i}_{t},\nu^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(2.1)

with initial conditions
X^{i}_{0}=\xi_{i}\sim\nu_{0}
and
\Lambda^{i}_{0}=0
. The driving noises
W^{0}
,
W^{1}
, …,
W^{N}
are Brownian motions and we introduce the
empirical subprobability distribution

\nu^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}I^{i}_{t}\delta_{X^{i}_{t}}

(2.2)

as well as the
loss

L^{N}_{t}=1-\nu^{N}_{t}(\mathbb{R})
, where
I^{i}_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\theta_{i}>\Lambda^{i}_{t}}
and
\theta_{1}
, …,
\theta_{N}
each follow a standard exponential distribution. The indicator process
I^{i}
models the killing of particles, which are triggered once
\Lambda^{i}_{t}\geq\theta_{i}
.

We assume that the collection
((\xi_{i},\theta_{i},W^{i})_{i},W^{0})
is i.i.d. and introduce the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{N}=(\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
defined by

\mathcal{F}^{N}_{t}=\sigma\bigl(\xi_{i},\,\theta_{i},\,W^{i}_{s},\,W^{0}_{s}\mathpunct{:}0\leq s\leq t,\,i=0,\dots,N\bigr).

The processes
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
are
controls
, which take values in a non-empty, closed, and convex subset
G
of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
. Characterising the set of admissible controls is slightly subtle. First, let us note that if
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
are
\mathbb{F}^{N}
-progressively measurable and satisfy
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
, then there exists a unique strong solution to SDE (
2.1
) (see the discussion at the outset of Section
3.1
). Now, we call
\mathbb{F}^{N}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty

admissible controls (for the particle system)
if they are progressively measurable with respect to the filtration generated by the collection
((\xi_{i},W^{i})_{i=1,\dots,N},W^{0})
and the processes
(\langle\eta^{N}_{t},\varphi\rangle)_{t\geq 0}
, for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T])\times D[0,T])
, where
\eta^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\xi_{i},W^{i}_{\cdot\land t},I^{i}_{\cdot\land t}}
. Here the space
D[0,T]
of càdlàg functions from
[0,T]
to
\mathbb{R}
is equipped with the
J1
-topology.

Remark 2.1
.

The definition ensures that admissible controls depend on
\theta_{1}
, …,
\theta_{N}
only through the information revealed by the indicator processes
I^{1}
, …,
I^{N}
up to the current time, and that the dependence is symmetric. Note that the evolution of the indicator processes depends on the controls themselves so that with respect to
I^{1}
, …,
I^{N}
admissible controls are in feedback form. This feature makes the definition slightly awkward but is necessary to ensure that the controller cannot anticipate the killing of particles. The symmetry in the dependence on the indicator processes is crucial to ensure that
\theta_{1}
, …,
\theta_{N}
become independent of the controls as
N\to\infty
, which leads to simplifications in the mean-field limit. In particular, the loss
L^{N}
becomes continuous, even differentiable, in the limit.

The central planner attempts to minimise the
cost functional

J^{N}(\gamma^{1},\dots,\gamma^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{\tau_{i}\land T}f(t,X^{i}_{t},\nu^{N}_{t},\gamma^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu^{N}_{T})\biggr]

(2.3)

for some final time horizon
T>0
over admissible controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
. Here
\tau_{i}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\Lambda^{i}_{t}\geq\theta_{i}\}
is the killing time of particle
i
. We denote the infimum of
J^{N}
over admissible controls by
V^{N}
and call it the
value
. Our goal is to study the behaviour of the controlled particle system as
N
tends to infinity.

First, let us discuss the coefficients of SDE (
2.1
) as well as the cost functions in Equation (
2.3
). For
p\geq 1
, we denote by
\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
the space of
p
-integrable subprobability measures. That is, the space of measures
\nu
on
\mathbb{R}
for which
\nu(\mathbb{R})\leq 1
and
M_{p}^{p}(\nu)=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lvert x\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\nu(x)<\infty
. We equip
\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
with the metric

d_{p}(\nu_{1},\nu_{2})=W_{p}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2})+\lvert\nu_{1}(\mathbb{R})-\nu_{2}(\mathbb{R})\rvert,

(2.4)

where
\mu_{i}=\nu_{i}+(1-\nu_{i}(\mathbb{R}))\delta_{0}
,
W_{p}
denotes the
p
-Wasserstein distance for probability measures on
\mathbb{R}
, and
\nu_{1}
,
\nu_{2}\in\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
. Clearly, the
loss function

\mathcal{M}^{p}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to[0,1]
,
\nu\mapsto 1-\nu(\mathbb{R})
is continuous, in fact
1
-Lipschitz continuous, with respect to the metric
d_{p}
.
Let us now state the assumptions on the coefficients and cost functions for the particle system.

Assumption 2.2
.

Let
b
and
f\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G\to\mathbb{R}
and
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
,
\alpha
, and
\lambda\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\psi\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
be measurable. Let
G
be a non-empty, closed, and convex subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
\nu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
. We assume there exists constants
C>0
and
p_{f}>2
, such that

(i)

the coefficients
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\alpha
are bounded by
C
and for all
t
,
x
,
\nu
,
g
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b(t,x,\nu,g)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(\nu)+\lvert g\rvert),\quad\ \ \ \lvert\lambda(t,x,\nu)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(\nu));

(ii)

the coefficient
b
is continuous in
g
and for
h\in\{\sigma,\sigma_{0},\alpha,\lambda\}
and all
t
,
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
\nu
,
\nu^{\prime}
,
g
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b(t,x,\nu,g)-b(t,x^{\prime},\nu^{\prime},g)\rvert+\lvert h(t,x,\nu)-h(t,x^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\rvert\leq C(\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert+d_{1}(\nu,\nu^{\prime}));

(iii)

for all
t
,
\nu
we have
\lambda(t,x,\nu)>0
if
x<0
and
\lambda(t,x,\nu)=0
otherwise;

(iv)

for all
t
,
x
,
\nu
,
g
we have

\lvert f(t,x,\nu,g)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\nu)+\lvert g\rvert^{2}),\qquad\lvert\psi(\nu)\rvert\leq C(1+M_{2}^{2}(\nu));

(v)

the running cost
f
is continuous in
x
,
\nu
,
g
and the terminal cost
\psi
is continuous;

(vi)

for all
t
,
x
,
\nu
,
g
we have
f(t,x,\nu,g)\geq-C(1+\lvert x\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\nu))+C\lvert g\rvert^{p_{f}}
.

We will often use subscripts to indicate constants for a specific coefficient or cost function. E.g. we may write
\lvert b(t,x,\nu,g)\rvert\leq C_{b}(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(\nu)+\lvert g\rvert)
.

Remark 2.3
.

Some remarks about Assumption
2.2
are in order.
First note that even though the functions in Assumption
2.2
are defined for elements in
\nu\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, the continuity statements in
\nu
are understood with respect to the metric
d_{1}
. Since
d_{1}\leq d_{2}
on
\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, continuity (as well as Lipschitz continuity) with respect to
d_{1}
is stronger than that with respect to
d_{2}
. We need this more restrictive assumption to obtain existence and uniqueness of the mean-field limit (
2.5
) of the particle system (see Proposition
3.12
). Otherwise the growth and continuity conditions, Items
(i)
,
(ii)
,
(iv)
, and
(v)
, are standard.

The nondegeneracy condition Assumption
2.2

(iii)
ensures that the killing times
\tau_{i}
converge weakly to the corresponding killing time of the mean-field limit (see Lemma
3.7
). Note that we do not require nondegeneracy of the diffusion coefficients, which is in stark contrast to the models with interaction through hitting times, see Assumption
2.13

(ii)
below.

Lastly, the coercivity from Assumption
2.2

(vi)
implies tightness of sequences of (approximately) optimal relaxed controls. This condition holds, for instance, if
G
is bounded.

Our goal is to prove that under Assumption
2.2
the optimal cost or value
V^{N}
of the
N
-particle system converges to the value of a mean-field control problem as the number of particles becomes infinite. If we suppose that the particles become asymptotically independent given the common noise
W^{0}
, we expect that
\nu^{N}_{t}
converges weakly to the measure
\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
. This suggests the
mean-field limit

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X_{t}&=b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}-\alpha(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}L_{t},\\
\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{t}&=\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(2.5)

with initial conditions
X_{0}=\xi\sim\nu_{0}
and
\Lambda_{0}=0
, Brownian motions
W
and
W^{0}
,
conditional subprobability distribution

\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
, and
loss

L_{t}=1-\nu_{t}(\mathbb{R})
. The control
\gamma
is an
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable process with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. We refer to controls satisfying these two conditions as
admissible strong controls
. Lastly, we require that
\theta
is independent of all other variables and, hence, purely auxiliary. Indeed, if
\theta
is independent of
X
,
W
,
W^{0}
, and
\gamma
, a simple computation shows
\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{t}}\varphi(X_{t})|W^{0}]
for any bounded measurable
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, so we can define
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
without recourse to
\theta
. This in particular implies that

L_{t}=1-\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{t}}|W^{0}]=\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}[e^{-\Lambda_{s}}\lambda(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})|W^{0}]\,\mathrm{d}s=\int_{0}^{t}\langle\nu_{s},\lambda(s,\cdot,\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s,

so we can rewrite the first line of (
2.5
) in the more standard form

\displaystyle\mathrm{d}X_{t}

\displaystyle=b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}

\displaystyle\ \ \ -\alpha(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\langle\nu_{t},\lambda(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t.

Since
x\mapsto\lambda(t,x,v)
may be of linear growth, the mapping
\nu\mapsto\alpha(t,x,v)\langle v,\lambda(t,\cdot,v)\rangle
is in general only locally Lipschitz continuous. However, the boundedness of
\alpha
ensures at most linear growth in
v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, so that we can guarantee strong existence and uniqueness for the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
). Here we call
(X,\nu)
a
strong solution
to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) if
X
is
\mathbb{F}^{X_{0},W,W^{0}}
-adapted and
\nu_{t}
is the conditional subprobability distribution of
X_{t}
with respect to
W^{0}
, i.e. there is no additional external information in the conditioning. Below we will encounter weak solutions of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) for which this is not necessarily the case.

The
cost functional
for the mean-field limit is

J(\gamma)=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{\tau\land T}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\Lambda_{t}}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr],

(2.6)

where
\tau=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\Lambda_{t}\geq\theta\}
. We denote the
value
, i.e. the infimum of the cost functional over admissible controls, by
V
. The following theorem establishes the convergence of the values
V^{N}
to
V
.

Theorem 2.4
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Then the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) has a unique strong solution for any admissible control
\gamma
. Moreover, the value
V^{N}
of the
N
-particle system converges to the value
V
of the mean-field limit.

What the theorem leaves open is whether the particle system itself converges when the controls for the particles are chosen optimally. Since it is difficult to establish existence of optimal controls for the particle system, owing to the nonstandard setup of the particle system (cf. Remark
2.1
) as well as the singular jump term
L^{N}
and the subprobability
\nu^{N}
, which do not vary smoothly with the controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
, we make the following definition.

Definition 2.5
.

Fix a sequence
(\epsilon_{N})_{N}
of positive real numbers with
\epsilon_{N}\to 0
as
N\to\infty
. We say that a sequence of admissible controls
(\gamma^{N,1}
, …,
\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
is
\epsilon_{N}
-optimal
if
J^{N}(\gamma^{N,1}
, …,
\gamma^{N,N})\leq V^{N}+\epsilon_{N}
, where
V^{N}
is the optimal cost achievable for the system with
N
particles.

In the definition, we made the dependence of the controls on the number of particles explicit. We will adopt this notation whenever necessary to avoid confusion.

To prove the convergence of
\epsilon_{N}
-optimally controlled particle systems, we will employ compactness arguments. Note that it is generally not possible to achieve tightness of the random variables
((X^{N,i},\gamma^{N,i})_{i},\nu^{N})_{N}
, in particular the controls
(\gamma^{N,1}
, …,
\gamma^{N,N})
, without relaxing the formulation of the control problem. Moreover, subsequential limits of the subprobabilities
(\nu^{N})_{N}
typically fail to be adapted to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
generated by the common noise
W^{0}
. So in addition we have to consider weak solutions to the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
), where we allow the mean-field component
\nu
to be adapted to a filtration which extends
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
in an appropriate sense.

Let us make this more concrete. We set
\mathbf{M}=\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
and equip
D[0,T]
and
D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
with the
J1
-topology. Next, we introduce the space
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
of square-integrable measures on
[0,T]\times G
with the Lebesgue measure as the time-marginal, topologised by the
2
-Wasserstein distance. We can identify an admissible strong control
\gamma
with an
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variable
\Gamma
, called a relaxed control, via
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma_{t}}\mathrm{d}t
. We define the Polish spaces
\mathcal{S}=D[0,T]\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\times[0,\infty)
and
\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})\times D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]\times C([0,T])
. The space
\mathcal{S}
can accommodate the variables
X
,
W
,
\Gamma
, and
\theta
and the meaning of
\Omega_{0}
will become clear in a moment. Finally, we set
\Omega_{\ast}=\mathcal{S}\times\Omega_{0}
and let
\mathcal{F}_{\ast}=\mathcal{B}(\Omega_{\ast})
be its Borel
\sigma
-algebra. We denote the canonical random element on
\Omega_{\ast}
by
\Theta^{\ast}=(X^{\ast},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast},\theta^{\ast},\mu^{\ast},\nu^{\ast},B^{\ast})
and set
\Theta^{0}=(\mu^{\ast},\nu^{\ast},B^{\ast})
.
As alluded to above, the process
X^{\ast}
will play the role of the weak solution to the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
),
W^{\ast}
and
B^{\ast}
are the idiosyncratic and common noise,
\theta^{\ast}
is the exponential time,
\nu^{\ast}
will be the mean-field component, and
\Gamma^{\ast}
is a relaxed control. We explain
\mu^{\ast}
next: to any probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
, we can associate a distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
by setting

\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A\times A_{0})=\int_{A_{0}}m(A)\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}(m,v,b)

(2.7)

for Borel subsets
A\subset\mathcal{S}
and
A_{0}\subset\Omega_{0}
. We denote the expectation with respect to
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
by
\mathbb{E}_{\ast}
. This construction implies that

\mu^{\ast}=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast},\theta^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast},\theta^{\ast}|\mu^{\ast}),

where
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}
is the law under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
.

We consider two filtrations
\mathbb{F}^{0}=(\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}=(\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t}&=\sigma\bigl(\langle\mu^{\ast}_{s},\varphi_{1}\rangle,\,\langle\nu^{\ast}_{s},\varphi_{2}\rangle,\,B^{\ast}_{s}\mathpunct{:}\varphi_{1}\in C_{b}(\mathcal{S}),\,\varphi_{2}\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R}),\,0\leq s\leq t\bigr),\\
\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t}&=\sigma\bigl(X^{\ast}_{s},\,W^{\ast}_{s},\,\Gamma^{\ast}([0,s]\times A)\mathpunct{:}A\in\mathcal{B}(G),\,0\leq s\leq t\bigr)\lor\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t}.\end{split}

(2.8)

Here
\mu^{\ast}_{t}
is the pushforward of
\mu^{\ast}
under the map
\mathcal{S}\ni(x,w,\mathfrak{g},p)\mapsto(x_{\cdot\land t},w,\mathfrak{g}_{t},p)
, where for
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
,
\mathfrak{g}_{t}
is defined through

\mathfrak{g}_{t}(A_{1}\times A_{2})=\mathfrak{g}((A_{1}\cap[0,t])\times A_{2})+\lvert A_{1}\cap(t,T]\rvert\delta_{g_{0}}(A_{2})

for measurable
A_{1}\subset[0,T]
and
A_{2}\subset G
and some fixed
g_{0}\in G
. In particular,

\mu^{\ast}_{t}=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{\cdot\land t},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast}_{t},\theta^{\ast}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{\cdot\land t},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast}_{t},\theta^{\ast}|\mu^{\ast})

(2.9)

under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
. Note that the map
D[0,T]\ni x\mapsto x_{\cdot\land t}
is measurable with respect to the Borel
\sigma
-algebra induced by the
J1
-topology, since the latter is generated by the projection maps
D[0,T]\ni x\mapsto x_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
. Hence, the pushforward is well-defined.

Remark 2.6
.

Equality (
2.9
) is a strengthening of the
immersion property
. The filtration
\mathbb{F}^{0}
is said to be
immersed in

\mathbb{F}^{\ast}

under

\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
if
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t})=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t}
. Equation (
2.9
) implies that the previous equality even holds for all sets
A\in\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t}\lor\sigma(W^{\ast})
. This property is essential to show that the relaxed control formulation from Definition
2.7
below yields the same value as the strong control problem introduced above Theorem
2.4
, see
[
13
, Remark 2.4]
for more details.

Definition 2.7
.

We call
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
an
admissible relaxed control rule
with initial condition
\nu_{0}\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
if

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{0},\theta^{\ast})=\nu_{0}\otimes\text{Exp}(1)
and
\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert X^{\ast}_{t}\rvert^{2}+\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)
is finite;

(ii)

W^{\ast}
and
B^{\ast}
are
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-Brownian motions, the pair
(X^{\ast}_{0},W^{\ast})
is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
, and
\theta^{\ast}
is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{T}
all under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
;

(iii)

for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}X^{\ast}_{t}=X^{\ast}_{0}&+\int_{[0,t]\times G}b(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(s,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W^{\ast}_{s}\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{0}(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}B^{\ast}_{s}-\alpha(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\langle\nu^{\ast},\lambda(s,\cdot,\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(2.10)

\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
, and
\nu^{\ast}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X^{\ast}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta^{\ast}>\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
hold
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-almost surely.

Here
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is the probability measure on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
via (
2.7
).

This definition borrows elements from Definition 2.5 in
[
13
]
. However, in contrast to
[
13
]
, Equation (
2.9
) is built into our probabilistic setup and not a postulate of Definition
2.7
.

The
cost functional

J_{0}
for relaxed control rules
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
is defined as

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})&=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\biggl[\int_{[0,\tau^{\ast}\land T]\times G}f(t,X^{\ast}_{t},\nu^{\ast}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)+\psi(\nu^{\ast}_{T})\biggr]\\
&=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times G}e^{-\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}}f(t,X^{\ast}_{t},\nu^{\ast}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)+\psi(\nu^{\ast}_{T})\biggr].\end{split}

(2.11)

Here
\tau^{\ast}=\inf\{0<t\leq T\mathpunct{:}\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}\geq\theta^{\ast}\}
with the convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. We denote the infimum of
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
over admissible relaxed control rules, called the
value
, by
V_{0}
. Since every admissible control
\gamma
together with the corresponding unique strong solution
X
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) induces an admissible relaxed control rule in
\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
(cf.
[
14
, Lemma 4.4]
), we obtain the trivial inequality
V_{0}\leq V
. Here as above, for any
G
-valued process
\gamma
with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
we can define a
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variable
\Gamma
by
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma_{t}}(g)\mathrm{d}t
. We call
\Gamma
the
relaxed control associated to

\gamma
.

Theorem 2.8
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Let
(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
be an
\epsilon_{N}
-optimal sequence of controls for the particle system and denote the associated relaxed controls by
\Gamma^{N,1}
, …,
\Gamma^{N,N}
. Next define
\mu^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{(X^{N,i},W^{i},\Gamma^{N,i},\theta_{i})}
. Then the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
is tight on
\Omega_{0}
and every subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
yields an admissible relaxed control rule. Moreover, the rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is optimal and
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})=V_{0}=V
.

The last equation in the theorem states that the value of the strong and relaxed formulation coincide. Our proof draws on
[
13
]
which studies the equivalence of different formulations for mean-field control.

Because of the appearance of the quantity
e^{-\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}}
in
J_{0}
, we do not expect the effective running cost
(t,x,p,\nu,g)\mapsto e^{-p}f(t,x,\nu,g)
to be jointly convex in
(x,p,g)
– even for simple choices of
f
, such as
f(t,x,\nu,g)=\lvert g\rvert^{2}
. Consequently, we cannot prove that optimal controls are unique so the subsequential convergence of the
\epsilon_{N}
-optimally controlled particle system is the best we can do. However, in the absence of controls, we can establish the following result that extends the well-known propagation of chaos to our framework.

Corollary 2.9
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied and further suppose that the drift coefficient does not depend on the control, i.e.
b(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}(t,x,v)
for some function
b_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
. Then
(X^{N,1},\nu^{N})_{N}
converges weakly on
D[0,T]\times D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
to the unique strong solution of the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
).

2.2
Convergence to the Singular Limit

The McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) can be viewed as a regularisation of a model with absorption and singular interaction through the hitting time. In this model particles are not killed according to an exponential clock but are removed from the system once their state hits zero. It is well-known that in this case, the system may exhibit jumps, which originate from discontinuities in the loss: a macroscopic portion of the system is removed at once (cf.
[
20
, Theorem 1.1]
). Under suitable assumptions (see Assumption
2.13
) we can obtain the singular framework as a limit of our regularised model as the intensity function
\lambda
converges to
(t,x,v)\mapsto\infty\mathbf{1}_{x<0}
in a suitable sense.

To deal with the singular framework, we have to adapt the probabilistic setup introduced above Theorem
2.8
. For convenience, we shall reappropriate some of the symbols used there. We set
\mathcal{S}=D[-1,T+1]\times C([0,T+1])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
and
\Omega_{0}=\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})\times C([0,T+1])
. We comment on the enlargement of the time domain in Remark
2.11
. In contrast to the regularised model, we equip the space of càdlàg functions with the
M1
-topology (we refer the reader to
[
43
, Section 12.3]
for a definition and basic properties of the
M1
-topology). Then, we set
\Omega_{\ast}=\mathcal{S}\times\Omega_{0}
and let the canonical random element on
\Omega_{\ast}
be
\Theta^{\ast}=(X^{\ast},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast},\mu^{\ast},B^{\ast})
. We define the filtrations
\mathbb{F}^{0}=(\mathcal{F}^{0}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T+1}
and
\mathbb{F}=(\mathcal{F}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T+1}
analogously to (
2.8
), where we extend
\mathfrak{g}\in\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
beyond
T
by
\mathfrak{g}(A_{1}\times A_{2})=\mathfrak{g}((A_{1}\cap[0,T])\times A_{2})+\lvert A_{2}\cap(T,T+1]\rvert\delta_{g_{0}}(A_{1})
for any measurable
A_{1}\subset[0,T+1]
,
A_{2}\subset G
, and some arbitrary element
g_{0}\in G
. As in Equation (
2.9
) we have

\mu^{\ast}_{t}=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{\cdot\land t},W^{\ast},\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{\cdot\land t},W^{\ast},\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}|\mu^{\ast}).

(2.12)

In addition to these random variables, we define the random time
\tau^{\ast}=\inf\{0<t<T+1\mathpunct{:}X^{\ast}_{t}\leq 0\}
with the convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
and the flow of subprobability distributions
\nu^{\ast}\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]
given for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
by
\langle\nu^{\ast}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\varphi(x_{0-})\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x,w,\mathfrak{g})
if
t\in[-1,0)
and

\langle\nu^{\ast}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathcal{S}}\varphi(x_{t})\mathbf{1}_{\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}x_{s}>0}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{\ast}(x,w,\mathfrak{g})

(2.13)

otherwise. By Equation (
2.12
) we have
\nu^{\ast}_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\tau^{\ast}>t|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
. Finally, we set
L^{\ast}_{t}=1-\nu^{\ast}_{t}(\mathbb{R})
. Note that by definition
L^{\ast}=(L^{\ast}_{t})_{-1\leq t\leq T+1}
is càdlàg and nondecreasing with
L^{\ast}_{t}=0
on
[-1,0)
.

Remark 2.10
.

In contrast to the regularised setup, the flow of subprobabilities
\nu^{\ast}
is explicitly constructed as a function of
\mu^{\ast}
and as such
\nu^{\ast}_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\tau^{\ast}>t|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
for
t\in[0,T+1]
holds under any
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
(or rather its associated probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
). The analogous equality for the regularised setup is a postulate of Definition
2.7
of admissible relaxed control rules.

Remark 2.11
.

The enlargement of the time domain as well as the shift from the
J1
- to the weaker
M1
-topology on the càdlàg space is necessary to derive subsequential convergence of the loss as we let the intensity tend to
\infty\mathbf{1}_{x<0}
. Indeed, in general, the loss of the limiting system will have jumps. However, no sequence of continuous functions can converge in
J1
to a function with jumps. Even in the
M1
-topology a sequence of continuous functions can only converge to a function that is continuous at the interval endpoints. We make sure of this property by extending the time domain and imposing continuity at the new endpoints.

We extend the coefficients to
[0,T+1]
in the following way: we define
b
and
\sigma_{0}
equal to zero after
T
and set
\sigma
to one after
T
. We also assume that
\alpha
is simply a continuous function
[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
, which we extend to
[0,T+1]
in a continuous way and such that it vanishes on
[T+1/2,T]
. The exact shape of
\alpha
on
[T,T+1]
does not matter, since the cost function, which we introduce below, only depends on our setup for times in
[0,T]
.

Definition 2.12
.

We call
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
an
admissible relaxed control rule
(for the singular model) with initial condition
\nu_{0-}\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
if

(i)

\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast}_{0-})=\nu_{0-}
and
\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert X^{\ast}_{t}\rvert^{2}+\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)
is finite;

(ii)

W^{\ast}
and
B^{\ast}
are
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-Brownian motions and the pair
(X^{\ast}_{0},W^{\ast})
is independent of
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
;

(iii)

for all
t\in[-1,0)
we have
X^{\ast}_{t}=X^{\ast}_{0-}
and for all
t\in[0,T+1]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}X^{\ast}_{t}=X^{\ast}_{0-}&+\int_{[0,t]\times G}b(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(s,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W^{\ast}_{s}\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{0}(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}B^{\ast}_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s)\,\mathrm{d}L^{\ast}_{s}\quad\mathbb{P}_{\ast}\textup{-almost surley}.\end{split}

(2.14)

Here
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is the probability measure on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
.

The definition of an admissible relaxed control rule implies that after time
T+1/2
the process
X^{\ast}
simply diffuses according to the idiosyncratic noise
W^{\ast}
, that is
X^{\ast}_{t}=X^{\ast}_{(T+1/2)-}+W^{\ast}_{t}-W^{\ast}_{T+1/2}
for
t\in[T+1/2,T+1]
. This together with the nondegeneracy of the diffusion coefficients ensures that the random time
\tau
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous for any admissible control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
(see Proposition
4.2
).

Unfortunately, in the singular case, we cannot use the same cost functional as for the regularised model. Indeed, functions of the form
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]\ni v\mapsto\psi(v_{T})
for continuous
\psi\mathpunct{:}\mathbf{M}\to\mathbb{R}
are generally not
M1
-continuous (or even upper semicontinuous) at elements of
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]
that jump at
T
. Since the process
{\nu}^{\ast}
might have a discontinuity at
T
with positive probability under an admissible relaxed control rule, the terminal cost in (
2.11
) is not suitable. However, we can replace the terminal cost by an expression of the form
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]\ni v\mapsto\frac{1}{\epsilon}\int_{T-\epsilon}^{T}\psi(v_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t
with
\epsilon>0
small. Indeed, this function is itself continuous and it approximates
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]\ni v\mapsto\psi(v_{T})
well for flows
v
that are continuous at
T
. Generalising this idea slightly, we introduce the
cost function

J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\biggl[\int_{[0,\tau^{\ast}\land T]\times G}f(t,X^{\ast}_{t},\nu^{\ast}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)+\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t,\nu^{\ast}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr]

(2.15)

for any admissible relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
, where
\psi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
. We define the
value

V^{\text{sg}}_{0}
as the infimum of
J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
over all admissible control rules
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
.

Next, we consider the sequence of regularised approximations. Let
\lambda^{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
be a family of functions satisfying the same assumptions as
\lambda
in Assumption
2.2
. As for the other coefficients we extend
\lambda^{n}
to a function
[0,T+1]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
by setting
\lambda(t,x,\nu)=\lambda(T,x,\nu)
for
t\in(T,T+1]
. Then for a sequence of admissible strong controls
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
we can uniquely solve the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X^{n}_{t}&=b(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t},\gamma^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}\\
&\ \ \ -\alpha(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}L_{t},\\
\mathrm{d}\Lambda^{n}_{t}&=\lambda^{n}(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(2.16)

with initial conditions
X^{n}_{0}=\xi
and
\Lambda^{n}_{0}=0
, conditional subprobability distribution
\nu^{n}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X^{n}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda^{n}_{t}|W^{0})
, and loss
L^{n}_{t}=1-\nu^{n}_{t}(\mathbb{R})
. Our goal is to show that the sequence
(\mathcal{L}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n}|W^{0}),W^{0})_{n}
converges to an admissible relaxed control rule (for the singular model) as
\lambda^{n}
tends to
\infty\mathbf{1}_{x<0}
. Here
\Gamma^{n}
is the relaxed control associated to
\gamma^{n}
, i.e.
\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{n}(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma^{n}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t
. We impose the following assumptions.

Assumption 2.13
.

Let the coefficients
b
,
\sigma
, and
\sigma_{0}
and the cost
f
satisfy Assumption
2.2
. Let
\alpha\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
,
\psi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, and
\lambda^{n}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
,
n\geq 1
, be measurable. We assume that there exists a
C>0
, such that

(i)

the function
\alpha
is continuous and nonnegative;

(ii)

for all
t
,
x
,
\nu
we have
\sigma^{2}(t,x,\nu)+\sigma_{0}^{2}(t,x,\nu)\geq\frac{1}{C}
;

(iii)

the intensity functions
\lambda^{n}
satisfy the same assumptions as
\lambda
in Assumption
2.2
, and for all
t
, and any sequence
(x_{n})_{n}
in
\mathbb{R}
with
\limsup_{n\to\infty}x_{n}<0
, we have

\inf_{v\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(R)}\lambda^{n}(t,x_{n},v)\to\infty;

(iv)

the map
\psi
is continuous in
v
and for all
t
,
v
we have
\lvert\psi(t,v)\rvert\leq C(1+M_{2}^{2}(v))
.

Theorem 2.14
.

Let Assumption
2.13
be satisfied. Fix a sequence
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
of admissible controls, such that
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Next, let
X^{n}
be the solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.16
) with control
\gamma^{n}
and intensity function
\lambda^{n}
, and denote the relaxed control associated to
\gamma^{n}
by
\Gamma^{n}
. Then the sequence
(\mathcal{L}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n}|W^{0}),W^{0})_{n}
is tight on
\Omega_{0}
, every subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
yields an admissible relaxed control rule (for the singular model), and

J_{n}(\gamma^{n})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\Lambda^{n}_{t}}f(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t},\gamma^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t,\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr]

(2.17)

converges to
J^{\textup{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
along the corresponding subsequence.

In the statement of the theorem, we extend
X^{n}
and
\nu^{n}
to
[-1,T+1]
in the same way as
X^{\ast}
and
\nu^{\ast}
in Definition
2.12
. Implicit in Theorem
2.14
is a (weak) existence theorem for McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.14
) which generalises the existing literature
[
37
,
34
,
22
]
.

Remark 2.15
.

Theorem
2.14
does not say anything about the subsequential weak convergence of the flow of subprobabilities
(\nu^{n})_{n}
on
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]
to
\nu^{\ast}
. In fact, at this time, we are not sure whether this convergence holds. The issue lies in the possibility that the feedback
\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{s}
may push a nonnegligible number of particles sufficiently far below zero before they are killed. Such a situation could arise if the limiting system jumps with positive probability at some time
t\in[0,T]
. It is then conceivable that we can find a sequence of (
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
-stopping) times
t_{n}\in[0,T]
with
t_{n}\to t
such that the sequence
(\nu^{n}_{t_{n}}((-\infty,0)))_{n}
is bounded away from zero, which precludes weak convergence to
\nu^{\ast}
.

In Proposition
4.2
below we instead prove that
(\nu^{n})_{n}
converges weakly along a subsequence on the space
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
of square-integrable functions
[-1,T+1]\to\mathbb{R}
.

Unlike in Theorem
2.8
, even if we assume that the sequence
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
of admissible controls in Theorem
2.14
satisfies
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq\inf_{\gamma}J_{n}(\gamma)+\epsilon_{n}
with
\epsilon_{n}\to 0
, we cannot conclude that
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})
converges to the infimum of
J^{\textup{sg}}_{0}
, since there is no uniqueness theory for the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.14
), which represents the singular model. In fact, pathwise and weak uniqueness are known to fail even for constant coefficients and without controls (unless solutions are restricted to a smaller class of so-called physical solutions). We refer the reader to
[
12
,
20
]
for more details. Consequently, it may not be possible to approximate every solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.14
) by the sequence of regularised models (
2.16
). If this is the case for all (nearly) optimally controlled solutions of (
2.14
), then
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})
will not converge to the infimum of
J^{\textup{sg}}_{0}
.

Therefore, we specialise to a simpler situation, where the diffusion coefficients
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
are only functions of time,
\lambda^{n}
depends solely on
x
, the drift coefficient is given by
b(t,g)=b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)g
for bounded and measurable functions
b_{0}
,
b_{1}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
, and
G
is a subset of
\mathbb{R}
. Then the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.14
) becomes

\mathrm{d}X^{\ast}_{t}=\int_{G}(b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)+\sigma(t)\,\mathrm{d}W^{\ast}_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}B^{\ast}_{t}-\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{\ast}_{t}.

(2.18)

with
L^{\ast}_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau\leq t|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
. While we still cannot guarantee uniqueness of the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.18
), we can exploit the monotonicity of the equation in the loss function. To this end, we formulate the following assumption.

Assumption 2.16
.

We assume that

(i)

for all
t
,
x
,
v
we have
f(t,x,v,0)=0
;

(ii)

for all
t
,
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
v
,
v^{\prime}
,
g
,
g
with
x\geq x^{\prime}
,
v\geq v^{\prime}
,
g\leq g^{\prime}
we have

f(t,x,v,g)\leq f(t,x^{\prime},v^{\prime},g^{\prime}),\qquad\psi(t,v)\leq\psi(t,v^{\prime}).

Here we say that
v\leq v^{\prime}
for two elements
v
,
v^{\prime}\in\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
if
v([x,\infty))\leq v^{\prime}([x,\infty))
for all
x\in\mathbb{R}
.

Being small with respect to the partial order introduced in Assumption
2.16
heuristically means that mass is predominately found on the lower half-line or has already disappeared.

Proposition 2.17
.

Let Assumptions
2.13
and
2.16
be satisfied and suppose that the coefficients and
G
have the form outlined above Equation (
2.18
). Let
(X^{n},\nu^{n},\gamma^{n})_{n}
be as in Theorem
2.14
and additionally assume that
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq V_{n}+\epsilon_{n}
for a sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n}
that converges to zero. Then
\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})=V^{\textup{sg}}_{0}
, where
V_{n}
denotes the infimum of
J_{n}(\gamma)
over all admissible strong controls
\gamma
.

Remark 2.18
.

We can disintegrate
\Gamma^{\ast}
as
\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}(t,g)=\Gamma^{\ast}_{t}(g)\,\mathrm{d}t
for an
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-predictable process
(\Gamma^{\ast}_{t})_{t}
with values in
\mathcal{P}^{2}(G)
(see
[
29
, Lemm 3.2]
). Then, if we set
\gamma^{\ast}_{t}=\int_{G}g\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{\ast}_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
, the process
\gamma^{\ast}=(\gamma^{\ast}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
is
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-progressively measurable and the state equation (
2.18
) becomes

\mathrm{d}X^{\ast}_{t}=(b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)\gamma^{\ast}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t)\,\mathrm{d}W^{\ast}_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}B^{\ast}_{t}-\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{\ast}_{t}.

Moreover, assuming that the running cost
f
is convex in
g
, Jensen’s inequality implies

J^{\textup{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\geq\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\biggl[\int_{0}^{\tau^{\ast}\land T}f(t,X^{\ast}_{t},\nu^{\ast}_{t},\gamma^{\ast}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t,\nu^{\ast}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr].

Consequently, in this natural setup, it is always possible to choose an optimal control that takes the form of an
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process as opposed to a random measure on
[0,T]\times G
.

As we mentioned before, even in the absence of controls, i.e. if
b_{1}(t)=0
, McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.18
) does not exhibit (weak or pathwise) uniqueness. Hence, obtaining a weak convergence result in the spirit of Corollary
2.9
seems hopeless. However, if we assume that the
(\lambda^{n})_{n}
form a nondecreasing sequence in the sense that
\lambda^{m}(x)\geq\lambda^{n}(x)
whenever
m\geq n
, then the same will be true for
(L^{n})_{n}
, i.e.
L^{m}_{t}\geq L^{n}_{t}
if
m\geq n
. Consequently, the random variables
L^{n}_{t}
converge a.s. for each
t\in[0,T+1]
. This allows us to define a càdlàg process
L=(L_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T+1}
as follows: we define
\ell_{t}
as the a.s. limit of
L^{n}_{t}
for
t\in[0,T+1]\cap(\mathbb{Q}\cup\{T+1\})
, where
\mathbb{Q}
are the rationals. Then we set
L_{t}=\lim_{s\searrow t,s\in(t,T+1]\cap\mathbb{Q}}\ell_{s}
for
t\in[0,T+1)
and
L_{T+1}=\ell_{T+1}
.
We show in the proof of Proposition
2.19
that
L^{n}_{t}\to L_{t}
a.s. for any
t\in[0,T+1]
with
\mathbb{P}(\Delta L_{t}=0)=1
and that there exists a càdlàg process
X
such that

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=b_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}-\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L_{t}

(2.19)

and
L_{t}=\mathbb{P}(\tau\leq t|W^{0})
, where
\tau=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}X_{t}\leq 0\}
. That is,
(X,L)
is a strong solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.18
). Moreover, we prove that the process
L
is
minimal
in the sense that if
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is any other solution of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
) on the same probability space then
L_{t}\leq L^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
.

For the purpose of the following proposition we extend
(X^{n},L^{n})
and
(X,L)
to the interval
[-1,T+1]
by setting
X^{n}_{t}=X_{t}=X_{0-}
and
L^{n}_{t}=L_{t}=0
on
[-1,0)
.

Proposition 2.19
.

Let Assumptions
2.13
and
2.16
be satisfied and suppose that the coefficients have the form outlined above Equation (
2.18
). Assume further that
b_{1}=0
and that
\lambda^{m}(x)\geq\lambda^{n}(x)
for
x\in\mathbb{R}
whenever
m\geq n
. Then
(X^{n},L^{n})_{n}
converges a.s. in
D[-1,T+1]\times D[-1,T+1]
to the minimal solution
(X,L)
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
).

3
Convergence of the Nearly Optimally Controlled Particle System

The goal of this section is to show the subsequential convergence of the (approximately) optimally controlled particle system to an optimal relaxed control rule. In particular, we prove Theorems
2.4
and
2.8
as well as Corollary
2.9
.

3.1
The Finite Particle System

Our first objective is to analyse the particle system and deduce tightness on
\Omega_{0}
of the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
as defined in Theorem
2.8
. Throughout this subsection Assumption
2.2
is in place and we fix admissible controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
for the particle system.

Initially, we note that the particle system (
2.1
) is well-posed and has a unique strong solution. In fact, we may inductively construct a solution on
[0,S)
for any
S>0
as follows: set
\varrho_{0}=0
and
D_{0}=\varnothing
, and define
\xi_{0,i}=\xi_{i}
,
i=1
, …,
N
. Next, suppose that a stopping time
\varrho_{n-1}\leq S
, a random subset
D_{n-1}
of
\{1,\dots,N\}
, and the
\mathbb{F}^{N}_{\varrho_{n-1}}
-measurable random variables
\xi_{n-1,i}
,
i=1
, …,
N
, are defined for
n\geq 1
. Then we let
Y^{n,i}
denote the unique solution to

\mathrm{d}Y^{n,i}_{t}=b(t,Y^{n,i}_{t},\nu^{n,N}_{t},\gamma^{i}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,Y^{n,i}_{t},\nu^{n,N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,Y^{n,i}_{t},\nu^{n,N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}

on the random interval
[\varrho_{n-1},S]
, where
Y^{n,i}_{\varrho_{n-1}}=\xi_{n-1,i}
and
\nu^{n,N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbf{1}_{i\notin D_{n-1}}\delta_{Y^{n,i}_{t}}
. Then, we define
\varrho_{n}
as the minimum between
S
and the first killing time of a previously alive particle, i.e. the first time
t\geq\varrho_{n-1}
such that
\theta_{i}\leq\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,Y^{n,i}_{s},\nu^{n,N}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
for some
i\notin D_{n-1}
. We obtain the set
D_{n}
by adding the index of the killed particle to
D_{n-1}
. If two particles are removed at the same time, we deal with them iteratively, selecting the particle with the smaller index first. Finally, we set
\xi_{n,i}=Y^{n,i}_{\varrho_{n-1}-}-\frac{1}{N}\alpha(\varrho_{n-1},Y^{n,i}_{\varrho_{n-1}-},\nu^{n,N}_{\varrho_{n-1}-})
. Since at most
N
killings can occur
\varrho_{N+1}=S
and it is not difficult to see that the processes
X^{1}
, …,
X^{N}
defined by
X^{i}_{t}=Y^{n,i}_{t}
for
t\in[\varrho_{n-1},\varrho_{n})
,
n=1
, …,
N+1
, form a solution to (
2.1
) on
[0,S)
. Uniqueness is an immediate consequence of the uniqueness on the individual intervals
[\varrho_{n-1},\varrho_{n})
.

We start our analysis of the particle system with a simple uniform boundedness and integrability result for the particle trajectories. Its proof is entirely standard and so we skip it.

Lemma 3.1
.

If
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{N}
is uniformly bounded in expectation (resp. uniformly integrable), then
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\bigr)_{N}
is uniformly bounded in expectation (resp. uniformly integrable).

Recall the processes
\Lambda^{i}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X^{i}_{s},\nu^{N}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
and
I^{i}_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\theta_{i}>\Lambda^{i}_{t}}
, and for
i=1
, …,
N
define
M^{i}
by

M^{i}_{t}=I^{i}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}I^{i}_{s}\lambda(s,X^{i}_{s},\nu^{N}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

for
t\geq 0
. If the intensity function
\lambda
only depended on time, it would be easy to see that
\int_{0}^{\cdot}I^{i}_{t}\lambda(t)\,\mathrm{d}t
is the compensator of
I^{i}
, so that
M^{i}
is a martingale. The same is still true if
\lambda
depends on
X^{i}_{t}
and
\nu^{N}_{t}
as we show in Lemma
3.2
below. The main challenge in the proof is to disentangle
\theta_{i}
from
X^{i}_{t}
and
\nu^{N}_{t}
, which we achieve by introducing an auxiliary particle system. First, let us introduce some notation. We set
\mathbf{W}=(W^{1},\dots,W^{N})
,
\mathbf{I}=(I^{1},\dots,I^{N})
, and
\mathbf{X}=(X^{1},\dots,X^{N})
, and introduce the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}=(\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
generated by
\mathbf{X}_{0}
,
\mathbf{I}
,
\mathbf{W}
, and
W^{0}
, i.e.

\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{t}=\sigma\bigl(\xi_{i},\,I^{i}_{s},\,W^{i}_{s},\,W^{0}_{s}\mathpunct{:}0\leq s\leq t,\,i=0,\dots,N\bigr).

(3.1)

Note that the controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
are progressively measurable with respect to this filtration, so we can find measurable functions
g_{i}\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(D[0,T])^{N}\times C([0,T])^{N}\times C([0,T])\to G
with
\gamma^{i}_{t}=g_{i}(t,\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{I},\mathbf{W},W^{0})
for
t\in[0,T]
.

Lemma 3.2
.

The processes
M^{i}
for
i=1
, …,
N
are martingales with respect to
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
.

Proof.

Fix an index
i\in\{1,\dots,N\}
. We will suppress
i
throughout the proof and only write indices
j\neq i
. Firstly, it is clear that
M
is adapted to
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
. Next, let us define an altered particle system in the killing of particle
i
does not feed into the remaining system. The particles’ states
\tilde{X}^{j}
,
j=1
, …,
N
, follow the dynamics

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}\tilde{X}^{j}_{t}&=b(t,\tilde{X}^{j}_{t},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t},\gamma^{j}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,\tilde{X}^{j}_{t},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{j}_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,\tilde{X}^{j}_{t},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}\\
&\ \ \ -\alpha(t,\tilde{X}^{j}_{t-},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t-})\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{L}^{N}_{t},\end{split}

(3.2)

started from
\tilde{X}^{j}_{0}=\xi_{j}
. Here
\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\bigl(\delta_{\tilde{X}^{i}_{t}}+\sum_{j\neq i}\tilde{I}^{j}_{t}\delta_{\tilde{X}^{j}_{t}}\bigr)
,
\tilde{L}^{N}_{t}=1-\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t}(\mathbb{R})
,
\tilde{I}^{j}_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\theta_{j}>\tilde{\Lambda}^{j}_{t}}
, and
\tilde{\gamma}^{j}_{t}=g_{i}(t,\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}^{\prime},\mathbf{W},W^{0})
, where
\tilde{\Lambda}^{j}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,\tilde{X}^{j}_{s},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
and
\tilde{\mathbf{I}}^{\prime}
arises from
\tilde{\mathbf{I}}=(\tilde{I}^{1},\dots,\tilde{I}^{N})
by replacing the
i
-th component with the process that is constantly equal to
1
. Then, we set
\tilde{M}_{t}=\tilde{I}_{t}+\int_{0}^{t}\tilde{I}_{s}\lambda(s,\tilde{X}_{s},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}s
and claim
\tilde{M}=M
. Clearly, the dynamics
\tilde{X}^{j}
and
X^{j}
coincide on
[0,\tau)
for
j=1
, …,
N
, which shows that
\tilde{M}
and
M
are equal on
[0,\tau)
. This readily implies that
\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}\tilde{\Lambda}_{t}\geq\theta\}=\tau
. Consequently, both
\tilde{M}
and
M
are constant once
\tau
has occurred, which means they also coincide on
[\tau,\infty)
, so they are indeed equal.

We now show that
\tilde{M}
is an
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
-martingale. Firstly,
\tilde{M}
is
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
-adapted and integrable at all times since it is bounded in
L^{2}
by Lemma
3.1
. Thus, it remains to verify the martingale property. Let
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{N}\times(D[0,T])^{N}\times C([0,T])^{N}\times C([0,T])\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded and measurable function. Our goal is to show that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{I}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr]=0
for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, as this implies the martingale property for
\tilde{M}
if we let
\varphi
range over all bounded and measurable functions. Note that if
s<\tau
, we have
\mathbf{I}_{\cdot\land s}=\tilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\cdot\land s}
, and, otherwise,
\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s}
vanishes. Consequently, we get

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{I}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr].

(3.3)

The difference
\tilde{I}_{t}-\tilde{I}_{s}
can only be nonzero if
\tilde{I}_{s}
, and therefore
\tilde{I}_{u}
for
0\leq u\leq s
, is equal to
1
. Thus,
we get

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{I}_{t}-\tilde{I}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{I}_{t}-\tilde{I}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}^{\prime}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr].

The process
\tilde{\Lambda}
as well as the random variable
\tilde{\Phi}=\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}^{\prime}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})
are independent of
\theta
. Since
\theta
follows a standard exponential distribution, we can explicitly compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{I}_{t}-\tilde{I}_{s})\tilde{\Phi}\bigr]&=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(e^{-\tilde{\Lambda}_{t}}-e^{-\tilde{\Lambda}_{s}}\bigr)\tilde{\Phi}\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\tilde{\Phi}\int_{s}^{t}-\lambda(u,\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{u})e^{-\tilde{\Lambda}_{u}}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\tilde{\Phi}\int_{s}^{t}-\lambda(u,\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{u})\tilde{I}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr].\end{split}

(3.4)

Again
\int_{s}^{t}-\lambda(u,\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{u})\tilde{I}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u
is only nonzero if
\tilde{I}_{s}=1
, whence

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\tilde{\Phi}\int_{s}^{t}-\lambda(u,\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{u})\tilde{I}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\int_{s}^{t}-\lambda(u,\tilde{X}_{u},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{u})\tilde{I}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr].

Combining this equality with the previous two equations and rearranging implies that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\tilde{\mathbf{I}}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr]=0
, so in view of Equation (
3.3
) we get
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\tilde{M}_{t}-\tilde{M}_{s})\varphi(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{I}_{\cdot\land s},\mathbf{W}_{\cdot\land s},W^{0}_{\cdot\land s})\bigr]=0
as required.
∎

The martingale property of
M^{i}
provides the crucial step in the proof of tightness of the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
, which is the subject of Proposition
3.4
below. However, this does not imply that the loss and, therefore, the particle trajectories become continuous in the limit. To establish that we need to prove that the martingales
M^{i}
,
i=1
, …,
N
, decorrelate asymptotically.

Lemma 3.3
.

Assume that
\sup_{N\geq 1}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. Then
\mathbb{E}\bigl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(M^{i}_{t}-1)\bigr\rvert^{2}=O(1/N)
as
N\to\infty
.

Proof.

For simplicity, we set
Z^{i}_{t}=M^{i}_{t}-1
. We have that

\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}Z^{i}_{t}\biggr\rvert^{2}=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\lvert Z^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}+\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\sum\limits_{j\neq i}\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}].

Lemma
3.1
implies that the expectations
\mathbb{E}\lvert Z^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}
are bounded uniformly in
N\geq 1
. Consequently, the first summand on the right-hand side is for order
1/N
as required. To show that the same is true for the second sum, we prove that
\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}]=0
whenever
i\neq j
. Fix distinct indices
i
,
j\in\{1,\dots,N\}
and set
\varrho=\tau_{i}\land\tau_{j}
. It follows from a simple computation:

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}]&=\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{i}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{j}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=t}]\\
&=\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{i}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{j}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=t}]\\
&=\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{i}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=\tau_{j}}]+\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}\mathbf{1}_{\varrho\land t=t}]\\
&=\mathbb{E}[Z^{i}_{\varrho\land t}Z^{j}_{\varrho\land t}].\end{split}

(3.5)

In the first line, we used that the
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
-stopping times
\tau_{i}
and
\tau_{j}
almost surely do not occur at the same time or at
t
. The second equality follows since
M^{k}
is constant after
\tau_{k}
, and the third equality exploits the martingale property of
M^{k}
(Lemma
3.2
) together with the
L^{2}
-bound from Lemma
3.1
for
k=i
,
j
.

Let us define a particle system
\tilde{X}^{1}
, …,
\tilde{X}^{N}
similar to (
3.2
). However, instead of only removing the possibility of killing the
i
-th particle, we remove the possibility of killing particles
i
and
j
. Further, we define
\tilde{Z}^{k}=\tilde{M}^{k}-1
for
k=i
,
j
. Then, reasoning as in the proof of Lemma
3.2
, it is easy to see that
\tilde{Z}^{k}_{\varrho\land t}=Z^{k}_{\varrho\land t}
for
0\leq t\leq T
. Thus, in view of Equation (
3.5
) it suffices to show that
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{i}_{\varrho\land t}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{\varrho\land t}]=0
. Since
\tilde{Z}^{i}
and
\tilde{Z}^{j}
are martingales, reiterating the steps in Equation (
3.5
) in reverse order, we obtain
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{i}_{\varrho\land t}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{\varrho\land t}]=\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{i}_{t}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{t}]
. Hence, let us prove that
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{i}_{t}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{t}]=0
. By design,
\theta_{i}
is independent of
(\tilde{X}^{i},\tilde{X}^{k},\tilde{I}^{k})_{k\neq i}
. So performing a calculation similar to that in Equation (
3.4
) yields

\mathbb{E}[(\tilde{I}^{i}_{t}-1)\tilde{Z}^{j}_{t}]=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\tilde{Z}^{j}_{t}\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,\tilde{X}^{i}_{s},\tilde{\nu}^{N}_{s})\tilde{I}^{i}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\biggr].

Rearranging implies
\mathbb{E}[\tilde{Z}^{i}_{t}\tilde{Z}^{j}_{t}]=0
.
∎

Let us associate to each control
\gamma^{i}
a random measure
\Gamma^{i}
on
[0,T]\times G
defined through
\mathrm{d}\Gamma^{i}(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma^{i}_{t}}\mathrm{d}t
. Obviously,
\Gamma^{i}
takes values in
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
, the space of square-integrable measures on
[0,T]\times G
with total mass
T
and the Lebesgue measure as the time-marginal. We can now prove the tightness of the particle system.

Proposition 3.4
.

Assume that
\sup_{N\geq 1}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
is finite and that the family
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\Gamma^{i}}\bigr)_{N}
is tight on
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G))
. Then the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
is tight on
\Omega_{0}
. Moreover, any limit point of
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(X^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
is concentrated on the space of continuous functions
C([0,T])
.

Proof.

We begin by proving tightness of
\nu^{N}
. The space
\mathbf{M}=\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
equipped with the distance
d_{1}
is a complete separable metric space. Thus by Theorem 8.6 of Chapter 3 in
[
15
]
to prove tightness it is enough to show that (i)
(\nu^{N}_{t})_{N}
is tight for all
t\in[0,T]
and (ii) there exists a sequence of random variables
(\zeta_{N})_{N}
with
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{N}<\infty
such that for all
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
,

\mathbb{E}\bigl[d_{1}(\nu^{N}_{t},\nu^{N}_{s})\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]\leq\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\mathbb{E}[\zeta_{N}|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}].

(3.6)

Item (i) follows straightforwardly from the uniform bound on
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}
that we established in Lemma
3.1
. For (ii), using the definition of the metric
d_{1}
from Equation (
2.4
), it is not difficult to see that

d_{1}(\nu^{N}_{t},\nu^{N}_{s})\leq\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\lvert X^{i}_{t}-X^{i}_{s}\rvert+\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}(1+\lvert X^{i}_{s}\rvert)\lvert I^{i}_{t}-I^{i}_{s}\rvert.

(3.7)

We will derive appropriate bounds in terms of a random variable
\zeta_{N}
for both summands on the right-hand side above. We start with the first term. Define
\chi^{k}_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}(\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{k}
and note that by Lemma
3.1
that
\sup_{N\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\chi^{k}_{N}<\infty
for
k=1
,
2
. Then we estimate using Assumption
2.2

(i)
and the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert

\displaystyle X^{i}_{t}-X^{i}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\biggl(\sqrt{T}\mathbb{E}\bigl[1+\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T}+\chi^{1}_{N}\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\biggl[\biggl(\int_{s}^{t}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr)^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigg|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\biggr]\biggr)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +C_{1}C_{\sigma}\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}+C_{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert L^{N}_{t}-L^{N}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]

for some constant
C_{1}>0
. Here we exploited that
\int_{s}^{t}\alpha(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\,\mathrm{d}L^{N}_{u}\leq C_{\alpha}\lvert L^{N}_{t}-L^{N}_{s}\rvert
. Using
a\leq 1+a^{2}
for
a\geq 0
, summing over
i=1
, …,
N
, and dividing by
N
yields

\displaystyle\begin{split}\frac{1}{N}\sum\limits_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert X^{i}_{t}&-X^{i}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]\\
&\leq\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\Bigl(C_{b}\bigl(2\sqrt{T}+2\sqrt{T}\mathbb{E}[\chi^{1}_{N}|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}]+\mathbb{E}[C_{\gamma,N}|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}]\bigr)+C_{1}C_{\sigma}\Bigr)\\
&\ \ \ +C_{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert L^{N}_{t}-L^{N}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr],\end{split}

(3.8)

where we defined
C_{\gamma,N}^{2}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
, which is bounded in
L^{1}
uniformly in
N\geq 1
by assumption. The first term on the right-hand side is of the desired form. We deal with the expression involving the loss increment next. We can do this together with the second summand in Equation (
3.7
). First note that
\lvert I^{i}_{t}-I^{i}_{s}\rvert=I^{i}_{s}-I^{i}_{t}\leq M^{i}_{s}-M^{i}_{t}+\int_{s}^{t}\lvert\lambda(u,X^{i}_{u},\nu^{N}_{u})\rvert\,\mathrm{d}u
. The process
M^{i}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
by Lemma
3.2
. Thus, since
\varphi(X^{i}_{s})
is
\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}
-measurable for any measurable function
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
, assuming that
\varphi
is of at most linear growth we obtain that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert\varphi(X^{i}_{s})\rvert\lvert I^{i}_{t}-I^{i}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq\lvert\varphi(X^{i}_{s})\rvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[M^{i}_{t}-M^{i}_{s}\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]
