\displaystyle\ \ \ +C_{\lambda}\lvert t-s\rvert\lvert\varphi(X^{i}_{s})\rvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[1+\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T}+\chi^{1}_{N}\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]

\displaystyle=C_{\lambda}\lvert t-s\rvert\lvert\varphi(X^{i}_{s})\rvert\mathbb{E}\bigl[1+\lvert X^{i}\rvert^{\ast}_{T}+\chi^{1}_{N}\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr].

Averaging over all particles and choosing
\varphi(x)=x
allows us to bound the second term on the right-hand side of Equation (
3.7
) by
2C_{\lambda}\sqrt{T}\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(1+\mathbb{E}[\chi^{2}_{N}|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}]\bigr)
. Setting
\varphi(x)=1
yields a bound for the loss in (
3.8
):

C_{\alpha}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\lvert L^{N}_{t}-L^{N}_{s}\rvert\big|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}\bigr]\leq 2\alpha C_{\lambda}\sqrt{T}\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}\bigl(1+\mathbb{E}[\chi^{1}_{N}|\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}]\bigr).

Hence, upon defining

\zeta_{N}=2(C_{b}+C_{\alpha}C_{\lambda})\sqrt{T}(1+\chi^{1}_{N})+C_{b}C_{\gamma,N}+C_{1}C_{\sigma}+2C_{\lambda}\sqrt{T}(1+\chi^{2}_{N}),

we obtain the desired bound (
3.6
) with
\sup_{N}\mathbb{E}\zeta_{N}<\infty
.

Next, we address the tightness of
\mu^{N}
. It is enough to prove tightness of the empirical laws of
(X^{N,i})_{i}
,
(W^{i})_{i}
,
(\Gamma^{N,i})_{i}
, and
(\theta_{i})_{i}
for
N\geq 1
separately. For the Brownian motions and the exponential times, this is obvious and for the controls tightness is assumed. Thus it remains to show that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{N,i}}
is tight on
\mathcal{P}(D[0,T])
. However, by Proposition 2.2 (ii) of
[
41
]
this boils down to proving that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}(X^{N,i})
is tight on
D[0,T]
. But this follows from essentially the same argument we used to establish tightness of
\nu^{N}
above.

Let us now move to the last statement, that any limit point of
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(X^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
is concentrated on
C([0,T])
. We can write
X^{N,i}
as the sum of a continuous process
C^{N,i}
and the pure jump process
J^{N,i}
defined by
J^{N,i}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s,X^{N,i}_{s-},\nu^{N}_{s-})\,\mathrm{d}M^{N,j}_{s}
. We will prove below that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert J^{N,i}\rvert^{2}
converges to zero as
N\to\infty
. Consequently, the families
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(X^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
and
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(C^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
have the same weak limit points on
D[0,T]
. However, the processes
C^{N,i}
are continuous and the space of continuous functions
C([0,T])
is closed in
D[0,T]
with respect to the topology of convergence in
J1
. Thus, any limit point of
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(C^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
and, therefore,
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(X^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
is concentrated on
C([0,T])
as desired.

It remains to show that
J^{N,i}
vanishes in the
L^{2}
-
\sup
-limit. For brevity, let us set
H^{i}_{t}=\alpha(t,X^{i}_{t-},\nu^{N}_{t-})
and
\bar{M}^{N}_{t}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(M^{j}_{t}-1)
. Then by the Burkholder-Davis-Gundy inequality,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert J^{N,i}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert H^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\langle\bar{M}^{N}\rangle_{t}\leq 4C_{\alpha}^{2}\mathbb{E}\langle\bar{M}^{N}\rangle_{T}=4C_{\alpha}^{2}\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}(M^{j}_{T}-1)\biggr\rvert^{2}.

The expression on the right-hand side is in
O(1/N)
by Lemma
3.3
, which concludes the proof.
∎

3.2
Properties of the Limit System

As in the previous subsection we impose Assumption
2.2
and fix admissible controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
for the particle system. We also assume that
\sup_{N\geq 1}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
and that the sequence
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\Gamma^{i}}\bigr)_{N}
is tight on
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G))
. In view of Proposition
3.4
this implies tightness of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
. For notational convenience in this and the subsequent subsection, we suppress the asterisk in the superscript of the canonical variables
\Theta^{\ast}=(X^{\ast},W^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast},\theta^{\ast},\mu^{\ast},\nu^{\ast},B^{\ast})
on the space
\Omega_{\ast}
. E.g. we write
X
instead of
X^{\ast}
. We also set

\Theta^{N,i}=(X^{N,i},W^{i},\Gamma^{N,i},\theta_{i},\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})

for
i=1
, …,
N
and
\Theta^{N,0}=(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})
. The goal of this subsection is to show that for any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
, we have
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
a.s. under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
, where
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is the probability distribution on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
via (
2.7
). We start with the following lemma.

Lemma 3.5
.

Let
\mathbb{P}_{0}
be a subsequential limit of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
, then
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}(\Theta^{N,i})
converges weakly to
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
along the same subsequence. In particular, the process
X
has
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous trajectories.

Proof.

For ease of notation assume that convergence holds along the entire sequence. Let
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
be a bounded and continuous function. Then the map
\Omega_{0}\ni(m,v,b)\mapsto\langle m,\varphi(\cdot,m,v,b)\rangle
is continuous. Thus the continuous mapping theorem implies

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\varphi(\Theta^{N,i})=\mathbb{E}\langle\mu^{N},\varphi(\cdot,\Theta^{N,0})\rangle\to\int_{\Omega_{0}}\langle m,\varphi(\cdot,m,v,b)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}(m,v,b).

However,
\int_{\Omega_{0}}\langle m,\varphi(\cdot,m,v,b)\rangle\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}(m,v,b)=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\langle\mu,\varphi(\cdot,\Theta^{0})\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\varphi(\Theta)
, which gives the desired convergence.

Next, by Proposition
3.4
any limit point of
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(X^{N,i})\bigr)_{N}
is concentrated on
C([0,T])
. By the above the law of
X
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is precisely such a limit point, so that
X
has a.s. continuous trajectories under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}

∎

Lemma 3.6
.

For any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
, it holds that
X
,
W
, and
\Gamma
are independent of
\theta
conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
.

Proof.

Let us define the empirical measure
\tilde{\eta}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\xi_{i},W^{i},W^{0},\theta_{i}}
. Owing to the measurability conditions imposed on the controls
\gamma^{N,1}
, …,
\gamma^{N,N}
in Section
2
, we can find measurable maps
\Phi^{N,i}_{\gamma}
, such that
\gamma^{N,i}=\Phi^{N,i}_{\gamma}(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{W},W^{0},\tilde{\eta}^{N})
. Since
X^{N,1}
, …,
X^{N,N}
are strong solutions to the SDE (
2.1
), we can similarly find measurable maps
\Phi^{N,i}_{X}
for which
X^{N,i}=\Phi^{N,i}_{X}(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{W},W^{0},\tilde{\eta}^{N})
. Together this allows us to represent the triple
(X^{N,i},W^{i},\Gamma^{N,i})
as a measurable function
\Phi^{N,i}
of
(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{W},W^{0},\tilde{\eta}^{N})
. Hence, letting
E_{1}=\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T])^{2}
,
E_{2}=\mathbb{R}
and
F=D[0,T]\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
, and setting the variables
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
from Lemma
A.6
to
(\mathbf{X}_{0},\mathbf{W},W^{0})
and
\theta_{i}
, respectively, we find that all assumptions in Lemma
A.6
are satisfied. Consequently,
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X,W,\Gamma,\theta|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X,W,\Gamma|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})\otimes\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(\theta|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
as desired.
∎

Let us introduce the process
\Lambda
on
\Omega_{\ast}
defined by
\Lambda_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
as well as the random time
\tau=\inf\{0<t\leq T\mathpunct{:}\Lambda_{t}\geq\theta\}
with the convention
\inf\emptyset=\infty
. By exploiting the independence of
\theta
from
X
and
\Lambda
guaranteed by Lemma
3.6
we can prove that
\tau
is a.s. continuous for any limiting probability on
\Omega_{\ast}
.

Lemma 3.7
.

Assume that
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
is such that
X
,
W
, and
\Gamma
are independent of
\theta
conditionally on
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
,
\theta
follows an exponential distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
, and that
X
has
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous trajectories. Then the random time
\tau
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. In particular,
\tau
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous for any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})
.

Proof.

We proceed in three steps.

Step 1
: We show that for
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega_{\ast}
it holds that
\tau=\infty
or
X_{\tau}<0
. Note that if
\tau<\infty
we clearly must have that
X_{\tau}\leq 0
. Because if
X_{\tau}>0
then the continuity of the trajectories of
X
implies that
X
must be above zero for all times sufficiently close to
\tau
. But in that case, the intensity
\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})
vanishes for all
t
close to
\tau
, so that
\Lambda_{t}
is constant around
\tau
. Hence,
\Lambda_{t}=\Lambda_{\tau}=\theta
for some
t<\tau
in contradiction to the definition of
\tau
. Thus, it remains to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{\tau}=0,\,\tau<\infty)=0
. We define
\mathcal{Z}=\{t\in[0,T]\mathpunct{:}X_{t}=0\}
and compute

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{\tau}=0,\,\tau<\infty)&\leq\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau\in\mathcal{Z})=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Lambda_{\tau}\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}})=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}})\\
&\leq\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}})\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t,\end{split}

(3.9)

where
\Lambda_{A}=\{\Lambda_{t}\mathpunct{:}t\in A\}
for a subset
A
of
[0,T]
and where we applied Tonelli’s theorem in the last equality. Note that
\mathcal{Z}
is closed as the preimage of a closed set under a continuous function and contained in the bounded set
[0,T]
, so that it is compact. Since
\Lambda
is continuous, this implies that
\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
is compact and, therefore, measurable. Hence, the expression
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\theta\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}})
and similar ones make sense. We will show that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t
vanishes
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-almost surely. First, we write

\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\lim_{s\to\infty}\int_{0}^{s}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\lim_{s\to T}\int_{0}^{\Lambda_{s}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t.

(3.10)

This is possible because all elements of
\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
are contained in the interval
[0,\Lambda_{T}]
. Next, we apply the chain rule to obtain

\int_{0}^{\Lambda_{s}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{s}\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{t}\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t,

where we used that
\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})
is the time derivative of
\Lambda_{t}
. But now we claim that if
\Lambda_{t}\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
, then
\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})
vanishes, so that the integral is zero. To prove the claim assume that
\Lambda_{t}\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}
, so that we find a
u\in\mathcal{Z}
with
\Lambda_{t}=\Lambda_{u}
. Let us choose the minimal such
u
, so that we have
u\leq t
. Then we can conclude that either
u=t
, in which case
t\in\mathcal{Z}
so that
X_{t}=0
and, therefore,
\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})=0
, or
(u,t)\neq\emptyset
. In the former case we are done, so let us assume that
(u,t)\neq\emptyset
. Since
\Lambda
is nondecreasing,
\Lambda
must be constant on
[u,t]
. But by Assumption
2.2

(iii)
, it holds that
\lambda(r,x,\nu)>0
whenever
x<0
. Hence, if
X
were negative at any of the times in
[u,t]
, then it would be negative on some nonempty open subset of
(u,t)
, which would lead
\Lambda
to increase on
[u,t]
. This yields a contradiction, so it must hold that
X_{r}\geq 0
on
[u,t]
, so in particular we get that
X_{t}=0
which means that
\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})=0
. This proves the claim and thus we get
\int_{0}^{\Lambda_{s}}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=\int_{0}^{s}\lambda(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\mathbf{1}_{\Lambda_{t}\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=0
. We substitute this back into Equation (
3.10
) to find that
\int_{0}^{\infty}\mathbf{1}_{t\in\Lambda_{\mathcal{Z}}}\,\mathrm{d}t=0
. In view of Equation (
3.9
), we deduce that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{\tau}=0,\,\tau<\infty)=0
as required.

Step 2
: From Step 1 we know that the set
\{X_{\tau}<0,\,\tau<\infty\}\cup\{\tau=\infty\}
has full
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-measure, so it is enough to show continuity of
\tau
on this set. We start with
\{X_{\tau}<0,\,\tau<\infty\}
. Fix
\omega\in\{X_{\tau}<0,\,\tau<\infty\}
as well as a sequence
(\omega_{n})_{n}
in
\Omega_{\ast}
that converges to
\omega
. We show that
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})=\tau(\omega)
. Since
X_{\tau(\omega)}(\omega)<0
, for any
\delta>0
we can choose
t\in[\tau(\omega),\tau(\omega)+\delta/2]
, such that
X_{t}(\omega)<0
. By continuity of
X
, we find a further
\delta_{0}>0
with
t+\delta_{0}\leq\tau(\omega)+\delta
and the property that
X_{s}(\omega)<0
whenever
s\in[t,t+\delta_{0}]
. Since
\lambda
is positive on the negative half-line (cf. Assumption
2.2

(iii)
), we get that

\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)+\delta}(\omega)=\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)}(\omega)+\int_{\tau(\omega)}^{\tau(\omega)+\delta}\lambda(s,X_{s}(\omega),\nu_{s}(\omega))\,\mathrm{d}s>\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)}(\omega)+\eta

for some
\eta>0
. Now for large
n\geq 1
, we have that
\bigl\lvert\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)+\delta}(\omega)-\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)+\delta}(\omega_{n})\bigr\rvert\leq\eta/2
and
\lvert\theta(\omega)-\theta(\omega_{n})\rvert\leq\eta/2
, which shows that

\displaystyle\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)+\delta}(\omega_{n})\geq\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)+\delta}(\omega)-\eta/2\geq\Lambda_{\tau(\omega)}(\omega)+\eta/2=\theta(\omega)+\eta/2\geq\theta(\omega_{n}).

Consequently, we have
\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)+\delta
. Since
\delta>0
was arbitrary, it follows that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)
. Next we prove that
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\geq\tau(\omega)
. Firstly, we note that

t_{0}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)<T.

Let us select a subsequence
(\tau(\omega_{n_{k}}))_{k}
for which
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n_{k}})=t_{0}
. Then, since
\Lambda
converges uniformly on compacts, we have

\theta(\omega)=\lim_{k\to\infty}\theta(\omega_{n_{k}})=\lim_{k\to\infty}\Lambda_{\tau(\omega_{n_{k}})}(\omega_{n_{k}})=\Lambda_{t_{0}}(\omega),

which readily implies
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})=t_{0}\geq\tau(\omega)
.

Step 3
: Finally, we prove that
\tau
is continuous on
\{\tau=\infty\}
. If
\tau(\omega)=\infty
for
\omega\in\Omega_{\ast}
, then
\Lambda_{t}(\omega)<\theta(\omega)
for all
t\in[0,T]
. Since both
\Lambda
and
\theta
are continuous in
\omega
, given
t\in[0,T]
, for all
n\geq 1
large enough, we must have
\Lambda_{t}(\omega_{n})<\theta(\omega_{n})
, which implies
\tau(\omega_{n})\geq t
. Since
t\geq 0
was arbitrary, we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})=\infty=\tau(\omega)
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Remark 3.8
.

If we considered an
n
-dimensional state space for
X
, the support of the intensity function
x\mapsto\lambda(t,x,v)
, for given
(t,v)\in[0,T]\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R}^{n})
, would demarcate the region
R
in which particles can be killed. If we assume that this region is independent of
t
and
v
and has a regular boundary, then the proof of Lemma
3.7
applies with minor modifications. Instead of proving
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{\tau}=0,\,\tau<\infty)=0
, we must show that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{\tau}\in\partial R,\,\tau<\infty)=0
. The arguments remain the same.

Using the a.s. continuity of
\tau
we can finally show that
\nu
coincides with the conditional law of the remaining particles.

Proposition 3.9
.

For any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
, it holds
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. that
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
for all
t\in[0,T]
.

Proof.

We know from Lemma
3.7
that
\tau
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Moreover, by Lemma
3.6
the process
\Lambda
is independent of
\theta
conditional on
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
, so since
\theta
follows an exponential distribution under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\cdot|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
it holds for any
t\in[0,T]
that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Delta I_{t}>0)=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Lambda_{t}=\theta|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=0
and
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Delta I_{T}>0)=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Lambda_{T}=\theta|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=0
. Thus, it follows from the definition of
J1
-convergence that
I
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous as a function
\Omega_{\ast}\to D[0,T]
.
But then for any
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and
\Phi\in C_{b}(\Omega_{0})
we have

\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle\Phi(\Theta^{0})]=\lim_{k\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\langle\nu^{N_{k}}_{t},\varphi\rangle\Phi(\Theta^{{N_{k}},0})\bigr]=\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[I^{i}_{t}\varphi(X^{i}_{t})\Phi(\Theta^{{N_{k}},0})\bigr]

for some subsequence
(N_{k})_{k\geq 1}
. The expression
I^{i}_{t}\varphi(X^{i}_{t})\Phi(\Theta^{N,0})
is a
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous function of the random variable
\Theta^{N_{k},i}
. Since
\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}\mathcal{L}(\Theta^{N_{k},i})
converges weakly to
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
by Lemma
3.5
, the continuous mapping theorem implies that

\lim_{k\to\infty}\frac{1}{N_{k}}\sum_{i=1}^{N_{k}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[I^{i}_{t}\varphi(X^{i}_{t})\Phi(\Theta^{N_{k},0})\bigr]=\mathbb{E}\bigl[I_{t}\varphi(X_{t})\Phi(\Theta^{0})\bigr].

Since
\Phi
was arbitrary and the random variables
(\Phi(\Theta^{0}))_{\varphi\in C(\Omega_{0})}
generate
\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}
, it holds that
\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[I_{t}\varphi(X_{t})|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}]
almost surely, so that
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|F^{0}_{T})
a.s. for all
t\in[0,T]
.
Since both sides are a.s. càdlàg in
t
, equality holds for all times almost surely.
∎

3.3
The Controlled Martingale Problem

In this subsection, we define a controlled martingale problem associated to the relaxed formulation of the mean-field control problem from Definition
2.7
. Furthermore, we prove that subsequential limits of the particle system solve the martingale problem and, hence, yield admissible relaxed control rules. Let us fix admissible controls
\gamma^{1}
, …,
\gamma^{N}
, for which
\sup_{N\geq 1}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
and such that
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\Gamma^{i}}\bigr)_{N}
is tight on
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G))
.

We introduce the differential operator
\mathcal{L}
acting on twice continuously differentiable functions
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}\varphi(t,x,y

\displaystyle,z,v,g)

\displaystyle=\bigl(b(t,x,v,g)-\alpha(t,x,v)\langle v,\lambda(t,\cdot,v)\rangle\bigr)\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +a(t,x,v)\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\sigma(t,x,v)\partial_{xy}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\sigma_{0}(t,x,v)\partial_{xz}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)

for
(t,x,y,z,v,g)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
with
a(t,x,v)=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{2}(t,x,v)+\sigma_{0}^{2}(t,x,v))
. The operator
\mathcal{L}
is the infinitesimal generator of the process
(X,W,B)
under an admissible relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
(see Definition
2.7
). Next, for any
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
we define the process
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
by

\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\omega)=\varphi(x_{t},w_{t},b_{t})-\int_{[0,t]\times G}\mathcal{L}\varphi(s,x_{s},w_{s},b_{s},v_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)

(3.11)

for
\omega=(x,w,\mathfrak{g},p,m,v,b)\in\Omega_{\ast}
. We prove that
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is a martingale under any probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
induced by a subsequential limit of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
.

Proposition 3.10
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Then for any
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the process
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is an
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
for any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
. In particular, the limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is an admissible relaxed control rule in the sense of Definition
2.7
.

Proof.

Let us associate to any continuous and bounded function
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
and
t\in[0,T]
a map
\Phi_{t}\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
defined by

\Phi_{t}(x,w,\mathfrak{g},p,m,v,b)=\Phi(x_{t\land\cdot},w_{t\land\cdot},\mathfrak{g}_{t},0,\pi_{t}^{\#}m,v_{t\land\cdot},b_{t\land\cdot}),

where
\pi_{t}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}
is given by
(x,w,\mathfrak{g},p)\mapsto(x_{t\land\cdot},w,\gamma_{t},p)
. That is,
\Phi_{t}
arises from
\Phi
by stopping the inputs at time
t
and removing the dependence on the exponential time. It follows from a straightforward monotone class argument that the
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t}
is generated by the random variables
\Phi_{t}(\Theta)
, where
\Phi
ranges over the space
C_{b}(\Omega_{\ast})
. Thus, establishing the martingale property of
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
boils down to showing that

\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\Theta)\bigr]=0

(3.12)

for any
\Phi\in C_{b}(\Omega_{\ast})
and
0\leq s\leq t\leq T
, where we recall that
\Theta=(X,W,\Gamma,\theta,\mu,\nu,B)
and
\Theta^{0}=(\mu,\nu,B)
. We will approximate the expectation on the left-hand side through the particle system and then use our knowledge of the particle system to conclude Equation (
3.12
). To perform the approximation argument, we need to know that the expressions in the expectation are
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous in
\omega\in\Omega_{\ast}
. We shall show this next.

The map
\Phi_{t}
is continuous at any point
(x,w,\mathfrak{g},p,m,v,b)
, for which
x
and
v
have continuous trajectories and
m(C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\times[0,\infty))=1
. To verify the last assertion simply note the projection
\pi_{t}
is continuous on
C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\times[0,\infty)\subset\mathcal{S}
, so by the continuous mapping theorem the pushforward
\pi^{\#}_{t}
is continuous at distributions with
m(C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\times[0,\infty))=1
. Let us check that the desired properties hold
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-almost surely. The processes
X
and
\nu
are
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous by Lemma
3.5
and Proposition
3.9
, respectively. Next, since
\mu=\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X,W,\Gamma,\theta|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
we get that

\mu\Bigl(C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\times[0,\infty)\Bigr)=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}\bigl(X\in C([0,T])\big|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}\bigr).

But
\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X\in C([0,T])|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X\in C([0,T]))=1
, so that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. it holds that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(X\in C([0,T])|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=1
. Consequently, the pushforward
\pi^{\#}_{t}
is continuous at
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(\mu)
-a.e. element of
\mathcal{P}(\mathcal{S})
and we can conclude that
\Phi_{t}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous.

Now we choose
E_{1}=E_{2}=E_{3}=\mathbb{R}
,
E_{4}=\mathbf{M}
, and
\Phi=\mathcal{L}\varphi
in Lemma
A.7
to deduce that along a suitable subsequence

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\Rightarrow\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\Theta)

Here
\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i}=(X^{i},W^{i},\Gamma^{i},\theta_{i},\tilde{\mu}^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})
and
\tilde{\Theta}^{N,0}=(\tilde{\mu}^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})
, where we define
\tilde{\mu}^{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X^{i},W^{i},\Gamma^{i}}\otimes\text{Exp}(1)
. We need to work with
\tilde{\mu}^{N}
instead of
\mu^{N}
, because the processes
M^{j}
,
j=1
,…,
N
, are not martingales with respect to filtrations which include
\mathbb{F}^{\mu^{N}}
, since
\mu^{N}_{0}
anticipates information about the exponential times
\theta_{1}
, …,
\theta_{N}
. However, the martingale property of
M^{j}
is crucial in the argument below. Since
\tilde{\mu}^{N}
converges to
\mu
along the same subsequence as
\mu^{N}
, we can simply replace
\mu^{N}
by
\tilde{\mu}^{N}
. As
\Omega_{\ast}\ni\omega_{\ast}\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{u}(\omega_{\ast})
is of linear growth for any
u\in[0,T]
and
\Phi_{t}
is bounded, while

\sup_{N\geq 1}\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sup_{0\leq u\leq T}\bigl(\lvert X^{N,i}_{u}\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\nu^{N}_{u})\bigr)+M_{2}^{2}(\Gamma^{N,i})\biggr]<\infty

by Lemma
3.1
, we deduce from Lemma
3.5
that

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\Bigr]\to\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\Theta)\bigr]

along the same subsequence as above. Thus, to conclude (
3.12
) it suffices to show that the left-hand side above vanishes in the limit
N\to\infty
. Applying Itô’s formula for jump diffusions to
\varphi(X^{i},W^{i},W^{0})
shows that

\displaystyle\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}

\displaystyle(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})

\displaystyle=\int_{s}^{t}\bigl(\sigma(u,X^{i}_{u},\nu^{N}_{u})\partial_{x}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})+\partial_{y}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}W^{i}_{u}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\int_{s}^{t}\bigl(\sigma_{0}(u,X^{i}_{u},\nu^{N}_{u})\partial_{x}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})+\partial_{z}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{u}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{s}^{t}\alpha(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\partial_{x}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})\,\mathrm{d}M^{j}_{u}+E^{N,i}_{t}-E^{N,i}_{s},

where the error term
E^{N,i}
is given by
E^{N,i}_{t}=\sum_{0\leq u\leq t}\Delta\varphi(X^{i}_{u})-\partial_{x}\varphi(X^{i}_{u-})\Delta X^{i}_{u}
. Lemma
3.2
states that the process
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\partial_{x}\varphi(X^{i}_{u},W^{i}_{u},W^{0}_{u})\,\mathrm{d}M^{j}_{u}
is a martingale with respect to the filtration
\mathbb{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}
defined in Equation (
3.1
). Obviously, the same is true for the integrals with respect to the Brownian motions
W^{i}
and
W^{0}
. Since
\Phi_{s}(\cdot,\tilde{\Theta}^{N,0})
is
\mathcal{F}^{N,\mathbf{I}}_{s}
-measurable, we obtain

\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\Bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\tilde{\Theta}^{N,i})\Bigr]=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(E^{N,j}_{t}-E^{N,j}_{s})\Phi_{s}(\cdot,\tilde{\Theta}^{N,0})\bigr].

To show that the right-hand side converges to zero, we prove that the error terms
E^{N,i}_{t}-E^{N,i}_{s}
are of order
\frac{1}{N}
. Using Taylor’s theorem together with the two identities
\Delta X^{i}_{u}=-\alpha(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\Delta L^{N}_{u}
and
X^{i}_{u}=X^{i}_{u-}-\alpha(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\Delta L^{N}_{u}
yields

\displaystyle E^{N,i}_{t}-E^{N,i}_{s}=\sum_{s\leq u\leq t}\alpha^{2}(u,X^{i}_{u-},\nu^{N}_{u-})\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(\zeta^{i}_{u})(\Delta L^{N}_{u})^{2}

for some
\zeta^{i}_{u}\in[X^{i}_{u-},X^{i}_{u}]
. Since
L^{N}
jumps at most
N
times and each jump has size
1/N
, this yields
\lvert E^{N,i}_{t}-E^{N,i}_{s}\rvert\leq C_{\alpha}^{2}\lVert\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi\rVert_{\infty}N^{-1}
as desired. Consequently,
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
.

Lastly, we verify that
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is an admissible relaxed control rule. First, note that
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Indeed, the integral appearing in Equation (
3.11
) varies continuously with
t
, since the time marginal of
\Gamma
is the Lebesgue measure. Moreover, the process
X
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous by Lemma
3.5
, so the same holds for
t\mapsto\varphi(X_{t},W_{t},W^{0}_{t})
. Then, combining the martingale property of
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
with Theorem II.7.1 from
[
24
]
, we conclude that
X
satisfies the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.10
) under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
, which gives Item
(iii)
in Definition
2.7
. The bound on the second moment of
X
and
\Gamma
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
follows from Lemma
3.1
and the tightness of
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\Gamma^{i}}\bigr)_{N}
. The identity
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X_{0},\theta)=\nu_{0}\otimes\text{Exp}(1)
is obvious. Finally, Item
(ii)
is a consequence of Lemmas
3.5
and
3.6
as well as the stability of independence under weak convergence, see
[
13
, Proposition 4.17]
for details.
∎

We proved that any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
converges to an admissible relaxed control rule. Next, we show that the associated costs converge as well.

Proposition 3.11
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Let
(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})_{n}
be a uniformly square-integrable sequence of probability measures on
\Omega_{0}
that converges to a relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
. Then we have
\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
.

Proof.

First, we note that since
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is an admissible control rule, it satisfies the assumptions of Lemma
3.7
, so that
\tau
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Now we use Corollary
A.8
with
E_{1}=\mathbb{R}
,
E_{2}=\mathbf{M}
, and
\Phi=f
to see that the law of
F=\int_{[0,\tau\land T]\times G}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(F)
. Here
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}
is the probability measure on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0}
(cf. (
2.7
)). Then, since the family
(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})_{n}
is uniformly square-integrable and the running cost function
f
has at most quadratic growth in its last three variables uniformly in
t\in[0,T]
by Assumption
2.2

(iv)
, the laws
F^{\#}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, are uniformly integrable. Consequently, we get that
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}}F\to\mathbb{E}_{\ast}F
as
n\to\infty
by the continuity of
f
in
(x,\nu,g)
from Assumption
2.2

(v)
.

Next, let us analyse the terminal cost. We know that
\nu^{\#}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(\nu)
on
D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
equipped with the
J1
-topology. However,
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is an admissible control rule, so the trajectories of
\nu
are
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Since a
J1
-convergent sequence with continuous limit converges uniformly, the weak convergence of
\nu^{\#}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}
to
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(\nu)
holds with respect to the uniform topology on
D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
. Consequently, the law of
\nu_{T}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}
converges weakly to
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(\nu_{T})
. Now we note again that the family
(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})_{n}
is uniformly square integrable and that the terminal cost function
\psi
has at most quadratic growth and is continuous by Assumptions
2.2

(iv)
and
(v)
, so that
\mathbb{E}^{\mathbb{P}_{\ast}^{n}}\psi(\nu_{T})\to\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\psi(\nu_{T})
as
n\to\infty
. Combining the convergence of both running and terminal cost yields
\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{n})=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
.
∎

3.4
Existence, Uniqueness, and Stability of the Mean-Field Limit

In this subsection, we introduce an alternative formulation of the relaxed control setup from Definition
2.7
. Our goal is to then apply the theory in
[
13
]
, which establishes the equivalence of the strong and relaxed formulation for mean-field control.
[
13
]
proves the equivalence of both formulations for McKean-Vlasov SDEs with Lipschitz continuous coefficients, whereas in our case the coefficients are only locally Lipschitz continuous in the measure argument. The key properties
[
13
]
requires for their proof are existence, uniqueness, and stability in the control argument of the state equation. We show that these properties also hold for locally Lipschitz coefficients.

In the following, let us fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with two filtrations
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{F}
with
\mathcal{G}_{t}\subset\mathcal{F}_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
, an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable random variable
\xi
with finite second moment, an exponentially distributed random variable
\theta
, and two
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motions
W
and
W^{0}
. We assume that
W^{0}
is adapted to
\mathbb{G}
, the pair
(\xi,W)
is independent of
\mathcal{G}_{T}
, and
\theta
is independent from
\mathcal{F}_{T}
. Finally, we require that for all
t\in[0,T]
we have
\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{T})
a.s. for all
A\in\mathcal{F}_{t}\lor\sigma(W)
.

We say that a
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variable
\Gamma
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
if for all
t\in[0,T]
, the random variable
\Gamma([0,s]\times A)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for any
s\in[0,t]
and
A\in\mathcal{B}(G)
. The set of
admissible relaxed controls
consists of all
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variables
\Gamma
for which
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)<\infty
. For a given admissible relaxed control
\Gamma
we consider the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}X_{t}=\xi&+\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X_{s},\nu_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(s,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}\\
&+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{0}(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{s}-\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\langle\nu_{s},\lambda(s,\cdot,\nu_{s})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s\end{split}

(3.13)

with
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
and
\Lambda_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
.

When say that the pair
(X,\nu)
is a
strong solution
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
3.13
) if (i) the process
X
is a strong solution to (
3.13
) when viewed as an SDE with random coefficients, the randomness coming from the mean-field component
\nu_{t}
, and (ii)
\nu_{t}
is the conditional subprobability distribution of
X_{t}
with respect to
\mathcal{G}_{T}
, so there is no additional external information in the conditioning.

Proposition 3.12
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. For any admissible relaxed control
\Gamma
the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
3.13
) has a unique strong solution. If
b
does not depend on the control, i.e.
b(t,x,\nu,g)=b_{0}(t,x,\nu)
for some function
b_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to\mathbb{R}
, then
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
for all
t\in[0,T]
almost surely.

Moreover, if
(\epsilon_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of positive real numbers tending to zero and
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of admissible relaxed controls such that
\mathbb{E}W^{2}_{2}(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)\to 0
, then
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Here
X^{n}
is the unique strong solution to SDE (
3.13
) started from
\xi
at time
\epsilon_{n}
with control
\Gamma_{n}
.

In the statement of the proposition starting
X^{n}
from
\xi
at time
\epsilon_{n}
means that
X^{n}
solves the SDE (
3.13
) on the interval
[\epsilon_{n},T]
with initial condition
X^{n}_{\epsilon_{n}}=\xi
.

Proof of Proposition
3.12
.

Both statements follow from a simple application of Proposition
A.2
. To get SDE (
3.13
) into the same form as SDE (
A.1
), we replace the coefficients
b
,
\sigma
, and
\sigma_{0}
of the latter by the functions

\displaystyle\begin{split}(t,(x,y)&,\mu,g)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}b(t,x,\Phi(\mu),g)-\alpha(t,x,\Phi(\mu))\langle\Phi(\mu),\lambda(t,\cdot,\Phi(\mu))\rangle\\
\lambda(t,x,\Phi(\mu))\end{pmatrix},\\
&(t,(x,y),\mu)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\sigma(t,x,\Phi(\mu))\\
0\end{pmatrix},\quad(t,(x,y),\mu)\mapsto\begin{pmatrix}\sigma_{0}(t,x,\Phi(\mu))\\
0\end{pmatrix},\end{split}

(3.14)

where
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathbf{M}
is defined by
\langle\Phi(\mu),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-y}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y)
for
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}
continuous and bounded.
Now, one simply needs to verify Assumption
A.1
for the coefficients in (
3.14
). We will not provide the details here.
∎

Corollary 3.13
.

Let Assumption
2.2
be satisfied. Fix an admissible relaxed control
\Gamma
and denote the associated solution to the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
3.13
) by
X
. Let
n\geq 1
and define
t^{n}_{i}=T\frac{i}{n}
for
i=0
, …
n
. Then there exists a sequence of bounded
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
with the following properties

(i)

\gamma^{n}_{0}=g_{0}
for some
g_{0}\in G
and
\gamma^{n}
is constant on the intervals
[t^{n}_{i},t^{n}_{i+1})
,
i=0
, …,
n-1
;

(ii)

\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Here
\Gamma_{n}
is defined by
\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{n}(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma^{n}_{t}}(g)\mathrm{d}t
. In particular, it holds that
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
, where
X^{n}
is the unique strong solution to SDE (
3.13
) started from
\xi
at time
t^{n}_{1}
with control
\Gamma_{n}
.

Proof.

First of all, we may assume that the set
G
is bounded. Otherwise we define a sequence of measures
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
by
(\id_{[0,T]}\times\pi_{n})^{\#}\Gamma
, where
\pi_{n}\mathpunct{:}G\to G
is some measurable function with
\pi_{n}(g)=g
whenever
\lvert g\rvert\leq n
and
\lvert\pi_{n}(g)\rvert\leq n
. Then, we estimate the
W_{2}
-distance between
\Gamma
and
\Gamma_{n}
by the cost of the coupling induced by the transport map
\id_{[0,T]}\times\pi_{n}
from
\Gamma
to
\Gamma_{n}
gives

\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(\Gamma,\Gamma_{n})\leq\mathbb{E}\int_{[0,T]\times G}\lvert t-t\rvert^{2}+\lvert g-\pi_{n}(g)\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)\leq 2\mathbb{E}\int_{[0,T]\times G}\mathbf{1}_{\lvert g\rvert\geq n}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g).

The right-hand side converges to zero as
n\to\infty
. Hence, we may successively approximate the elements of the sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
instead of
\Gamma
and, thus, can assume
G
is bounded.

Next we reduce the problem to controls
\Gamma
of the form
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\delta_{\gamma_{t}}(g)\mathrm{d}t
for an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma
. If
\Gamma
is not of that form, we can find a sequence
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
of
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes with associated measure
\Gamma_{n}
, for which almost surely
\lim_{n\to\infty}W_{2}(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)=0
, so that
\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)\to 0
by Vitali’s convergence theorem. Indeed, the former is guaranteed by the chattering lemma (see e.g.
[
28
, Theorem 2.2(b)]
), which is applicable since
G
is convex by Assumption
2.2
.

The final approximation step from piecewise constant to
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable controls is provided by
[
35
, Lemma 4.4]
.
∎

3.5
Proof of Theorems
2.4
and
2.8
and Corollary
2.9

We start with the proof of Theorem
2.8
as it implies Theorem
2.4
.

Proof of Theorem
2.8
.

First we establish the equality
V_{0}=V
, where
V_{0}
is the infimum of
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
over admissible relaxed control rules and
V
denotes the optimal cost over admissible strong controls. We achieve this by approximating an arbitrary admissible relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}
by a sequence of admissible (strong) controls
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
. First note that under the probability distribution
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
, the random measure
\Gamma^{\ast}
is an admissible relaxed control as defined in the paragraph above Proposition
3.12
and
X^{\ast}
solves the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
3.13
). Now Corollary
3.13
reformulates the conclusions of Lemma 4.3 in
[
13
]
in the context of our setup. Thus, we can execute the subsequent programme in
[
13
]
, Lemma 4.4 and Proposition 4.5, to find a sequence
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
of
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes on the probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
for which
\bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{n}
is uniformly integrable and with the following property: if we set
\mu^{n}=\mathcal{L}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\theta|W^{0})
, where
\Gamma^{n}
is the relaxed control associated to
\gamma^{n}
,
X^{n}
is the unique strong solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
3.13
) with
\mathbb{G}=\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
, and
\nu^{n}=(\mathbb{P}(X^{n}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\Lambda^{n}_{t}>\theta|W^{0}))_{t\in[0,T]}
; and let
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0}
denote the law of
(\mu^{n},\nu^{n},W^{0})
, then
(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0})_{n}
is uniformly square-integrable and
\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0}\Rightarrow\mathbb{P}_{0}
. Thus, by Proposition
3.11
we get that
\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0})=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
. Note that since
X^{n}
solves the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
), we get that
J(\gamma^{n})\geq V
.

Now, we specifically choose
\mathbb{P}_{0}
such that
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\leq V_{0}+\epsilon
for a fixed
\epsilon>0
. This implies

V\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}J(\gamma^{n})=\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathbb{P}^{n}_{0})=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\leq V_{0}+\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
gives
V\leq V_{0}
. Since every
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process
\gamma
with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma<\infty
induces an admissible control rule, we also have the reverse inequality
V_{0}\leq V
, whence
V_{0}=V
.

Next we show that
\lim_{N\to\infty}V^{N}=V_{0}
. We start with the inequality
\liminf_{N\to\infty}V^{N}\geq V_{0}
. Let
(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
be a sequence of
\epsilon_{N}
-optimal controls. Then appealing to the coercivity condition from Assumption
2.2

(vi)
, we get that
\bigl(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{i}_{t}\rvert^{p_{f}}\,\mathrm{d}t\bigr)_{N}
is uniformly integrable, which implies that
(\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{\Gamma^{N,i}})
is tight on
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G))
by Proposition B.1 of
[
29
]
. Here we use that
G
is closed by Assumption
2.2
. Thus the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
is tight on
\Omega_{0}
by Proposition
3.4
. Next, Proposition
3.10
states that any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
induces an admissible relaxed control rule and the cost
J^{N}(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})=J_{0}(\mathcal{L}(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0}))
converges subsequentially to
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\geq V_{0}
by Proposition
3.11
. Since the sequence of controls
(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
is
\epsilon_{N}
-optimal, we obtain that

\liminf_{N\to\infty}V^{N}=\liminf_{N\to\infty}V^{N}+\epsilon_{N}\geq\liminf_{N\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathcal{L}(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0}))\geq V_{0}.

It remains to demonstrate that
\limsup_{N\to\infty}V^{N}\leq V
. Let us fix an arbitrary
\epsilon>0
and choose an
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process
\gamma
with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\gamma<\infty
and
J(\gamma)\leq V+\epsilon
. Since
\gamma
is
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable, we can find a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\to\mathbb{R}
such that
\gamma_{t}=g(t,\xi,W_{\cdot\land t},W^{0}_{\cdot\land t})
for
\textup{Leb}\otimes\mathbb{P}
-almost every
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
. Now we set
\gamma^{N,i}_{t}=g(t,\xi_{i},W^{i}_{\cdot\land t},W^{0}_{\cdot\land t})
. Then it is not difficult to show that the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})_{N}
induced by the controls
(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
converges weakly to the admissible relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}
induced by the unique strong solution
X
of the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) with control
\gamma
. Moreover, the sequence
(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})_{N}
is uniformly square-integrable, so applying Proposition
3.11
once again shows that

\displaystyle\limsup_{N\to\infty}V^{N}

\displaystyle\leq\lim_{N\to\infty}J^{N}(\gamma^{N,1},\dots,\gamma^{N,N})=\lim_{N\to\infty}J_{0}(\mathcal{L}(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0}))

\displaystyle=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})=J(\gamma)\leq V+\epsilon.

Since
\epsilon>0
was arbitrary, we get
\limsup_{N\to\infty}V^{N}\leq V=V_{0}
as desired.

Combining everything shows that
V=V_{0}\leq\liminf_{N\to\infty}V^{N}\leq\limsup_{N\to\infty}V^{N}\leq V
. This concludes the proof.
∎

Now we can prove Theorem
2.4
.

Proof of Theorem
2.4
.

We note that the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) has a unique strong solution for every
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued control
\gamma
for which the expectation
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t
is finite by Proposition
3.12
. The second statement of Theorem
2.4
follows immediately from the convergence
V^{N}\to V
established in Theorem
2.8
.
∎

Lastly, we move to the proof of Corollary
2.9
. Recall that Corollary
2.9
assumes that
b
is of the form
b(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}(t,x,v)
, so there is no control present.

Proof of Corollary
2.9
.

Since
b
does not depend on
g
, the particle system is exchangeable. By Theorem
2.8
the sequence
(\mu^{N},\nu^{N},W^{0})
subsequentially converges to a probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{0}
on
\Omega_{0}
such that
(X^{\ast},\nu^{\ast})
solves the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.10
) under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
, where
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is the probability measure on
\Omega_{\ast}
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
. Using the exchangeability of the particle system together with Lemma
3.5
gives that

\mathcal{L}(X^{N,1},\nu^{N})=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathcal{L}(X^{N,i},\nu^{N})\Rightarrow\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast},\nu^{\ast})

on
D[0,T]\times D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
along the subsequence from above. By Proposition
3.12
, the process
(X^{\ast},\nu^{\ast})
is the unique strong solution to the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) on the probability space
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
with idiosyncratic noise
W^{\ast}
and common noise
B^{\ast}
. Hence, by the Yamada-Watanabe theorem
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(X^{\ast},\nu^{\ast})
is equal to the law of the unique strong solution
(X,\nu)
to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) (on the setup
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
with noises
W
and
W^{0}
). Thus,
\mathcal{L}(X^{N,1},\nu^{N})
converges subsequentially to
\mathcal{L}(X,\nu)
on
D[0,T]\times D_{\mathbf{M}}[0,T]
. However, by the weak uniqueness of
(X,\nu)
, the weak limit of
\mathcal{L}(X^{N,1},\nu^{N})
along any other subsequence must also coincide with
\mathcal{L}(X,\nu)
so that the weak convergence actually holds along the entire sequence.
∎

4
Convergence to the Singular Limit

In this section, we analyse the convergence of the regularised system as we let the regularisation vanish. This will culminate in the proof of Theorem
2.14
as well as Propositions
2.17
and
2.19
.

4.1
Subsequential Convergence of the Regularised Sequence

Here as well as in the subsequent three subsections we adopt the convention from Subsection
3.2
and drop the asterisk in the superscript of variables defined on
\Omega_{\ast}
. Note that this means that we will use the same notation for the idiosyncratic noises on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
, but in each case it will be clear from context which random variable we are referring to.

We fix a sequence
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
of admissible strong controls, that is
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued processes
\gamma^{n}
with
\int_{0}^{T}\mathbb{E}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
, and further assume that the family
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Then we let
X^{n}
be the solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.16
) with input
\gamma^{n}
and denote the relaxed control associated to
\gamma^{n}
by
\Gamma^{n}
. The objective of this section is to establish the subsequential weak convergence of the family
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
, with
\mu^{n}=\mathcal{L}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n}|W^{0})
, on
\Omega_{0}
to an admissible relaxed control rule (for the singular model), cf. Definition
2.12
. First, we note that similar to Lemma
3.1
we can show that the family
((\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2})_{n}
is uniformly integrable. This allows us to deduce the following tightness result.

Proposition 4.1
.

Let Assumption
2.13
be satisfied and suppose that
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Then the sequence
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
is tight on
\Omega_{0}
.

Proof.

Clearly
(W^{0})_{n}
is tight, so we only need to worry about tightness of
(\mu^{n})_{n}
. By
[
41
, Proposition 2.2 (ii)]
this follows from tightness of
(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n})_{n}
. We establish the tightness of each component separately. Tightness of
(\Gamma^{n})_{n}
follows from the uniform integrability of the sequence
(M_{2}^{2}(\Gamma^{n}))_{n}
of second moments, while
(W)_{n}
is trivially tight. Thus, it remains to prove tightness of
(X^{n})_{n}
. We write
X^{n}=\xi+\beta^{n}+Z^{n}-F^{n}_{t}
with

\beta^{n}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}b(s,X^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s},\gamma^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s,\qquad Z^{n}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,X^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{0}(s,X^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{s},

and
F^{n}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\alpha(s)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{s}
for
t\in[0,T+1]
, where we recall that we extended the coefficients
b
,
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\alpha
to
[0,T+1]
in a suitable way, see the paragraph above Definition
2.12
. Further setting
\beta^{n}_{t}=Z^{n}_{t}=F^{n}_{t}=0
for
t\in[-1,0)
turns
\beta^{n}
,
Z^{n}
, and
F^{n}
into random variables with values in
C([-1,T+1])
,
C([-1,T+1])
, and
D[-1,T+1]
, respectively. We will prove that as such the sequences
(\beta^{n})_{n}
,
(Z^{n})_{n}
,
(F^{n})_{n}
are tight, which immediately implies tightness of
(X^{n})_{n}
on
D[-1,T+1]
. We begin by analysing the process
\beta^{n}
. For any
s
,
t\in[0,T]
it holds that

\lvert\beta^{n}_{t}-\beta^{n}_{s}\rvert^{2}=\biggl\lvert\int_{s}^{t}b^{n}_{u}\,\mathrm{d}u\biggr\rvert^{2}\leq\lvert t-s\rvert\int_{s}^{t}\lvert b^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq\lvert t-s\rvert\int_{0}^{T}\lvert b^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u,

where
b^{n}_{u}=b(u,X^{n}_{u},\nu^{n}_{u},\gamma^{n}_{u})
. As we mentioned at the beginning of the section, the family
((\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2})_{n}
is uniformly integrable, which implies that the quantity
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert b^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u
is bounded uniformly in
n\geq 1
. Thus, for any
\epsilon>0
, we can find
R>0
large enough, such that
\mathbb{P}\bigl(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert b^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq R^{2}\bigr)\geq 1-\epsilon
. Now we define
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}\subset C([-1,T+1])
to be the set of functions
f\mathpunct{:}[-1,T+1]\to\mathbb{R}
, such that
f(t)=0
for
t\in[-1,0]
and

\lvert f(t)-f(s)\rvert\leq R\lvert t-s\rvert^{\frac{1}{2}}

for all
s
,
t\in[0,T+1]
. Clearly, the set
\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon}
is a compact subset of
C([-1,T+1])
, and

\mathbb{P}(\beta^{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{\epsilon})\geq\mathbb{P}\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert b^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}u\leq R^{2}\biggr)\geq 1-\epsilon.

Consequently, the laws
\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(\beta^{n})
,
n\geq 1
, form a tight sequence on
C([-1,T+1])
and any subsequential limit is concentrated on the space of functions with
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous trajectories.

Next, the bound
\sigma^{2}(t,x,\nu)+\sigma_{0}^{2}(t,x,\nu)\leq 2C_{\sigma}^{2}
implied by Assumption
2.2

(i)
together with Kolmogorov’s tightness criterion imply that the sequence
(\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}}(Z^{n}))_{n}
is tight on
C([-1,T+1])
.

Finally, we show that
(\mathcal{L}(F^{n}))_{n}
form a tight family on
D[-1,T+1]
. It follows from Theorem 12.12.2 of
[
43
]
that the set of nondecreasing càdlàg functions on
[-1,T+1]
, which are started from zero, constant on
[-1,0)
and
[T+1/2,T]
(recall that
\alpha
vanishes on
[T+1/2,T]
), and bounded by
\lVert\alpha\rVert_{\infty}
, form a compact subset of
D[-1,T+1]
. Every member of the sequence
(F^{n})_{n}
is a.s. a member of this set because
\alpha
is nonnegative by Assumption
2.13

(i)
, so
(\mathcal{L}(F^{n}))_{n}
is tight.
∎

Recall that
\mathbf{M}=\mathcal{M}^{1}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
and denote by
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
the space of
\mathbf{M}
-valued measurable functions
v
on
[-1,T+1]
with
\int_{-1}^{T+1}M_{1}^{2}(v_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. We endow
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
with the metric

(v^{1},v^{2})\mapsto\biggl(\int_{-1}^{T+1}d_{1}^{2}(v^{1}_{t},v^{2}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr)^{1/2},

which turns it into a separable complete metric space. Moreover, the inclusion
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]\to L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is continuous, so that we can view
\nu
, defined in Equation (
2.13
), as a random variable with values in
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
.

The reason for introducing the space
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is that it is not clear whether the sequence
(\nu^{n})_{n}
is tight on
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]
nor whether it converges subsequentially to
\nu
in the space
D_{\mathbf{M}}[-1,T+1]
, cf. Remark
2.15
. We can, however, establish both statements for the space
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
.

Proposition 4.2
.

Let Assumption
2.13
be satisfied and suppose that
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Then for any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
, the map
\nu\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Moreover,
\nu
is the subsequential weak limit of
(\nu^{n})_{n}
on
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
.

Proof.

We proceed in three steps.

Step 1
: We begin by showing that the random time
\tau=\inf\{0<t\leq T+1\mathpunct{:}X_{t}\leq 0\}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Recall the sequence of processes
(\beta^{n},Z^{n},F^{n})_{n}
introduced in Proposition
4.1
. Since the law of these processes is tight on
C([-1,T+1])\times C([-1,T+1])\times D[-1,T+1]
,
X^{n}=\xi+\beta^{n}+Z^{n}+F^{n}
, and
X^{n}
converges subsequentially to the law of
X
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
, by enlarging the probability space
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
if necessary, we can find processes
\beta
,
Z
, and
F
, such that
(\beta,Z,F)
is the subsequential weak limit of
(\beta^{n},Z^{n},F^{n})_{n}
and
X=X_{0}+\beta+Z+F
. We let
\mathbb{G}=(\mathcal{G}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T+1}
be the right-continuous extension of the filtration generated by
X
,
\beta
,
Z
, and
F
.

The proof of Proposition
4.1
shows that
\beta
has
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s.
\frac{1}{2}
-Hölder continuous trajectories. The process
Z
is a continuous
\mathbb{G}
-martingale since the local martingale property is conserved by weak convergence and
Z
is square-integrable. Moreover, by Theorem 6.26 of
[
26
]
it holds that
\langle Z^{n}\rangle
converges weakly on
C([0,T])
to
\langle Z\rangle
along a subsequence, which in view of the nondegeneracy condition, Assumption
2.13

(ii)
, allows us to deduce that
(C_{\sigma}^{-1}\land 1)(t-s)\leq\langle Z\rangle_{t}-\langle Z\rangle_{s}
for
0\leq s\leq t\leq T+1
. The minimum with
1
comes from having
Z
diffuse according to a Brownian motion between time
T
and
T+1
. Lastly, the process
F
is nondecreasing as the weak limit of nondecreasing functions in
D[-1,T+1]
. We will use this decomposition of
X
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
to prove that
X
has the downcrossing property. That is, for
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.e.
\omega\in\{\tau\leq T+1\}
, given any
0\leq\delta\leq T+1-\tau
there exists
t\in[\tau,\tau+\delta]
such that
X_{t}<0
.

First, note that
X^{n}
diffuses according to a Brownian motion on the interval
[T+1/2,T+1]
, so the same holds for
X
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
. Consequently, we have
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau=T+1)=0
and we may assume that
\tau<T+1
. Now, let us denote the smallest (random) constant
C>0
for which
\lvert\beta_{t}-\beta_{s}\rvert\leq C\lvert t-s\rvert^{1/2}
for all
s
,
t\in[0,T_{1}]
by
C_{\beta}
. Here for notational simplicity, we set
T_{1}=T+1
. Then, we define
Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}_{t}=Z_{T_{1}\land(\tau+t)}-Z_{T_{1}\land\tau}
for
t\in[0,T_{1}]
, which is a martingale with respect to the filtration
(\mathcal{G}_{T_{1}\land(\tau+t)})_{0\leq t\leq T_{1}}
by the optional stopping theorem. It has quadratic variation
q_{t}=\langle Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}\rangle_{t}=\langle Z\rangle_{T_{1}\land(\tau+t)}-\langle Z\rangle_{T_{1}\land\tau}
, so by the Dambis-Dubins-Schwarz theorem there is a Brownian motion
M
with
Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}_{t}=M_{q_{t}}
. Hence, on some event
A_{0}\subset\{\tau<T_{1}\}
with
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A_{0})=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau<T_{1})
, we have

\liminf_{t\to 0}\frac{Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}_{t}}{\sqrt{t}}=\liminf_{t\to 0}\frac{M_{q_{t}}}{\sqrt{q_{t}}}\biggl(\frac{q_{t}}{t}\biggr)^{1/2}\leq\liminf_{t\to 0}(C_{\sigma}^{-1/2}\land 1)\frac{M_{q_{t}}}{\sqrt{q_{t}}}=-\infty,

where we used that
\liminf_{t\to 0}\frac{M_{t}}{\sqrt{t}}=-\infty
a.s. together with the bound
(C_{\sigma}^{-1}\land 1)t\leq q_{t}
for
0\leq t\leq T_{1}-\tau
. Consequently, for any
0<\delta\leq T_{1}-\tau
we find
t\in[0,\delta]
, such that

X_{\tau+t}\leq X_{\tau+t}-X_{\tau}=\beta_{\tau+t}-\beta_{\tau}+Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}_{t}+F_{\tau+t}-F_{\tau}\leq\sqrt{t}\biggl(C_{\beta}+\frac{Z^{(T_{1}\land\tau)}_{t}}{\sqrt{t}}\biggr)<0,

where we used that
X_{\tau}\leq 0
and that
F
is nondecreasing. Hence,
X(\omega)
has the downcrossing property for any
\omega\in A_{0}
. We shall use this property to prove that
\tau
is continuous at every
\omega\in A=A_{0}\cup\{\tau=\infty\}
.

First, let us fix
\omega\in A_{0}
as well as a sequence
(\omega_{n})_{n}
in
\Omega_{\ast}
which converges to
\omega
. On the one hand, employing the downcrossing property, for any
0<\delta\leq T+1-\tau
there is a
t\in[\tau(\omega),\tau(\omega)+\delta/2]
with
X_{t}(\omega)<0
. Now
X
has right-continuous trajectories, so we can find a continuity point
s\in[t,\tau(\omega)+\delta]
for which
X_{s}(\omega)
is negative as well. Since
s
is a continuity point of
X(\omega)
, it holds that
X_{s}(\omega_{n})\to X_{s}(\omega)
as
n\to\infty
. In particular, we have that
X_{s}(\omega_{n})<0
for all sufficiently large
n
. Consequently,
\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)+\delta
. Then we let
\delta
go to zero to see
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)
. Next we prove the
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\geq\tau(\omega)
. First note that

t_{0}=\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})\leq\tau(\omega)<T+1.

Then we select a subsequence
(\tau(\omega_{n_{k}}))_{k}
with
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n_{k}})=t_{0}
. By the definition of
M1
-convergence, choosing a further subsequence if necessary, it holds that

0\geq\lim_{k\to\infty}X_{\tau(\omega_{n_{k}})}(\omega_{n_{k}})\in[X_{t_{0}}(\omega),X_{t_{0}-}(\omega)],

where we implicitly use that
X_{t_{0}}(\omega)\leq X_{t_{0}-}(\omega)
since
X(\omega)
can only have negative jumps. In particular, we obtain
X_{t_{0}}(\omega)\leq 0
, whence
\liminf_{n\to\infty}\tau(\omega_{n})=t_{0}\geq\tau(\omega)
as required.

To prove continuity of
\tau
on
A
, it remains to show that
\tau(\omega_{n})\to\infty
whenever
\tau(\omega)=\infty
. But the latter means that
X(\omega)_{t}>0
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
and implies that
X(\omega_{n})>0
on
[0,T+1]
if
n
is large enough, so we get
\tau(\omega_{n})=\infty
.

Step 2
: We proceed by proving that
\nu\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. Let us define the inclusion map
\iota\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{S}\to\Omega_{\ast}
,
(x,w,\gamma)\mapsto(x,w,\gamma,\bar{\omega}_{0})
for some arbitrary element
\bar{\omega}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. Then we introduce the indicator process
I
defined by
I_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t}
, and set
I^{0}=I\circ\iota
as well as
X^{0}=X\circ\iota
. Note that
I^{0}
and
X^{0}
are independent of the choice of
\bar{\omega}_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
, since
I
and, trivially,
X
are completely determined by the trajectory of
X
. We saw above that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau=T+1)=0
and by definition
\tau\geq 0
. From this and the continuity of
\tau
on
A
it is easy to deduce that
I
is continuous as a function
\Omega_{\ast}\to D_{[0,1]}[-1,T+1]
on the set
A
. Now for any element
\omega_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
, we define the measurable set
A_{\omega_{0}}=\{s\in\mathcal{S}\mathpunct{:}(s,\omega_{0})\in A\}
. Then,

1=\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A)=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(A|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=\int_{\Omega_{0}}\int_{\mathcal{S}}\mathbf{1}_{A}(s,\omega_{0})\,\mathrm{d}m(s)\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}(\omega_{0})=\int_{\Omega_{0}}m(A_{\omega_{0}})\,\mathrm{d}\mathbb{P}_{0}(\omega_{0}),

(4.1)

which means that
m(A_{\omega_{0}})=1
for
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-a.e.
\omega_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
. However,
I^{0}
is continuous on
A_{\omega_{0}}
, which means that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. the map
(X^{0},I^{0})
is continuous at
\mu
-a.e. element of
\mathcal{S}
. Thus, the continuous mapping theorem implies that
(X^{0},I^{0})^{\#}\mu
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous. The random variable
(X^{0},I^{0})^{\#}\mu
takes values in
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[-1,T+1]\times D_{[0,1]}[-1,T+1])
, so we may invoke Lemma
A.9
together with the continuous mapping theorem to deduce that
\nu\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous.

Step 3
: Next, let us define
\tilde{\nu}^{n}
by
\tilde{\nu}^{n}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X^{n}_{t}\in\cdot,\,\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}X^{n}_{s}>0|W^{0})
for
t\in[0,T+1]
and
\tilde{\nu}^{n}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(\xi)
if
t\in[-1,0)
. Since
\nu\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous, the continuous mapping theorem implies that along a subsequence the flow of subprobabilities
(\tilde{\nu}^{n})_{n}
converges weakly to
\nu
on
L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})
. Indeed, simply note that
\tilde{\nu}^{n}=\nu(\Theta^{n})
with
\Theta^{n}=(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0})
. Thus, if we can show that
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}d_{1}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{t},\tilde{\nu}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\to 0
in the limit as
n\to\infty
, the second statement of the lemma follows. Let us set
\tau_{n}=\inf\{0<t\leq T+1\mathpunct{:}\Lambda^{n}_{t}\geq\theta\}
and
\tilde{\tau}_{n}=\inf\{0<t\leq T+1\mathpunct{:}X^{n}_{t}\leq 0\}
as well as
I^{n}_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tau_{n}>t}
and
\tilde{I}^{n}_{t}=\mathbf{1}_{\tilde{\tau}_{n}>t}
. To establish the desired convergence, we shall use that
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau_{n}-\tilde{\tau}_{n})=0
almost surely whenever
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n}<\infty
. We will prove this fact first.

Note that by definition
\tau_{n}\geq\tilde{\tau}_{n}
, so it suffices to prove that
\limsup_{n\to\infty}(\tau_{n}-\tilde{\tau}_{n})\leq 0
. Let us fix a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
for which
(X^{n_{k}},\tilde{\tau}_{n_{k}})\Rightarrow(X,\tau)
. This is possible owing to the
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuity of
\tau
established above. Then appealing to Skorokhod’s representation theorem (and changing the probability space if necessary), we may assume that there exists a
D[-1,T+1]
-valued random variable
\tilde{X}
and a random time
\tilde{\tau}
with values in
[0,T+1]\cup\{\infty\}
, such that
(X^{n_{k}},\tilde{\tau}_{n_{k}})
converges to
(\tilde{X},\tilde{\tau})
almost surely. On the event
\{\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n}<\infty\}
, we can extract a further (random) subsequence, which for simplicity we denote again by
(n_{k})_{k}
, such that
\lim_{k\to\infty}(\tau_{n_{k}}-\tilde{\tau}_{n_{k}})=\limsup_{n\to\infty}(\tau_{n}-\tilde{\tau}_{n})
. Now by the above, with probability one either
\tilde{\tau}=\infty
or
\tilde{X}
has the downcrossing property. If
\tilde{\tau}=\infty
then
\lim_{k\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n_{k}}=\tilde{\tau}=\infty
, which contradicts
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n}<\infty
. Hence, for a.e. element of
\{\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n}<\infty\}
the process
\tilde{X}
possesses the downcrossing property. Consequently, given any
\delta>0
, we find a continuity point
t\in[\tilde{\tau},\tilde{\tau}+\delta/2]
of
\tilde{X}
with
\tilde{X}_{t}<0
. Hence, we can choose a sufficiently small
0<\epsilon\leq\delta/2
so that for all large enough
k\geq 1
we have
X^{n_{k}}_{s}\leq\tilde{X}_{t}/2<0
whenever
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
. This implies that
\lambda^{n}(s,X^{n_{k}}_{s},\nu^{n_{k}}_{s})\to\infty
for all
s\in[t,t+\epsilon]
by Assumption
2.13

(iii)
. Hence, we see

\Lambda^{n_{k}}_{\tilde{\tau}+\delta}\geq\Lambda^{n_{k}}_{t+\epsilon}\geq\Lambda^{n_{k}}_{t+\epsilon}-\Lambda^{n_{k}}_{t}=\int_{t}^{t+\epsilon}\lambda^{n_{k}}(s,X^{n_{k}}_{s},\nu^{n_{k}}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s\to\infty

as
n\to\infty
. But this means
\limsup_{k\to\infty}\tau_{n_{k}}\leq\tilde{\tau}+\delta
. Upon letting
\delta
to zero, we find that

\limsup_{n\to\infty}(\tau_{n}-\tilde{\tau}_{n})=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\tau_{n_{k}}-\tilde{\tau}_{n_{k}}=\limsup_{k\to\infty}\tau_{n_{k}}-\tilde{\tau}\leq 0

as required.

In summary, we have
\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tau_{n}=\limsup_{n\to\infty}\tilde{\tau}_{n}=\infty
or
\lim_{n\to\infty}(\tau_{n}-\tilde{\tau}_{n})=0
almost surely, which means a.s.
\tau_{n}\land T_{1}-\tilde{\tau}_{n}\land T_{1}\to 0
. Finally, this gives

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T_{1}}d_{1}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{t},\tilde{\nu}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\int_{0}^{T_{1}}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(1+\lvert X^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2})\lvert I^{n}_{t}-\tilde{I}^{n}_{t}\rvert\bigr]\,\mathrm{d}t

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bigl(1+(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T_{1}})^{2}\bigr)(\tau_{n}\land T_{1}-\tilde{\tau}_{n}\land T_{1})\bigr].

In view of Vitali’s convergence theorem, the expression on the right-hand side vanishes as we let
n\to\infty
, since the sequence
((\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2})_{n}
is uniformly integrable. This concludes the proof.
∎

From Proposition
4.2
we can immediately deduce that
(L^{n})_{n}
converges weakly to
L
on
L^{2}([-1,T+1])
along a subsequence, say
(n_{k})_{k}
. Here
L
is the process defined in the paragraph above Remark
2.10
. However, as in the proof of Proposition
4.1
we can show that the sequence
(L^{n})_{n}
is tight on
D[-1,T+1]
, where we set
L^{n}_{t}=0
for
t\in[-1,0)
and
L^{n}_{t}=L^{n}_{T}
for
t\in(T,T+1]
. Thus
L^{n}
converges weakly on
D[-1,T+1]
along a further subsequence. But since elements of
D[-1,T+1]
coincide if they are equal as elements of
L^{2}([0,T])
, the subsequential weak limit (along the subsequence of
(n_{k})_{k}
) of
(L^{n})_{n}
on
D[-1,T+1]
must be
L
. Since the limit of
(L^{n})_{n}
along all such further subsequences of
(n_{k})_{k}
coincide, we can conclude that
L^{n}
converges weakly to
L
on
D[-1,T+1]
along the original subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
.

Next we wish to identify the limit of the integrals
(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{t})_{n}
, which are tight on
D[-1,T+1]
according to the proof of Proposition
4.1
. Let
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T+1]
denote the set of nondecreasing càdlàg functions
\ell\mathpunct{:}[-1,T+1]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\ell_{t}=0
for
t\in[-1,0)
. This is a closed subset of
D[-1,T+1]
and hence Polish. We define the map
I^{\alpha}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T+1]\to D[-1,T+1]
by
I^{\alpha}_{t}(\ell)=\int_{0}^{t\lor 0}\alpha(s)\,\mathrm{d}\ell_{s}
for
t\in[-1,T+1]
, so that
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{t}=I^{\alpha}(L^{n})
.

Corollary 4.3
.

Under the assumptions of Proposition
4.2
, for any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
, the processes
(L^{n})_{n}
and
(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{t})_{n}
converge weakly to
L
and
I^{\alpha}(L)
, respectively, on
D[-1,T+1]
along the given subsequence.

Note that
I^{\alpha}(L)
is well-defined since
L
is a nondecreasing càdlàg process with
L_{t}=0
on
[-1,0)
by construction.

Proof of Corollary
4.3
.

Assume that the weak convergence of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
holds along the subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
. We discussed the weak convergence of
(L^{n})_{n}
along
(n_{k})_{k}
to
L
on
D[-1,T+1]
above the statement of the corollary, so let us turn to the convergence of
(\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{t})_{n}
. Since both
L^{n}
and
L
are nondecreasing, the weak convergence actually holds on
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T+1]
. By Assumption
2.13

(i)
the coeffcient
\alpha
is continuous. Hence, we can apply Lemma
A.10
whereby the map
I^{\alpha}
is
\mathcal{L}_{\ast}(L)
-a.s. continuous on
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T+1]
, so that by the continuous mapping theorem we have
\int_{0}^{\cdot}\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n_{k}}_{t}=I^{\alpha}(L^{n_{k}})\Rightarrow I^{\alpha}(L)
on
D[-1,T+1]
.
∎

4.2
The Controlled Martingale Problem for the Singular Model

In this subsection we use a slight variation of the martingale problem from Subsection
3.3
to show that
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
converges subsequentially to an admissible relaxed control rule. Since the loss process
L_{t}=1-\nu_{t}(\mathbb{R})
might jump with positive probability under the limiting probabilities, we remove it from the state
X
to obtain a continuous process. More precisely, we introduce the differential operator
\mathcal{L}
given by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}

\displaystyle\varphi(t,x,y,z,v,g)

\displaystyle=b(t,x,v,g)\partial_{x}\varphi(x,y,z)+a(t,x,v)\partial_{x}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{y}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\frac{1}{2}\partial_{z}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\sigma(t,x,v)\partial_{xy}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)+\sigma_{0}(t,x,v)\partial_{xz}^{2}\varphi(x,y,z)

for any twice continuously differentiable function
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{3}\to\mathbb{R}
and
(t,x,y,z,v,g)\in[0,T+1]\times\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times G
, where
a(t,x,v)=\frac{1}{2}(\sigma^{2}(t,x,v)+\sigma_{0}^{2}(t,x,v))
. Now, for any
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
we define the process
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
on
\Omega_{\ast}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\omega)&=\varphi\bigl(x_{t}-I^{\alpha}_{t}(L(\omega)),w_{t},b_{t}\bigr)\\
&\ \ \ -\int_{[0,t]\times G}\mathcal{L}\varphi\bigl(s,x_{s}-I^{\alpha}_{s}(L(\omega)),w_{s},b_{s},\nu_{s}(\omega)_{s},g\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)\end{split}

(4.2)

for
t\in[0,T+1]
and
\omega=(x,w,\mathfrak{g},m,b)\in\Omega_{\ast}
. Here
\mathfrak{g}
is extended to
(t,g)\in(T,T+1]\times G
by
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{g_{0}}(g)\mathrm{d}t
for some arbitrary
g_{0}\in G
. We prove that
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is a martingale under any probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
induced by a subsequential limit of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
, which – as we demonstrate in the proof of Proposition
4.4
– implies that
Y=X-I^{\alpha}(L)\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to D[-1,T+1]
satisfies

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}Y_{t}&=\int_{G}b(t,Y_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L),\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)+\sigma(t,Y_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L),\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}\\
&\ \ \ +\sigma_{0}(t,Y_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L),\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}\end{split}

(4.3)

under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
. Note, however, that the martingale problem under consideration is not associated to the SDE

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}Y^{n}_{t}&=b(t,Y^{n}_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L^{n}),\nu^{n}_{t},\gamma^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma(t,Y^{n}_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L^{n}),\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}\\
&\ \ \ +\sigma_{0}(t,Y^{n}_{t}+I^{\alpha}_{t}(L^{n}),\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}\end{split}

(4.4)

satisfied by the process
Y^{n}=X^{n}-I^{\alpha}(L^{n})
. Indeed, the processes

L_{t}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0})=\mathbb{P}\biggl(\inf_{0\leq s\leq t}X^{n}_{s}<0|W^{0}\biggr)

and
L^{n}_{t}=\mathbb{P}(\Lambda^{n}_{t}\leq\theta|W^{0})
are distinct, which leads us to introduce the process
\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}
on
\Omega
defined as

\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{t}=\varphi(Y^{n}_{t},W_{t},W^{0}_{t})-\int_{0}^{t}\mathcal{L}\varphi(s,Y^{n}_{s},W_{s},W^{0}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s},\gamma^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s

for
t\in[0,T+1]
and
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
. Now,
\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}
is a martingale for all
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
if and only if
Y^{n}
solves SDE (
4.4
).

Proposition 4.4
.

Let Assumption
2.13
be satisfied and suppose that
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Then, for any
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R}^{3})
, the process
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is an
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
-martingale under any probability measure
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
induced by a subsequential limit of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
. In particular, the limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
is an admissible relaxed control rule in the sense of Definition
2.12
.

Proof.

Recall the decomposition
X=X_{0-}+\beta+Z+F
from the proof of Proposition
4.2
. Since
F
is the subsequential weak limit of the sequence
(I^{\alpha}(L^{n}))_{n}
, we can use Corollary
4.3
to identify
F
as
I^{\alpha}(L)
. Consequently,
Y=X_{0-}+\beta+Z
has
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous trajectories, so the same is true for
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
. Moreover, we deduce that along a subsequence

(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0},Y^{n},\nu^{n},I^{\alpha}(L^{n}))\Rightarrow(X,W,\Gamma,\mu,B,Y,\nu,I^{\alpha}(L))

on
\Omega_{\ast}\times C([-1,T+1])\times L^{2}([-1,T+1];\mathbf{M})\times D[-1,T+1]
. (Here with slight abuse of notation we use the same symbol
W
for the idiosyncratic noise on
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
and
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
, which are generally distinct.) Hence, with the choices
E=\mathbb{R}^{3}\times\mathbf{M}
and
\Phi=\mathcal{L}\varphi
in Lemma
A.7
, we can deduce that for any bounded and measurable
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
, which is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous, and all
s
,
t\in[0,T+1]
, we have

\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{t}-\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{s})\Phi(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0})\bigr]\to\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi(\Theta)\bigr]

(4.5)

along a suitable subsequence. Here we used the linear growth of
\Omega_{\ast}\ni\omega\mapsto\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{u}(\omega)
for any
u\in[0,T+1]
and the boundedness of
\Phi
together with
\sup_{n\geq 1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\sup_{0\leq u\leq T}\bigl(\lvert X^{n}_{u}\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\nu^{n}_{u})\bigr)+M_{2}^{2}(\Gamma^{n})\bigr]<\infty
to upgrade weak convergence to convergence in mean. We shall use the result (
4.5
) to establish the martingale property of
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
.

Similarly to the proof of Proposition
3.10
, for any
\Phi\in C_{b}(\Omega_{\ast})
and
t\in[0,T+1]
let us define the map
\Phi_{t}\mathpunct{:}\Omega_{\ast}\to\mathbb{R}
by

\Phi_{t}(x,w,\mathfrak{g},m,b)=\Phi(x_{t\land\cdot},w_{t\land\cdot},\mathfrak{g}_{t},\pi_{t}^{\#}m,b_{t\land\cdot}),

where
\pi_{t}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{S}\to\mathcal{S}
is given by
(x,w,\mathfrak{g})\mapsto(x_{t\land\cdot},w,\mathfrak{g}_{t})
. The
\sigma
-algebra
\mathcal{F}^{\ast}_{t}
is generated by the random variables
\Phi_{t}(\Theta)
for
\Phi\in C_{b}(\Omega_{\ast})
. Hence, if we can prove that

\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\Theta)\bigr]=0

(4.6)

for all
\Phi\in C_{b}(\Omega_{\ast})
and all
s\leq t
in some dense set of times
\mathbb{T}\subset[0,T+1]
which includes
T+1
, then it clearly follows
that
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
. Let us choose
\mathbb{T}
to be the set of times
t\in[0,T+1]
for which
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\Delta X_{t}=0)=1
, which we know to be cocountable and, hence, dense in
[0,T+1]
and inclusive of
T+1
. Now, we have to verify Equation (
4.6
) for all
s\leq t
in
\mathbb{T}
. Note that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{t}-\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{s})\Phi_{s}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0})\bigr]=0
because
\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}
is a martingale. Further, we claim that
\Phi_{s}
is
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous for any
s\in\mathbb{T}
. Then, in view of (
4.5
) we get the desired equality

\displaystyle 0

\displaystyle=\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\bigl[(\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{t}-\mathcal{M}^{n,\varphi}_{s})\Phi_{s}(X^{n},W,\Gamma^{n},\mu^{n},W^{0})\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\bigl[\bigl(\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{t}(\Theta)-\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}_{s}(\Theta)\bigr)\Phi_{s}(\Theta)\bigr].

Let us prove the claim. Clearly, it is enough to establish continuity of the maps
\omega\mapsto x_{s\land\cdot}
and
\omega\mapsto\pi_{s}^{\#}m
at
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.e.
\omega=(x,w,\mathfrak{g},m,b)\in\Omega_{\ast}
. For the first map
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-almost sure continuity follows from our choice of
s
as a
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-almost sure continuity point of
X
. To show the desired continuity for
\omega\mapsto\pi_{s}^{\#}m
, let us choose a Borel set
A\subset\Omega_{\ast}
of full measure on which the trajectories of
X
are continuous at
s
. Then for any
\omega_{0}\in\Omega_{0}
, we set
A_{\omega_{0}}=\{u\in\mathcal{S}\mathpunct{:}(u,\omega_{0})\in A\}
and just as in Equation (
4.1
) find that
m(A_{\omega_{0}})=1
for
\mathbb{P}_{0}
-a.e.
\omega_{0}=(m,b)\in\Omega_{0}
and, thus, at
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.e.
\omega=(u,\omega_{0})
of
\Omega_{\ast}
. However, the map
\pi_{s}
is continuous on
A_{\omega_{0}}
by our choice of
A
, so that
\omega\mapsto\pi_{s}^{\#}m
is
continuous at
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.e.
\omega\in\Omega_{\ast}
by the continuous mapping theorem. This proves the claim. Consequently,
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
is a martingale under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
.

As we mentioned above the martingale
\mathcal{M}^{\varphi}
has
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
-a.s. continuous trajectories, so by Theorem II.7.2 from
[
24
]
it follows that
Y
solves SDE (
4.3
). This implies that
X
is a solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.14
) under
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
. From there we proceed as at the end of the proof of Proposition
3.10
to show that
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
is a relaxed control rule.
∎

4.3
Proof of Theorem
2.14

Proof of Theorem
2.14
.

By Lemma
4.1
the sequence
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
is tight and Proposition
4.4
states that any subsequential limit
\mathbb{P}_{0}
of
(\mu^{n},W^{0})_{n}
is an admissible relaxed control rule. Thus it remains to prove that the costs
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})=\mathbb{E}[\int_{0}^{\tau^{n}\land T}f(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t},\gamma^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t,\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t]
converge subsequentially to
J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
. However, this is a simple application of Lemma
A.7
along the lines of the proof of Proposition
3.11
.
∎

4.4
The Case of Time-Dependent Coefficients

We now consider the situation where
\lambda_{n}
is just a function of
x
, the coefficients
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
only depend on time, and
b(t,x,v,g)=b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)g
for measurable functions
b_{0}
,
b_{1}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
. We can immediately proceed to the proof of Proposition
2.17
.

Proof of Proposition
2.17
.

We first show that there exists a sequence of controls admissible controls
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
for the regularised model with
\limsup_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq V_{0}^{\text{sg}}
, where
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})
is defined in Equation (
2.17
). Fix
\epsilon>0
and let
\mathbb{P}_{0}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
be a relaxed control rule with
J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\leq V_{0}^{\text{sg}}+\epsilon
. By Proposition
3.12
, on the probability space
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
, we can uniquely solve the McKean–Vlasov SDE
