\mathrm{d}X^{n}_{t}=\int_{G}(b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)g)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Gamma}(t,g)+\sigma(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}-\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n}_{t},\quad X^{n}_{0}=X_{0-},

(4.7)

with
L^{n}_{t}=1-\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[e^{-\Lambda^{n}_{t}}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}]
,
\Lambda^{n}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{n}(X^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
, and admissible relaxed control
\tilde{\Gamma}
defined by
\int_{[0,T]\times G}\varphi(t,g)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Gamma}(t,g)=\int_{[0,T]\times G}\varphi(t,\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t}g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)
for
\varphi\in C_{b}([0,T]\times G)
. It is important to add the indicator
\mathbf{1}_{\tau>t}
, where we recall
\tau=\inf\{0<t\leq T+1\mathpunct{:}X_{t}\leq 0\}
, to the control
\Gamma
to ensure that
X^{n}
does not have larger running cost than
X
.
We claim that
X^{n}_{t}\geq X_{t}
for all
t\in[0,\tau]
, which by Assumption
2.16
implies that

J_{0}^{n}:=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times G}e^{-\Lambda^{n}_{t}}f(t,X^{n}_{t},\nu^{n}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Gamma}(t,g)+\int_{0}^{T}\psi(t,\nu^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr]\leq J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})\leq V_{0}^{\text{sg}}+\epsilon,

(4.8)

where
\nu^{n}
is defined by
\langle\nu^{n}_{t},\varphi\rangle=\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[e^{-\Lambda^{n}_{t}}\varphi(X^{n}_{t})|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}]
for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and
t\in[0,T]
. We prove the claim through an approximation argument.

We set
L^{n,0}_{t}=0
and then for
m\geq 1
define

\mathrm{d}X^{n,m}_{t}=\int_{G}(b_{0}(t)+b_{1}(t)g)\,\mathrm{d}\tilde{\Gamma}(t,g)+\sigma(t)\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t)\,\mathrm{d}B_{t}-\alpha(t)\,\mathrm{d}L^{n,m-1}_{t}

(4.9)

with
X^{n,m}_{0}=X_{0-}
, and set
L^{n,m}_{t}=1-\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[e^{-\Lambda^{n,m}_{t}}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}]
, where
\Lambda^{n,m}_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda_{n}(X^{n,m}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
. It is not difficult to see that
L^{n,m}_{t}\leq L_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
. This is certainly true for
m=0
. Then, for the purpose of induction let us assume the statement holds for some
m\geq 0
. It follows that
X^{n,m}_{t}\geq X_{t}
on
[0,\tau]
, but
\tau
is the first time that
X
visits
(-\infty,0]
, so that the first hitting time
\tilde{\tau}_{n,m}
of
X^{n,m}_{t}
on
(-\infty,0]
must be at least as large as
\tau
. Hence, we see that

\displaystyle L^{n,m}_{t}=1-\mathbb{E}_{\ast}[e^{-\Lambda^{n,m}_{t}}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T}]\leq\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tilde{\tau}_{n,m}\leq t|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})\leq\mathbb{P}_{\ast}(\tau\leq t|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})=L_{t},

which concludes the induction. From
L^{n,m}_{t}\leq L_{t}
we obtain that
X^{n,m}_{t}\geq X_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T]
and
m\geq 1
. Now we simply take the limit as
m\to\infty
. Since
(L^{n,m})_{m}
is an increasing sequence, it converges and its limit coincides with
L^{n}
. Similarly, the processes
(X^{n,m})_{m}
form a decreasing sequence with limit
X^{n}
, which implies the desired inequality
X^{n}_{t}\geq X_{t}
for
0\leq t\leq T
and proves the claim.

Next we invoke Theorem
2.8
, whereby the minimal cost
V_{n}
achievable for McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.16
) with intensity function
\lambda^{n}
over admissible strong controls (i.e. those which are square-integrable and
\mathbb{F}^{\xi,W,W^{0}}
-progressively measurable) and over admissible relaxed control in the sense of Definition
2.7
coincides. Consequently, for any sequence
(\epsilon_{n})_{n}
of positive numbers tending to zero, we can find a sequence of admissible strong controls
(\gamma^{n})_{n}
, such that if
\tilde{X}^{n}
denotes the solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.16
) with control
\gamma^{n}
, then

J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq V_{n}+\epsilon_{n}\leq J_{0}^{n}+\epsilon_{n}\leq V^{\text{sg}}_{0}+\epsilon+\epsilon_{n},

(4.10)

where
J_{n}(\gamma^{n})
is defined in Equation (
2.17
) and the last inequality is simply Equation (
4.8
). Taking the limit superior as
n\to\infty
implies
\limsup_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq V^{\text{sg}}_{0}+\epsilon
.

Let us next deduce the lower bound
V^{\text{sg}}_{0}\leq\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})
. It follows from the coercivity of
f
(see Assumption
2.2

(vi)
which holds true by Assumption
2.13
), and the easily obtained boundedness of
V_{n}
in
n\geq 1
that the family
(\int_{0}^{T}\lvert\gamma^{n}_{t}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t)_{n}
is uniformly integrable. Take a subsequence
(n_{k})_{k}
which realises the limit inferior of the sequence
(J_{n}(\gamma^{n}))_{n}
and for which
(\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}^{n_{k}},W,\Gamma^{n_{k}}|W^{0}),W^{0})_{k}
converges to some relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\ast}\in\mathcal{P}(\Omega_{0})
(for the singular model), where
\Gamma^{n}
is the relaxed control associated to
\gamma^{n}
. This can be achieved according to Theorem
2.14
, which further states that the costs
J_{n_{k}}(\gamma^{n_{k}})
converge to the cost
J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\ast})
of the limiting relaxed control rule. Consequently, we have

V_{0}^{\text{sg}}\leq J^{\text{sg}}_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0}^{\ast})\leq\lim_{k\to\infty}J_{n_{k}}(\gamma^{n_{k}})=\liminf_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq\limsup_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})\leq V_{0}^{\text{sg}}+\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
we obtain
\lim_{n\to\infty}V_{n}=\lim_{n\to\infty}J_{n}(\gamma^{n})=V_{0}^{\text{sg}}
, which concludes the proof.
∎

Finally, we assume that
b_{1}=0
, so we are considering a problem without controls.

Proof of Proposition
2.19
.

Let
L
be the process constructed above Proposition
2.19
and define
X
through Equation (
2.19
). Our first goal is to verify that for every time
t\in[-1,T+1]
the sequence
(L^{n}_{t})_{n}
converges to
L_{t}
on the set
\{\Delta L_{t}=0\}
. Note that we may assume that
t\in[0,T+1)
, since the convergence holds for
t\in[-1,0)
and
t=T+1
by definition. We split the convergence into two inequalities:
\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\leq L_{t}
and
\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\geq L_{t}
. The first one holds even outside of
\{\Delta L_{t}=0\}
. Indeed, let
\epsilon>0
and fix
s\in(t,T+1]\cap\mathbb{Q}
with
\ell_{s}-L_{t}\leq\epsilon
, which is possible by construction of
L
. Then by monotonicity of
L^{n}
we have

\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\leq\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{s}=\ell_{s}\leq L_{t}+\epsilon.

Letting
\epsilon\to 0
gives
\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\leq L_{t}
. Next, assuming that
\Delta L_{t}=0
, for any
\epsilon>0
we can find
s\in[-1,t)\cap\mathbb{Q}
with
L_{t}-\ell_{s}\leq\epsilon
. Then

\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\geq\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{s}=\ell_{s}\geq L_{t}-\epsilon.

Now we let
\epsilon\to 0
again to see that
\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}\leq L_{t}
and thus
\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}=L_{t}
. Since the jumps in
X
are caused by
L
, we can transfer the convergence of
(L^{n})_{n}
to
(X^{n})_{n}
, so altogether we have
(X^{n}_{t},L^{n}_{t})\to(X_{t},L_{t})
on
\{\Delta L_{t}=0\}
.

By
[
43
, Theorem 12.5.1 (iv)]
a sequence of nondecreasing maps
(x^{n})_{n}
in
D[-1,T+1]
converges to
x\in D[-1,T+1]
if
x^{n}_{t}\to x_{t}
for a dense set of times
t\in[-1,T+1]
including
-1
and
T+1
. This is a.s. the case for
(L^{n})_{n}
, so that
L^{n}\to L
a.s. in
D[-1,T+1]
. Since
X^{n}
is the sum of a continuous process (that does not change with
n
) and
L^{n}
, we deduce that
X^{n}\to X
a.s. in
D[-1,T+1]
as well.

It remains to show that
(X,L)
is minimal. For that, we use the approximation scheme from (
4.9
). Let
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
be any other solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
). Then defining
(X^{n,m},L^{n,m})
as in (
4.9
) we can inductively show that
L^{n,m}_{t}\leq L^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
. Since
L^{n,m}_{t}\to L^{n}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
we have that

L_{t}=\lim_{n\to\infty}L^{n}_{t}=\lim_{n\to\infty}\lim_{m\to\infty}L^{n,m}_{t}\leq L^{\prime}_{t}

on
\{\Delta L_{t}=0\}
. From this we can conclude that
L_{t}\leq L^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
. Since
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
was arbitrary it follows that
(X,L)
is minimal.
∎

Remark 4.5
.

The minimality of
(X,L)
does not only hold among the class of strong solutions
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
for which
L^{\prime}
is
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
-adapted. Indeed, if we assume that
\mathbb{G}
is another filtration that satisfies the conditions outlined at the beginning of Subsection
3.4
and that
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is a solution of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
) with
L^{\prime}=\mathbb{P}(\tau^{\prime}\leq t|\mathcal{G}_{T})
, where
\tau^{\prime}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}X^{\prime}_{t}\leq 0\}
, then the arguments from the proof of Proposition
2.19
above still apply. Hence, we can conclude that
L_{t}\leq L^{\prime}_{t}
for all
t\in[0,T+1]
.

In fact, since
(X,L)
is a strong solution, we can transfer it to any other probability space that can accommodate random variables with the law
\mathcal{L}(\xi,W,W^{0})
. We simply use that
(X,L)=S(\xi,W,W^{0})
for a measurable function
S\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\times C([0,T+1])\times C([0,T+1])\to D[0,T+1]\times D[0,T+1]
. So if
(X^{\prime},L^{\prime})
is a weak solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
) (in the above sense that
L^{\prime}
is not necessarily adapted to the filtration of the common noise but a possibly larger filtration satisfying the conditions outlined at the beginning of Subsection
3.4
) on any given probability space equipped with random variables
\xi^{\prime}
,
W^{\prime}
, and
B^{\prime}
such that
(\xi^{\prime},W^{\prime},B^{\prime})\sim(\xi,W,W^{0})
, then
S(\xi^{\prime},W^{\prime},B^{\prime})
is a strong solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.19
) and
L^{\prime}
will be lower bounded by the second component of
S(\xi^{\prime},W^{\prime},B^{\prime})
.

5
Numerical Simulation

In this section, we discuss a scheme for simulating the regularised mean-field control problem (
2.5
). We use a policy gradient method to search for optimal controls. That is, we approximate the dynamics (
2.5
) for a parametrised control
\gamma
, compute the associated cost
J(\gamma)
, and then update the parameters of
\gamma
based on the gradient of
J(\gamma)
with respect to the parameters.

5.1
Finite Element Scheme for the Stochastic Fokker–Planck Equation

It is extremely costly to use a particle system to simulate (
2.5
), as this requires a two-fold approximation: for each realisation of the common noise we need to estimate
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
through a Monte-Carlo approximation based on
K\geq 1
samples of the idiosyncratic noise
W
. Then, we approximate the cost
J(\gamma)
by averaging over these estimates for
K_{0}\geq 1
realisations of the common noise
W^{0}
. The resulting Monte-Carlo error is at best of order
(K_{0}\land K)^{-1/2}
while the computational complexity is
O(K_{0}K)
. This is too expensive, so instead we proceed via the stochastic Fokker–Planck equation satisfied by the flow
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
. To pursue this approach we have to assume that the control
\gamma
is in
feedback form
, i.e. there exists a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to G
with
\gamma_{t}=g(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})
for
t\in[0,T]
. We call such a control
closed-loop
.

Remark 5.1
.

Since we do not allow
g
to depend on the cumulative intensity
\Lambda_{t}
, it is by no means clear that the infimum of
J
over such closed-loop controls equals
V
(the value of the open-loop formulation defined below Equation (
2.6
)). If we did allow
g
to depend on
\Lambda_{t}
(and
\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
instead of
\nu_{t}
), then under an additional convexity assumption the equivalence between both formulations follows from
[
31
, Theorem 8.3]
, see Subsection
A.2
of the appendix for details. However, having
\Lambda_{t}
as an input to
g
means that the flow
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
does not satisfy an autonomous stochastic Fokker–Planck equation and instead we have to solve the two-dimensional stochastic Fokker–Planck equation for the conditional joint law
\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
. This is undesirable from a computational point of view. Moreover, the dependence on
\Lambda_{t}
simply does not seem necessary. Indeed, since
\theta
possesses the memoryless property, we compute for
t\leq s
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\theta>\Lambda_{s}|W^{0},\,\theta>\Lambda_{t})=\mathbb{P}\bigl(\theta>(\Lambda_{s}-\Lambda_{t})+\Lambda_{t}|W^{0},\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}\bigr)=\mathbb{P}(\theta>\Lambda_{s}-\Lambda_{t}|W^{0}),

which suggests that future killings do not depend on the current state of
\Lambda_{t}
but only on the increment
\Lambda_{s}-\Lambda_{t}
. Consequently, it seems unnecessary to keep track of
\Lambda_{t}
in the control
g
. Nonetheless, this intuition is not easily translated into a proof of the equivalence between the open- and closed-loop formulation and addressing the issue would go beyond the scope of this paper.

To ensure that McKean–Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) is well-posed for the closed-loop control
g
, we assume that
g
satisfies the assumptions imposed on
\lambda
in Assumptions
2.2

(i)
and
(ii)
. We call such
g

admissible closed-loop controls
and denote the collection of such
g
by
\mathbb{G}_{\text{cl}}
. The
cost functional
for closed-loop controls
g\in\mathbb{G}_{\text{cl}}
is defined by

\displaystyle\begin{split}J_{\text{cl}}(g)&=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}e^{-\Lambda_{t}}f\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr]\\
&=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}\langle\nu_{t},f\bigl(t,\cdot,\nu_{t},g(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr].\end{split}

(5.1)

As mentioned above, the flow
\nu
satisfies a stochastic Fokker–Planck equation. We can derive this SPDE by applying Itô’s formula to the process
e^{-\Lambda}\varphi(X)
for a test function
\varphi\in C_{c}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
and then taking conditional expectations with respect to
W^{0}
. This gives

\mathrm{d}\langle\nu_{t},\varphi\rangle=\langle\nu_{t},\mathcal{L}_{g}\varphi(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t+\langle\nu_{t},\sigma_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\partial_{x}\varphi\rangle\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}

(5.2)

with initial condition
\nu_{0}=\mathcal{L}(\xi)
. Here for
g\in\mathbb{G}_{\text{cl}}
, the generator
\mathcal{L}_{g}
acts on
\varphi\in C^{2}_{c}(\mathbb{R})
by

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{g}\varphi(t,x,v)

\displaystyle=\Bigl(b\bigl(t,x,v,g(t,x,v)\bigr)-\alpha(t,x,v)\langle v,\lambda(t,\cdot,v)\rangle\Bigr)\partial_{x}\varphi(x)

\displaystyle\ \ \ +a(t,x,v)\partial^{2}_{x}\varphi(x)-\lambda(t,x,v)\varphi(x)

for
(t,x,v)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})
, and
a
is given by
a(t,x,v)=\frac{1}{2}\sigma^{2}(t,x,v)+\frac{1}{2}\sigma_{0}^{2}(t,x,v)
. We refer to
[
23
, Theorem 1.9]
and its proof for details regarding the derivation of SPDE (
5.2
).

Remark 5.2
.

Under Assumption
2.2
it is possible to show that the conditional subprobability
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
is the unique strong solution to SPDE (
5.2
), where uniqueness is understood in a pathwise sense. This can be achieved through arguments similar to those in
[
9
]
.

We will next propose a discretisation scheme for SPDE (
5.2
). Our discussion will be informal and we do not justify the method theoretically. We discretise SPDE (
5.2
) in space by a finite element scheme with hat basis and in time by a semi-implicit Euler-Maruyama scheme. More precisely, fix
x_{0}<0<x_{n+1}
and let
x_{0}<x_{1}<\dots x_{n+1}
be an equidistant grid on
[x_{0},x_{n+1}]
with mesh size
h=x_{1}-x_{0}
. For
1\leq i\leq n
we define the hat functions
v_{i}
by
v_{i}(x)=0\lor(1-h^{-1}\lvert x-x_{i}\rvert)
. Next, let
0=t_{0}<t_{1}<\dots<t_{m}=T
be a uniform time grid with mesh size
t_{1}
. We define the map
A\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\times\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{R};G)\to\mathbb{R}^{n\times n}
by

\displaystyle\begin{split}A_{ij}(t,\nu,u)&=\int_{\mathbb{R}}\Bigl(b(t,x,\nu,u(x))-\alpha(t,x,\nu)\langle\nu,\lambda(t,\cdot,\nu)\rangle\Bigr)v_{j}(x)\partial_{x}v_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\\
&\ \ \ -\int_{\mathbb{R}}\partial_{x}(a(t,x,\nu)v_{j}(x))\partial_{x}v_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x-\int_{\mathbb{R}}\lambda(t,x,\nu)v_{j}(x)v_{i}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x\end{split}

(5.3)

for
1\leq i,j\leq n
. Here
\mathcal{B}_{1}(\mathbb{R};G)
denotes the space of measurable functions
\mathbb{R}\to G
of at most linear growth. In practice the integrals in (
5.3
) have to be approximated via some numerical integration method.

Next, we consider the SDE

\mathrm{d}\rho^{ni}_{t}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}(t,\nu^{n}_{t},g(t,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t}))\rho^{nj}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}t+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl\langle v_{j},\sigma_{0}(t,\cdot,\nu^{n}_{t})\partial_{x}v_{i}\bigr\rangle\rho^{nj}_{t}\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}

(5.4)

with initial condition
\rho^{ni}_{0}=\langle\nu_{0},v_{i}\rangle
. For a standard finite element scheme we would define
\nu^{n}_{t}
as the measure on
\mathbb{R}
whose density
\rho^{n}_{t}
is given as the unique element in the linear span of
(v_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with
\langle\rho^{n}_{t},v_{i}\rangle=\rho^{ni}_{t}
for
1\leq i\leq n
. However, there is no guarantee that
\rho^{ni}_{t}\geq 0
nor that
r^{n}_{t}=\sum_{i=1}^{n}(\rho^{ni}_{t})_{+}\leq 1
, so this naive definition of
\nu^{n}_{t}
would not yield a subprobability measure. Instead, we define
\rho^{n}_{t}
as the unique element in the linear span of
(v_{i})_{1\leq i\leq n}
with
\langle\rho^{n}_{t},v_{i}\rangle=(\rho^{ni}_{t})_{+}/(r^{n}_{t}\lor 1)
and define
\nu^{n}_{t}
by
\mathrm{d}\nu^{n}_{t}(x)=\rho^{n}_{t}(x)\,\mathrm{d}x
.

We discretise SDE (
5.4
) in time with a semi-implicit Euler-Maruyama scheme: we set
p^{ni}_{0}=\rho^{ni}_{0}
and for
k=0
, …,
m-1
let
p^{ni}_{k+1}
,
i=1
, …,
n
, be the solution to

\displaystyle\begin{split}p^{ni}_{k+1}+t_{1}\sum_{j=1}^{n}A_{ij}&\bigl(t_{k},\nu^{nm}_{k},g(t_{k},\cdot,\nu^{nm}_{k})\bigr)p^{nj}_{k+1}\\
&=p^{ni}_{k}+\sum_{j=1}^{n}\bigl\langle v_{j},\sigma_{0}(t_{k},\cdot,\nu^{nm}_{k})v_{i}\bigr\rangle p^{nj}_{k}(W^{0}_{t_{k+1}}-W^{0}_{t_{k}}).\end{split}

(5.5)

As above the measure
\nu^{nm}_{k}
has density
p^{n}_{k}
which is defined as the unique element in the linear span of
(v_{j})_{1\leq j\leq n}
with
\langle p^{n}_{k},v_{j}\rangle=(p^{nj}_{k})_{+}/(r^{nm}_{k}\lor 1)
,
where
r^{nm}_{k}=\sum_{j=1}^{n}(p^{nj}_{k})_{+}
. With this discretisation we can approximate the cost
J_{\text{cl}}(g)
by

\mathbb{E}\biggl[\sum_{k=0}^{m-1}\int_{\mathbb{R}}f\bigl(t_{k},x,\nu^{nm}_{k},g(t_{k},x,\nu^{nm}_{k})\bigr)\mathrm{d}\nu^{nm}_{k}(x)+\psi(\nu^{nm}_{m})\biggr].

(5.6)

We can estimate this expectation using the Monte-Carlo method with
K
independent samples of the common noise
W^{0}
.

Our discretisation scheme involves a threefold approximation: in time, space, and the randomness of the common noise. For each of the
K
realisations of the common noise and at each of the
m
time steps we have to solve the linear system (
5.5
). Since the matrix
A\bigl(t_{k},\nu^{nm}_{k},g(t_{k},\cdot,\nu^{nm}_{k})\bigr)
is tridiagonal this can be achieved with a cost of
O(n)
. Assuming that the complexity of computing the entries of
A\bigl(t_{k},\nu^{nm}_{k},g(t_{k},\cdot,\nu^{nm}_{k})\bigr)
is of order
n
, we therefore get a total cost of
O(nmK)
.

5.2
Policy Gradient Method for the Discretisation of the Stochastic Fokker–Planck Equation

To make use of the gradient descent algorithm to search for optimal closed-loop controls, we require an (approximate) parametrisation of the space
\mathbb{G}_{\text{cl}}
. An application of the Stone-Weierstrass theorem implies that functions
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{M}^{2}_{\leq 1}(\mathbb{R})\to G
of the form
(t,x,v)\mapsto g_{1}(t,x,\langle v,g_{0}\rangle)
for Lipschitz continuous maps
g_{0}\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}
and
g_{1}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
with
d_{0}\geq 1
are locally dense in
\mathbb{G}_{\text{cl}}
with respect to the supremum metric. For a given
d_{0}\geq 1
we can approximate the functions
g_{0}
and
g_{1}
by neural networks
g^{\vartheta}_{0}\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}
and
g^{\vartheta}_{1}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{0}}\to\mathbb{R}
with parameters
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\vartheta}}
. Let us fix such a
d_{0}\geq 1
as well as neural networks
g^{\vartheta}_{0}
and
g^{\vartheta}_{1}
and set
g^{\vartheta}(t,x,v)=g^{\vartheta}_{1}(t,x,\langle v,g^{\vartheta}_{0}\rangle)
. Then we can compute the finite element approximation of
\nu
for the control
g=g^{\vartheta}
for
K
realisations of the common noise. We denote the resulting Monte-Carlo estimate of the discretised cost by
\hat{J}_{\text{cl}}(\vartheta)
. For a given learning rate
\eta>0
, we can iteratively update the parameters
\vartheta
according to
\vartheta\mapsto\vartheta-\eta\nabla_{\vartheta}\hat{J}_{\text{cl}}(\vartheta)
. We terminate this scheme after a predetermined number of iterations.

5.3
Numerical Experiments

For our numerical experiments we assume that the coefficients
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
, and
\alpha
are constant,
\lambda(t,x,v)=\lambda_{0}x_{-}
for some positive number
\lambda_{0}
, and
b(t,x,v,g)=g
, which leads to the state

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\bigl(g^{\vartheta}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})-\alpha\lambda_{0}\langle\nu_{t},(\cdot)_{-}\rangle\bigr)\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t},\quad\mathrm{d}\Lambda_{t}=\lambda_{0}(X_{t})_{-}\,\mathrm{d}t

with
\nu_{t}=\mathbb{P}(X_{t}\in\cdot,\,\theta>\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
for
\vartheta\in\mathbb{R}^{d_{\vartheta}}
. We set
G=[0,\infty)
,
f(t,x,\nu,g)=wg
, and
\psi(\nu)=1-\nu(\mathbb{R})
, so the cost functional is

J_{\text{cl}}(g^{\vartheta})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{0}^{T}we^{-\Lambda_{t}}g^{\vartheta}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+L_{T}\biggr],

In the context of the financial model discussed in Section
1.2
, the weight
w>0
captures the trade-off between the cost of capital injection and the cost of insolvencies. We fix
w=5
and set the time horizon
T=1
. The choice of
w
ensures that the running and terminal cost are of the same order of magnitude and that the neural networks produce sensible results. The remaining parameters vary based on the experiment. Following
[
10
]
, we assume the initial condition
\xi
is distributed according to a gamma distribution with density

f(x;k,\theta)=\frac{1}{\Gamma(k)\theta^{k}}x^{k-1}e^{-\frac{x}{\theta}}.

We set the shape
k=6
and scale
\theta=1/60
, meaning that mass is very tightly concentrated around the mean
1/10
and all on the positive half-line. (Here
\theta
is of course not the exponential random variable from above but the scale parameter of the gamma distribution.) For the finite element scheme we truncate space to the interval
[-1,1]
.

We choose feedforward neural networks with two hidden layers for the functions
g^{\vartheta}_{0}
and
g^{\vartheta}_{1}
. The dimension
d_{0}
as well as the size of the hidden layers of
g^{\vartheta}_{0}
are equal to
10
while the number of units in the hidden layers of
g^{\vartheta}_{1}
is
50
. We follow a two-level approach to train the neural networks in each experiment: first, we train for
500
epoch with
n=128
grid points,
m=128
time steps, and
K=128
realisations of the common noise and then a further
100
epochs with
n=256
,
m=256
, and
K=256
. The code for our numerical experiments can be found on GitHub
1
1

1

https://github.com/philkant/systemic-risk-model
.

Next, we discuss the different numerical experiments. We frame the experiments in terms of the financial model that described in Section
1.2
. The particles represent commercial banks with mutual obligations. Killing corresponds to a default of an institution and the contagion results from failure to repay obligations. The control
g^{\vartheta}
can be interpreted as a capital injection by a government, intended to prevent default cascades.

Feedback.
First, we vary the feedback parameter
\alpha
within
\{0.5,1.0,1.5,2.0,2.5\}
while fixing
\lambda_{0}=10
and
\sigma=\sigma_{0}=0.1
. In Figure
1
we report the convergence of the stochastic gradient descent algorithm for the two levels of discretisation. Further, we compare how the running cost

\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}we^{-\Lambda_{t}}g^{\vartheta}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t=\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}w\langle\nu_{t},g^{\vartheta}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t

and terminal cost
\mathbb{E}L_{T}=1-\mathbb{E}\nu_{T}(\mathbb{R})
change for increasing levels of feedback
\alpha
. As expected their sum
J_{\text{cl}}(g^{\vartheta})
becomes larger. However, this trend is fully driven by the running cost whereas the terminal cost at first shrinks for higher
\alpha
. We believe this is because at low levels of feedback, individual insolvencies barely impact the larger network, so the central agent is content with letting institutions default. As the interconnectedness mounts, insolvencies become more costly as they precipitate further defaults, so the controller injects larger levels of capital to prevent default cascades from materialising.

Figure 1
:
Convergence of stochastic gradient descent and cost for different feedback parameters
\alpha
. The dotted line in the left panel indicates the split between the two training regimes with different values for
n
,
m
, and
K
.

Figure 2
:
Heat plot of flow of subprobability distributions
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and the control
(g^{\vartheta}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
for
\alpha=0.5
,
\alpha=2.5
and two different realisations of the common noise
W^{0}
in the upper and lower panel.

We provide a heat plot of the flow of subprobability distributions
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
(or rather their densities) and the control
(g^{\vartheta}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
in Figure
2
for two realisations of the common noise. Bright colours indicate a high concentration of institutions with a given distance-to-breach
X_{t}
(cf. Section
1.2
). The idiosyncratic noise spreads banks apart, while their mean is directed by the common noise, the interaction term
\int_{0}^{t}\alpha\lambda_{0}\langle\nu_{s},(\cdot)_{-}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s
, and the control. The realisation of the second noise (bottom row) is more adverse as it pushes banks below the breach threshold. For the larger feedback parameter
\alpha=2.5
we can clearly perceive the controller’s activity, which attempts to keep institutions above the capital threshold, and as a result squeezes the banks together (cf. the bottom right depiction on the left plot of Figure
2
).

The different levels of activity by the central agent for
\alpha=0.5
and
\alpha=2.5
are contrasted by the heat plot of the control in the right panel of Figure
2
. The controls are of bang-bang type, meaning that a bailout only occurs when a bank breaches the capital threshold, at which point the central agent injects heavily. Finally, we see that an adverse realisation of the noise leads to prolonged activity by the controller.

Correlation.
Next, we vary the correlation
\rho=\frac{\sigma_{0}^{2}}{\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2}}
between the institutions’ outside assets within
\{0.2,0.4,0.6,0.8\}
while keeping the total volatility
\sigma^{2}+\sigma_{0}^{2}
fixed at
0.04
and
\lambda_{0}=10
. We contrast the impact of increased volatility with that of mounting interconnectedness in Figure
3
. While the controller is capable of fighting the endogenous feedback even for large
\alpha
, there is a marked phase transition for asset correlation. As the correlation moves up from
0.6
to
0.8
, the central agent precipitously cuts capital injections. We interpret this as an inability to combat the exogenous effect of the common exposures once they reach sufficiently large levels. In other words, if the controller keeps the number of insolvencies in check, the endogenous feedback
\int_{0}^{t}\alpha\lambda_{0}\langle\nu_{s},(\cdot)_{-}\rangle\,\mathrm{d}s
is contained. However, this has no impact on the exogenous common exposures, which can still drag on banks’ balance sheets.

Figure 3
:
Cost for different correlation parameters
\rho
versus different feedback parameters
\alpha
(note the different scales).

Intensity.
Finally, we vary the intensity
\lambda_{0}
within
\{5,10,25,50\}
while fixing
\alpha=1.5
and
\sigma=\sigma_{0}=0.1
. As the heat plot of the controls in Figure
4
indicates, the control boundary in green, which separates the regions of activity and inactivity of the controller, is pushed outwards for larger intensities, because for large
\lambda_{0}
banks default almost immediately upon breaching the capital threshold. By lifting the control boundary the central agent lowers the risk of breaches by financial institutions. This is also illustrated by the heat plot of the subprobability distribution on the left panel of Figure
4
, where we plot the flow
\nu
for different
\lambda_{0}
and the same realisation of the common noise. For
\lambda_{0}=5
and to a lesser extent
\lambda_{0}=10
breaches are tolerated, whereas for higher intensity parameters institutions are kept above or even at a distance from the threshold.

Figure 4
:
Heat plot of flow of subprobability distributions
\nu=(\nu_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
and the control
(g^{\vartheta}(t,\cdot,\nu_{t}))_{0\leq t\leq T}
for different
\lambda_{0}
. The green lines in the plot on the right-hand side indicate the control boundary.

Appendix A
Appendix

A.1
Existence, Uniqueness and Stability for McKean–Vlasov SDEs with Common Noise under Local Lipschitz Condition

In the following, let us fix a probability space
(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
equipped with two filtrations
\mathbb{G}
and
\mathbb{F}
with
\mathcal{G}_{t}\subset\mathcal{F}_{t}
for
t\in[0,T]
, an
\mathcal{F}_{0}
-measurable
\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
-valued random variable
\xi
for
d_{X}\geq 1
, and two
d_{W}
-dimensional
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motions
W
and
W^{0}
for
d_{W}\geq 1
. We assume that
W^{0}
is adapted to
\mathbb{G}
and the pair
(\xi,W)
is independent of
\mathcal{G}_{T}
. Finally, we require that for all
t\in[0,T]
we have
\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{T})
a.s. for all
A\in\mathcal{F}_{t}\lor\sigma(W)
.

Let
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
be the space of square-integrable measures on
[0,T]\times G
, for a non-empty and closed subset
G\subset\mathbb{R}^{d}
with
d\geq 1
, which have the Lebesgue measure as their first marginal. We say that a
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variable
\Gamma
is
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable if for all
t\in[0,T]
, the random variable
\Gamma([0,s]\times A)
is
\mathcal{F}_{t}
-measurable for any
s\in[0,t]
and
A\in\mathcal{B}(G)
. Fix such a random measure
\Gamma
and assume that
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)<\infty
. Then we consider the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\mathrm{d}X_{t}=\int_{G}b(t,X_{t},\mu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)+\sigma(t,X_{t},\mu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}(t,X_{t},\mu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t},

(A.1)

started from
X_{0}=\xi
with
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
. The coefficients are functions
b\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\times G\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
and
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}\times d_{W}}
. Here
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
denotes the space of square-integrable probability measures on
\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
equipped with the
2
-Wasserstein distance
W_{2}
.

The pair
(X,\mu)
is called a
strong solution
of McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) if (i) the process
X
is a strong solution to (
A.1
) when viewed as an SDE with random coefficients, the randomness coming from the mean-field component
\mu_{t}
, and (ii)
\mu_{t}
is the conditional law of
X_{t}
with respect to
\mathcal{G}_{T}
, so there is no additional information other than
\mathbb{G}
in the conditioning. Under the above hypothesis
\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{t})=\mathbb{P}(A|\mathcal{G}_{T})
for all
A\in\mathcal{F}_{t}\lor\sigma(W)
, we have
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},|\mathcal{G}_{t})
, so that
\mu
is
\mathbb{G}
-adapted.

Assumption A.1
.

Let
b\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\times G\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
and
\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}\times d_{W}}
be measurable and
\mathcal{L}(\xi)\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
. We assume there exists a constant
C>0
, such that

(i)

the norms of the coefficients
\sigma
and
\sigma_{0}
are bounded by
C
and for all
t
,
x
,
\mu
,
g
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b(t,x,\mu,g)\rvert\leq C(1+\lvert x\rvert+M_{2}(\mu)+\lvert g\rvert);

(ii)

the coefficient
b
is continuous in
g
and for
h=\sigma
,
\sigma_{0}
and all
t
,
x
,
x^{\prime}
,
\mu
,
\mu^{\prime}
,
g
we have

\displaystyle\lvert b(t,x,\mu,g)-b(t,x^{\prime},\mu^{\prime}

\displaystyle,g)\rvert+\lvert h(t,x,\nu)-h(t,x^{\prime},\nu^{\prime})\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C\bigl(1+M_{2}^{2}(\mu)\land M_{2}^{2}(\mu^{\prime})\bigr)(\lvert x-x^{\prime}\rvert+W_{2}(\mu,\mu^{\prime})).

Proposition A.2
.

Let Assumption
A.1
be satisfied. Then for any
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued
\Gamma
with
\mathbb{E}\int_{0}^{T}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)<\infty
, the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) has a unique strong solution. If
b
does not depend on
g
, i.e.
b(t,x,m,g)=b_{0}(t,x,m)
for some function
b_{0}\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
, then
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|W^{0})
for all
t\in[0,T]
almost surely.

Moreover, if
(\epsilon_{n})_{n}
is a sequence of positive real numbers tending to zero and
(\Gamma_{n})_{n\geq 1}
is a sequence of
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable processes such that
\mathbb{E}W_{2}^{2}(\Gamma_{n},\Gamma)\to 0
, then
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
. Here
X^{n}
is the unique strong solution to SDE (
A.1
) started from
\xi
at
\epsilon_{n}
with input
\Gamma_{n}
.

In the statement of the proposition starting
X^{n}
from
\xi
at time
\epsilon_{n}
means that
X^{n}
solves the SDE (
A.1
) on the interval
[\epsilon_{n},T]
with initial condition
X^{n}_{\epsilon_{n}}=\xi
. We extend
X^{n}
to the whole interval
[0,T]
by setting
X^{n}_{t}=\xi
for
t\in[0,\epsilon_{n})
. Here for a continuous function
h\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
we denote by
\lvert h\rvert^{\ast}_{T}
the running supremum
\sup_{0\leq t\leq T}\lvert h_{t}\rvert
.

Proof of Proposition
A.2
.

Existence and uniqueness
: Our proof strategy is as follows: we introduce a sequence of nonlinearities
b^{n}
,
\sigma^{n}
, and
\sigma_{0}^{n}
,
n\geq 1
, which coincide with
b
,
\sigma
, and
\sigma_{0}
for elements
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
with
M_{2}(\mu)\leq n
and are uniformly Lipschitz continuous in
x
and
\mu
. Then, we replace the coefficients of the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) by
b^{n}
,
\sigma^{n}
, and
\sigma_{0}^{n}
and obtain a unique strong solution
\tilde{X}^{n}
. Clearly, up to the first time
\varrho_{n}
at which the square of the second moment of
\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
exceeds
n
,
X^{n}
solves the original SDE (
A.1
). Hence, setting
X
equal to
\tilde{X}^{n}
on the interval
[0,\varrho_{n}]
yields the desired solution. Let us expand on this sketch.

Let
B_{n}(0)
denote the centered ball of radius
n
in the space
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
and let
\pi_{n}
be the projection onto
B_{n}(0)
, that is

\pi_{n}(X)=\biggl(\frac{n}{\lVert X\rVert_{L^{2}}}\land 1\biggr)X\quad\text{for }X\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}).

The map
\pi_{n}
is
1
-Lipschitz continuous and we can push it forward to
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
through the map
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\to\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
,
X\mapsto\mathcal{L}(X)
. That is, we define
\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})\to\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
by
\pi^{\ast}_{n}(\mu)=\mathcal{L}(\pi_{n}(X))
, where
X
is a random variable in
L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
with law
\mu
. Note that this map is well-defined, since
\mathcal{L}(\pi_{n}(X))=\mathcal{L}(\pi_{n}(Y))
whenever
X
,
Y\in L^{2}(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P};\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
have the same law and it inherits the
1
-Lipschitz continuity from
\pi_{n}
. Indeed, let
\mu
and
\nu\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}})
and choose random variables
X
and
Y
with laws
\mu
and
\nu
, respectively, such that
W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu)=\mathbb{E}\lvert X-Y\rvert^{2}
. Then we have

W_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu,\pi^{\ast}_{n}\nu)\leq\mathbb{E}\lvert\pi_{n}(X)-\pi_{n}(Y)\rvert^{2}\leq\mathbb{E}\lvert X-Y\rvert^{2}=W_{2}^{2}(\mu,\nu).

Now, we set
b^{n}(t,x,\mu,g)=b(t,x,\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu,g)
and similarly define
\sigma^{n}
and
\sigma_{0}^{n}
. Then it follows from Assumption
A.1

(ii)
that

\displaystyle\lvert b^{n}(t,x_{1},\mu_{1},g)

\displaystyle-b^{n}(t,x_{2},\mu_{2},g)\rvert

\displaystyle=\bigl\lvert b(t,x_{1},\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{1},g)-b(t,x_{2},\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{2},g)\bigr\rvert

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}\bigl(1+M_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{1})+M_{2}^{2}(\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{1})\bigr)\bigl(\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert+W_{2}(\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{1},\pi^{\ast}_{n}\mu_{2})\bigr)

\displaystyle\leq C_{b}(1+2n)(\lvert x_{1}-x_{2}\rvert+W_{2}(\mu_{1},\mu_{2}))

with obvious modifications for
\sigma^{n}
and
\sigma_{0}^{n}
. Moreover, all three coefficients satisfy the linear growth condition stated in Assumption
A.1

(i)
, so that the McKean–Vlasov SDE

\mathrm{d}\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}=\int_{G}b^{n}(t,\tilde{X}^{n}_{t},\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)+\sigma^{n}(t,\tilde{X}^{n}_{t},\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W_{t}+\sigma_{0}^{n}(t,\tilde{X}^{n}_{t},\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t},

started from
\tilde{X}^{n}_{0}=\xi
with
\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
is in the standard Lipschitz regime. Consequently, it has a unique strong solution
\tilde{X}^{n}
.

Define
\varrho_{n}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t})\geq n\}
. Then
\varrho_{n}
is a
\mathbb{G}
-stopping time, so that

\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{n}}=\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{s}|\mathcal{G}_{T})|_{s=t\land\varrho_{n}}=\mathcal{L}(\tilde{X}^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{n}}|\mathcal{G}_{T}).

Consequently, if we set
Y^{n}=\tilde{X}^{n}_{\cdot\land\varrho_{n}}
and
\nu^{n}=\tilde{\mu}^{n}_{\cdot\land\varrho_{n}}
, then for
t\in[0,\varrho_{n}]
it holds that

Y^{n}_{t}=\int_{[0,t]\times G}b(s,Y^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(s,g)+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma(s,Y^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W_{s}+\int_{0}^{t}\sigma_{0}(s,Y^{n}_{s},\nu^{n}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{s}

with
\nu^{n}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(Y^{n}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
. Hence,
Y^{n}
is a solution to the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) on the interval
[0,\varrho_{n}]
. Since
Y^{n}
is unique, we have that
Y^{n}=Y^{m}
on
[0,\varrho_{m}]
and
\varrho_{m}\leq\varrho_{n}
whenever
n\geq m
. This allows us to define the process
X
by
X_{t}=Y^{n}_{t}
if
t\in[0,\varrho_{n}]
. Clearly,
X
solves SDE (
A.1
) up to
\varrho=\lim_{n\to\infty}\varrho_{n}
. However,
\varrho_{n}
is the first time that
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
is equal to or greater than
n
and a simple Grönwall estimate, which relies on the linear growth condition from Assumption
A.1

(i)
implies that
\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\land\varrho}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s})<\infty
for any
t\geq 0
. Now assume that
\mathbb{P}(\varrho<\infty)>0
. Then, we can find a large enough
t\geq 0
such that
p=\mathbb{P}(\varrho\leq t)>0
. Further, we can choose
n\geq 1
with
pn>\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\land\varrho}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s})
. But this leads to the contradiction

pn>\mathbb{E}\sup_{0\leq s\leq t\land\varrho}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{s})\geq\mathbb{E}[\mathbf{1}_{\varrho_{n}\leq t}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{\varrho_{n}})]\geq\mathbb{P}(\varrho_{n}\leq t)n\geq\mathbb{P}(\varrho\leq t)n\geq pn.

Hence, it must hold that
\varrho=\infty
almost surely.

Finally, we remark that uniqueness of
X
immediately follows from the uniqueness of the
Y^{n}
on the intervals
[0,\varrho_{n}]
.

\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
-
adaptedness
: In the case that
b
does not depend on
g
, let us replace the filtration
\mathbb{G}
by
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
. Note that
\mathbb{F}^{W^{0}}
verifies the same conditions as
\mathbb{G}
outlined at the beginning of the section. Consequently, we can apply the existence and uniqueness result we just established to obtain a strong solution
(X^{0},\mu^{0})
to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) with
\mu^{0}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|W^{0})
. Now, if we can prove that
\mu^{0}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
, then
(X^{0},\mu^{0})
also solves the McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) for the filtration
\mathbb{G}
, so by the pathwise uniqueness we must have
(X,\mu)=(X^{0},\mu^{0})
, which implies that
\mu_{t}=\mu^{0}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|W^{0})=\mathcal{L}(X_{t}|W^{0})
as required. To show
\mu^{0}_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
, we use that
X^{0}
is a strong solution to McKean–Vlasov SDE (
A.1
) so that there exists a measurable function
S\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times C([0,T])\times C([0,T])\to\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}
with
X^{0}_{t}=S(X_{0},W,W^{0})
. Since
(X_{0},W)\perp\mathcal{G}_{T}
and
W^{0}
is
\mathcal{G}_{T}
-measurable we have for any bounded and measurable map
\varphi\mathpunct{:}\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\to\mathbb{R}
that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X^{0}_{t})|\mathcal{G}_{T}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(S(X_{0},W,W^{0})|\mathcal{G}_{T}]

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times C([0,T])}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(S(x,w,W^{0})|\mathcal{G}_{T}]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(X_{0},W)(x,w)

\displaystyle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times C([0,T])}\mathbb{E}[\varphi(S(x,w,W^{0})|W^{0}]\,\mathrm{d}\mathcal{L}(X_{0},W)(x,w)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(S(X_{0},W,W^{0})|W^{0}]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(X^{0}_{t})|W^{0}].

This readily implies
\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})=\mathcal{L}(X^{0}_{t}|W^{0})=\mu^{0}_{t}
.

Stability
: Let
(X^{n})_{n}
be as in the statement of the proposition. Note that the family
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}
,
n\geq 1
, is uniformly integrable as the same is true for
(M_{2}^{2}(\Gamma_{n}))_{n}
by the
L^{2}
-convergence of the sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
. Now, we proceed in two steps. First, we prove that the difference
\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert^{2}
tends to zero in expectation. Then, combining this with the easily established tightness of
(X^{n})_{n}
on
C([0,T])
we conclude that
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\to 0
.

For the first step, recall that
\varrho_{k}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}M_{2}^{2}(\tilde{\mu}^{k}_{t})\geq k\}=\inf\{t>0\mathpunct{:}M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t})\geq k\}
. Now, using Assumption
A.1
, elementary SDE estimates show that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\lvert

\displaystyle X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\leq C_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{s\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{s\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}s+2\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,t\land\varrho_{k}]\times G}b(s,X_{s},\mu_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}(\Gamma_{n}-\Gamma)(s,g)\biggr\rvert^{2}

\displaystyle\ \ \ +C\epsilon_{n}(1+\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2})+C\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,\epsilon_{n}]\times G}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)\biggr\rvert^{2},

with constants
C
,
C_{k}>0
which do not depend on
n\geq 1
. The expressions in the third line capture the difference in starting time between
X^{n}
and
X
, whereas the integrals in the second line bound the error between the two solutions as it unfolds over time. Let us define the quantities
M_{n}=C\epsilon_{n}(1+\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2})+C\mathbb{E}\bigl\lvert\int_{[0,\epsilon_{n}]\times G}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)\bigr\rvert^{2}
and
D^{n,k}_{t}=\bigl\lvert\int_{[0,t\land\varrho_{k}]\times G}b(s,X_{s},\mu_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}(\Gamma_{n}-\Gamma)(s,g)\bigr\rvert^{2}
. By Grönwall’s inequality we have

\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}\leq C(M_{n}+\mathbb{E}D^{n,k}_{t})+C_{k}\int_{0}^{t}\mathbb{E}D^{n,k}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s,

(A.2)

where we enlarge
C_{k}
if necessary. Note that the integral term appears because
s\mapsto\mathbb{E}D^{n,k}_{s}
is not necessarily nondecreasing. Clearly,
M_{n}
tends to zero as
n\to\infty
. Moreover, since
\Gamma^{n}
converges to
\Gamma
on
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
in
L^{2}
, it follows from Lemma
A.7
that
D^{n,k}_{t}\to 0
in probability. Moreover, we have that

D^{n,k}_{s}\leq C\bigl(1+(\lvert X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}+\mathbb{E}[(\lvert X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}|\mathcal{G}_{T}]\bigr)+C\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,T]\times G}\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}(\Gamma_{n}+\Gamma)(s,g)\biggr\rvert^{2},

and the expression on the right-hand side is uniformly integrable in
n\geq 1
by the uniform square-integrability of the family
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
. Thus, Vitali’s convergence theorem implies that the expression on the right-hand side of Equation (
A.2
) converges to zero as
n\to\infty
, whence
\lim_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}=0
.

From this, we can deduce that
\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert^{2}
tends to zero in probability. Indeed, fix
\delta
,
\epsilon>0
and choose
k\geq 1
such that
\mathbb{P}(\varrho_{k}\leq t)\leq\epsilon/2
and then
N\geq 1
large enough such that
\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}\leq\delta\epsilon/2
for
n\geq N
. Then, we have that

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}(\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert^{2}>\delta)

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{P}\bigl(\lvert X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}>\delta\bigr)+\mathbb{P}(\varrho_{k}\leq t)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{1}{\delta}\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t\land\varrho_{k}}-X_{t\land\varrho_{k}}\rvert^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{2}=\epsilon

for
n\geq N
. Since
\delta
and
\epsilon
were arbitrary this shows
\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert^{2}
converges to zero in probability. Finally, we know that the variables
(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}
,
n\geq 1
, are uniformly integrable, so by Vitali’s convergence theorem we conclude that
\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert^{2}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
.

Next, we strengthen the pointwise
L^{2}
-convergence to convergence in
L^{2}
-
\sup
. Since the diffusion coefficients are bounded and the sequence
(\Gamma_{n})_{n}
is uniformly square-integrable, it follows easily from Kolmogorov’s tightness criterion that the family
(X^{n})_{n}
is tight on
C([0,T])
. Consequently, for any
\delta>0
we can find a modulus of continuity
\omega_{\delta}\mathpunct{:}[0,\infty)\to[0,\infty)
, such that if we let
\mathcal{K}_{\delta}
be the set of function in
C([0,T])
with modulus
\omega_{\delta}
, it holds that
\mathbb{P}(X^{n}\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta})\geq 1-\delta
and
\mathbb{P}(X\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta})\geq 1-\delta
. Next, fix an
\epsilon>0
. Since the sequence
(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}
,
n\geq 1
is uniformly integrable, we can pick
\delta>0
sufficiently small such that

\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{X^{n}\notin\mathcal{K}_{\delta}}(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}+\mathbf{1}_{X\notin\mathcal{K}_{\delta}}(\lvert X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\bigr]\leq\frac{\epsilon}{3}

(A.3)

for all
n
larger than some appropriately chosen
N\geq 1
. Finally, let us choose
\epsilon_{0}>0
with the property that
\omega_{\delta}(\epsilon_{0})\leq\sqrt{\epsilon/6}
and define
t_{k}=k\epsilon_{0}
for
k=1
, …,
T/\epsilon_{0}
(where we assume for simplicity that
T/\epsilon_{0}
is an integer). Then, increasing
N
if necessary, we have that
\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t_{k}}-X_{t_{k}}\rvert^{2}\leq\frac{\epsilon\epsilon_{0}}{6T}
for
k=1
, …,
T/\epsilon_{0}
and all
n\geq N
by the pointwise
L^{2}
-convergence. Now, on the set
\{X^{n},X\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta}\}
, it holds that

(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})\leq\biggl(\sup_{1\leq k\leq T/\epsilon_{0}}\lvert X^{n}_{t_{k}}-X_{t_{k}}\rvert+\frac{\sqrt{\epsilon}}{\sqrt{6}}\biggr)^{2}\leq 2\sup_{1\leq k\leq T/\epsilon_{0}}\lvert X^{n}_{t_{k}}-X_{t_{k}}\rvert^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}.

Hence, we see that for all
n\geq N
,

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{X^{n},X\in\mathcal{K}_{\delta}\}}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\bigr]+\mathbb{E}\bigl[\mathbf{1}_{\{X^{n},X\notin\mathcal{K}_{\delta}\}}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}\sup_{1\leq k\leq T/\epsilon_{0}}\lvert X^{n}_{t_{k}}-X_{t_{k}}\rvert^{2}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}+\frac{\epsilon}{3}

\displaystyle\leq 2\sum_{k=1}^{T/\epsilon_{0}}\mathbb{E}\lvert X^{n}_{t_{k}}-X_{t_{k}}\rvert^{2}+\frac{2\epsilon}{3}=\epsilon,

where we applied the bound from (
A.3
) in the second equality. This implies the desired convergence of
\mathbb{E}(\lvert X^{n}-X\rvert^{\ast}_{T})^{2}
to zero.
∎

A.2
Equivalence of the Open- and Closed Loop Formulation of the McKean–Vlasov Control Problem

In this subsection we briefly address the so-called closed-loop formulation, in which the control
\gamma=(\gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
takes the form
\gamma_{t}=g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})
for a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to G
, where
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|W^{0})
. In what follows, we will briefly discuss how we can apply the theory developed in Lacker, Shkolnikov, and Zhang
[
31
]
to establish the equivalence between the relaxed formulation and the closed-loop formulation. Since the strong and the relaxed formulation are equivalent, this shows that all three formulation yield the same value
V
.

First, let us note that for an arbitrary measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to G
we cannot guarantee the strong well-posedness of McKean-Vlasov SDE (
2.5
) with
\gamma_{t}
replaced by
g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})
, so we have to switch to a weak control framework. Let us introduce the function
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to\mathcal{M}_{\leq 1}^{2}(\mathbb{R})
defined by

\langle\Phi(m),\varphi\rangle=\int_{\mathbb{R}^{2}}e^{-(y\land 0)}\varphi(x)\,\mathrm{d}m(x,y)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
and
m\in\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
. The following definition mirrors Definition 8.1 in
[
31
]
.

Definition A.3
.

A
weak relaxed control
is a tuple
\mathfrak{g}=(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P},W,W^{0},\mu,X,\Gamma)
such that

(i)

(\Omega,\mathcal{F},\mathbb{P})
is a filtered probability space and
\mathbb{G}
is a subfiltration of
\mathbb{F}
;

(ii)

W
and
W^{0}
are
\mathbb{F}
-Brownian motions,
W^{0}
is
\mathbb{G}
-adapted,
\mu
is a continuous
\mathbb{G}
-adapted
\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
-valued process,
X
is a continuous real-valued
\mathbb{F}
-adapted processes, and
\Gamma
is a
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued process;

(iii)

X_{0}
,
W
, and
\mathcal{G}_{T}
are independent and for all
t\in[0,T]
it holds that
(X_{\cdot\land t},\Gamma_{t})\perp\mathcal{F}^{W}_{t}\lor\mathcal{G}_{T}
conditional on
\mathcal{F}^{W}_{t}\lor\mathcal{G}_{t}
;

(iv)

it holds that
\mathbb{E}\bigl[\int_{[0,T]\times G}\lvert X_{t}\rvert^{2}+M_{2}^{2}(\mu_{t})+\lvert g\rvert^{2}\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)\bigr]<\infty
;

(v)

for all
t\in[0,T]
,

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathrm{d}X_{t}&=\int_{G}b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)+\sigma(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t+\sigma_{0}(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\,\mathrm{d}W^{0}_{t}\\
&\ \ \ -\alpha(t,X_{t},\nu_{t})\langle\nu_{t},\lambda(t,\cdot,\nu_{t})\rangle\,\mathrm{d}t\end{split}

(A.4)

and
\mu_{t}=\mathcal{L}(X_{t},\Lambda_{t}|\mathcal{G}_{T})
, where
\Lambda_{t}=\int_{0}^{t}\lambda(s,X_{s},\nu_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
and
\nu_{t}=\Phi(\mu_{t})
.

The
cost
of a weak control rule
\mathfrak{g}
is defined as

J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g})=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{[0,T]\times G}e^{-\Lambda_{t}}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)+\psi(\nu_{T})\biggr].

(A.5)

We say that
\mathfrak{g}
is a
weak control
if
\Gamma
is given by
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma_{t}}\mathrm{d}t
for an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process
\gamma
. Finally, we call a weak control a
(weak) closed-loop control
if there exists a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to G
such that
\gamma_{t}=g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})
for
\mathbb{P}\otimes[0,T]
-a.e.
(t,\omega)\in[0,T]\times\Omega
.

In Item
(ii)
the
\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
-valued random variable
\Gamma_{t}
is defined by
\Gamma_{t}(A\times B)=\Gamma((A\cap[0,t])\times B)+\lvert A\cap[t,T]\rvert\delta_{g_{0}}(B)
for some fixed
g_{0}\in G
.

Since any strong control
\gamma
as defined at the beginning of this section, induces a weak control
\mathfrak{g}
, we can easily see that the infimum
V_{\text{wk}}
of
J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g})
over all weak controls
\mathfrak{g}
is at most
V
. On the other hand, any weak control induces a relaxed control in the sense of Definition
2.7
(see
[
13
, Proposition 2.18]
for details), so that the value
V_{0}
for relaxed control rules is upper bounded by
V_{\text{wk}}
. Lastly, Theorem
2.8
says that
V_{0}=V
, so in total we get
V_{0}\leq V_{\text{wk}}\leq V=V_{0}
, so that all of these quantities are in fact equal.

Assumption A.4
.

For each
(t,x,m)\in[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})
the set

\Bigl\{\bigl(b(t,x,\Phi(m),g),z\bigr)\mathpunct{:}z\in\mathbb{R},\,g\in G,\,z\geq f(t,x,\Phi(m),g)\Bigr\}

is closed and convex.

Proposition A.5
.

Let Assumptions
2.2
and
A.4
be satisfied. Then there exists an optimal closed-loop control
\mathfrak{g}
.

Proof.

Under Assumption
A.4
, by
[
31
, Theorem 8.3]
if
\mathfrak{g}=(\Omega,\mathbb{F},\mathbb{G},\mathbb{P},W,W^{0},\mu,X,\Gamma)
is a weak control, then we can find a weak closed-loop control
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
with
J_{\text{wk}}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\leq J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g})
. It is not difficult to show that the same is true for weak relaxed controls. Indeed, the only occasion where the control process appears explicitly in the proof of
[
31
, Theorem 8.3]
is in a measurable selection argument at the beginning, which ensures the existence of a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to G
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}b\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})\bigr)&=\mathbb{E}\bigl[b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\big|X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t}\bigr]\\
f\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})\bigr)&\leq\mathbb{E}\bigl[f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},\gamma_{t})\big|X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t}\bigr],\end{split}

(A.6)

where
\gamma
is a
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
G
-valued process such that
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\delta_{\gamma_{t}}\mathrm{d}t
. However, this argument still works if the weak control
\mathfrak{g}
is relaxed. In that case, we can find an
\mathbb{F}
-progressively measurable
\mathcal{P}^{2}(G)
-valued process
(\Gamma_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
, see e.g.
[
29
, Lemma 3.2]
, such that
\mathrm{d}\Gamma(t,g)=\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t}(g)\mathrm{d}t
. Then a measurable selection argument similar to the one above allows us to find a measurable function
g\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times\mathbb{R}^{2}\times\mathcal{P}^{2}(\mathbb{R}^{2})\to G
such that

\displaystyle\begin{split}b\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})\bigr)&=\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{G}b(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t}(g)\bigg|X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t}\biggr]\\
f\bigl(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g(t,X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t})\bigr)&\leq\mathbb{E}\biggl[\int_{G}f(t,X_{t},\nu_{t},g)\,\mathrm{d}\Gamma_{t}(g)\bigg|X_{t},\Lambda_{t},\mu_{t}\biggr].\end{split}

(A.7)

Details can be found in the proof of Theorem 2.3 in
[
30
]
(in the absence of common noise). With the measurable selection argument extended to weak relaxed controls, we may simply follow the remainder of the proof of
[
31
, Theorem 8.3]
. It tells us that we can find a weak closed-loop control
\tilde{\mathfrak{g}}
with
J_{\text{wk}}(\tilde{\mathfrak{g}})\leq J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g})
. Hence, to conclude the proof of the proposition it is enough to establish the existence of an optimal weak relaxed control.

By Theorem
2.8
there exists an optimal relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}
in the sense of Definition
2.7
. Let
\mathbb{P}_{\ast}
denote the probability distribution on
(\Omega_{\ast},\mathcal{F}_{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast})
associated to
\mathbb{P}_{0}
via Equation
2.7
. Then, using the notation from Section
2.1
, we set

\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}=(\Omega_{\ast},\mathbb{F}^{\ast},\mathbb{P}_{\ast},\mathbb{F}^{0},W^{\ast},B^{\ast},\tilde{\mu}^{\ast},X^{\ast},\Gamma^{\ast}),

with
\tilde{\mu}^{\ast}=(\tilde{\mu}^{\ast}_{t})_{0\leq t\leq T}
defined by
\tilde{\mu}_{t}=\mathcal{L}^{\mathbb{P}_{\ast}}(X^{\ast}_{t},\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}|\mathcal{F}^{0}_{T})
, where
\Lambda^{\ast}_{t}=\lambda(s,X^{\ast}_{s},\nu^{\ast}_{s})\,\mathrm{d}s
. We claim that
\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
is a weak relaxed control. Indeed, all items of Definition
A.3
except
(iii)
follow immediately from Definition
2.7
. But by
[
31
, Remark 8.2]
, because
\mathbb{F}^{\ast}
is the filtration generated by
X^{\ast}
,
W^{\ast}
,
\Gamma^{\ast}
, and
\mathbb{F}^{0}
, Item
(ii)
of Definition
2.7
implies Definition
A.3

(ii)
. Clearly, the cost
J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})
of the relaxed control rule
\mathbb{P}_{0}
coincides with
J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})
, so that
J_{\text{wk}}(\mathfrak{g}^{\ast})=J_{0}(\mathbb{P}_{0})=V_{0}=V_{\text{wk}}
, which means that
\mathfrak{g}^{\ast}
is optimal.
∎

A.3
Technical Results for Section
3

Lemma A.6

(Asymptotic independence)
.

Let
E_{1}
,
E_{2}
and
F
be Polish spaces and fix two sequences
(X_{i})_{i}
and
(Y_{i})_{i}
random variables, which take values in
E_{1}
and
E_{2}
, respectively. Assume that
(Y_{i})_{i}
are i.i.d. and that the two sequences are independent of each other. Next, for each
N\geq 1
and
i=1
, …,
N
, let
g_{N,i}\mathpunct{:}E_{1}^{N}\times\mathcal{P}(E_{1}\times E_{2})\to F
be measurable and define
\mu_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{g_{N,i}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N},\tilde{\mu}_{N}),Y_{i}}
with
\tilde{\mu}_{N}=\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\delta_{X_{i},Y_{i}}
. Then for any subsequential weak limit
\mu
of
\mu_{N}
(if one exists), it holds that
\mu=\mu^{1}\otimes\mu^{2}
almost surely, where
\mu^{1}
and
\mu^{2}
are the first and second marginal of
\mu
, respectively.

Proof.

For simplicity, we assume that
\mu_{N}
converges weakly to
\mu
along the full sequences and we assume that the sequence
(\mu_{N})_{N}
and
\mu
are defined on the same probability space. We show that for any
\varphi_{1}\in C_{b}(F)
and
\varphi_{2}\in C_{b}(E_{2})
, we have

\langle\mu,\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\rangle=\langle\mu^{1},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\mu^{2},\varphi_{2}\rangle

almost surely. This implies
\mu=\mu^{1}\otimes\mu^{2}
a.s. by a simple monotone class argument. Set
\Phi_{N,i}=\varphi_{1}(g_{N,i}(X_{1},\dots,X_{N},\tilde{\mu}_{n}))
and let
\mu^{2}_{N}
denote the second marginal of
\mu_{N}
. Then it holds that

\displaystyle\begin{split}\mathbb{E}\bigl\lvert\langle\mu&,\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\rangle-\langle\mu^{1},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\mu^{2},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr\rvert^{2}\\
&=\lim\limits_{n\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{N,i}\varphi_{2}(Y_{i})-\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\Phi_{N,i}\varphi_{2}(Y_{j})\biggr\rvert^{2}\\
&=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\mathbb{E}\biggl\lvert\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\Phi_{N,i}\bigl(\varphi_{2}(Y_{i})-\langle\mu^{2}_{N},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr)\biggr\rvert^{2}\\
&=\lim\limits_{N\to\infty}\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi_{N,i}\Phi_{N,j}\Delta_{N,i}\Delta_{N,j}\bigr],\end{split}

(A.8)

where
\Delta_{N,i}=\varphi_{2}(Y_{j})-\langle\mu^{2}_{N},\varphi_{2}\rangle
. Since
\Phi_{N,i}
is a symmetric function of
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{N}
, and the sequence
(Y_{k})_{k}
is independent of
(X_{k})_{k}
, the quantity
\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}=\mathbb{E}[\Phi_{N,i}\Phi_{N,j}|Y_{1},\dots,Y_{N}]
is symmetric in
Y_{1}
, …,
Y_{N}
as well. Consequently, whenever
i\neq j
, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi_{N,i}\Phi_{N,j}\Delta_{N,i}\Delta_{N,j}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}\Delta_{N,1}\Delta_{N,2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=\frac{1}{N-1}\sum_{k=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}\Delta_{N,1}\Delta_{N,k}\bigr]-\frac{1}{N-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}\Delta_{N,1}^{2}\bigr]

\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{N-1}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}\Delta_{N,1}^{2}\bigr],

since
\sum_{k=1}^{N}\Delta_{N,k}=0
. Hence, we get

\displaystyle\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i,j=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi_{N,i}\Phi_{N,j}\Delta_{N,i}\Delta_{N,j}\bigr]=\frac{1}{N^{2}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\Phi_{N,i}^{2}\Delta_{N,i}^{2}\bigr]-\frac{1}{N^{2}(N-1)}\sum_{i\neq j}\mathbb{E}\bigl[\bar{\Phi}^{N}_{i,j}\Delta_{N,1}^{2}\bigr].

Both terms on the right-hand side are
O(\frac{1}{N})
, so in view of Equation (
A.8
), we obtain
\mathbb{E}\bigl\lvert\langle\mu,\varphi_{1}\otimes\varphi_{2}\rangle-\langle\mu^{1},\varphi_{1}\rangle\langle\mu^{2},\varphi_{2}\rangle\bigr\rvert^{2}=0
.
∎

Lemma A.7

(Continuity of integral operator)
.

Let
(E,\rho)
be a complete separable metric space and
\Phi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times E\times G\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function, such that
(x,g)\mapsto\Phi(t,x,g)
is continuous for every
t\in[0,T]
. Suppose that
\lvert\Phi(t,x,g)\rvert\leq C_{\Phi}(1+\rho^{p}(x,x_{\ast})+\lvert g\rvert^{p})
for some
1\leq p\leq 2
and a fixed
x_{\ast}\in E
. Then the map

[0,T]\times L^{p}([0,T];E)\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\ni(t,x,\mathfrak{g})\mapsto\int_{[0,t]\times G}\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)

is continuous.

Here
G
is a non-empty closed subset of
\mathbb{R}^{d}
and
L^{p}([0,T];E)
is the space of (equivalence classes of) measurable maps
x\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\to E
for which
\int_{0}^{T}\rho^{p}(x_{t},x_{\ast})\,\mathrm{d}t<\infty
. We equip
L^{p}([0,T];E)
with the metric

E\times E\ni(x,y)\mapsto\biggl(\int_{0}^{T}\rho^{p}(x_{t},y_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\biggr)^{1/p}.

Note that if
(x^{n})_{n}
converges in
L^{p}([0,T];E)
, then the family
((\rho^{p}(x_{t},x_{\ast}))_{t})_{n}
is uniformly integrable.

Proof of Lemma
A.7
.

Fix
(t,x,\mathfrak{g})\in[0,T]\times L^{p}([0,T];E)\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)
and assume that the sequence
(t_{n},x^{n},\mathfrak{g}^{n})_{n}
converges to
(t,x,\mathfrak{g})
. For
R>0
let
G_{R}
denote the elements
g
of
G
with
\lvert g\rvert\leq R
. Then we write

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t_{n}]\times G}\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)-\int_{[0,t]\times G}\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)

\displaystyle\leq\biggl\lvert\int_{([0,t]\Delta[0,t_{n}])\times G}\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)\biggr\rvert+\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,t]\times G_{R}^{c}}\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle\ \ \ +\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,t]\times G_{R}}\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)\biggr\rvert+\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,t]\times G}\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathfrak{g}^{n}-\mathfrak{g})(s,g)\biggr\rvert

\displaystyle=I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4},

where
A\Delta B=A\setminus B\cup B\setminus A
for two sets
A
and
B
. First, let us disintegrate the measure
\mathfrak{g}^{n}
as
\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)=\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(g)\mathrm{d}s
for a family of measures
(\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s})_{0\leq s\leq T}
on
G
. Then let
\epsilon>0
. We will prove that we can choose
R>0
and
N\geq 1
, such that
I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}\leq\epsilon
for all
n\geq N
.

By assumption
\lvert\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)\rvert\leq C_{\Phi}(1+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast})+\lvert g\rvert^{p})
. Since
((\rho^{p}(x_{s},x_{\ast}))_{s})_{n}
is uniformly integrable by the discussion below the statement of the lemma and
(\mathfrak{g}^{n})_{n}
is uniformly
p
-integrable as a
W_{2}
-convergent sequence, we can choose
N\geq 1
, such that for all
n\geq N
,

\displaystyle I_{1}

\displaystyle\leq\int_{([0,t]\Delta[0,t_{n}])\times G}C_{\Phi}(1+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast})+\lvert g\rvert^{p})\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)

\displaystyle=\int_{[0,t]\Delta[0,t_{n}]}C_{\Phi}(1+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast}))\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{([0,t]\Delta[0,t_{n}])\times G}C_{\Phi}\lvert g\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g)\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}.

For the second expression
I_{2}
we note that similarly to above

\lvert\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\rvert\leq C_{\Phi}\bigl(2+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast})+\rho^{p}(x_{s},x_{\ast})+2\lvert g\rvert^{p}\bigr).

(A.9)

Consequently, we have

I_{2}\leq\int_{[0,t]}C_{\Phi}\bigl(1+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast})+\rho^{p}(x_{s},x_{\ast})\bigr)\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(G_{R}^{c})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{[0,t]\times G_{R}^{c}}\lvert g\rvert^{p}\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}(s,g).

The second term on the right-hand side can be made arbitrarily small, say smaller than
\frac{\epsilon}{12}
, by choosing
R>0
large enough since
(\mathfrak{g}^{n})_{n}
is uniformly
p
-integrable. Estimating the first one is slightly more involved. Let us define
y^{n}_{t}=C_{\Phi}\bigl(1+\rho^{p}(x^{n}_{s},x_{\ast})+\rho^{p}(x_{s},x_{\ast})\bigr)
, so that
((y^{n}_{t})_{t})_{n}
forms a uniformly integrable family. Further, for
\delta>0
we set
A_{n,R,\delta}=\{s\in[0,t]\mathpunct{:}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(G^{c}_{R})>\delta\}
. Then, replacing
R
by
R\lor 1
if necessary,

\sup_{n\geq 1}\text{Leb}(A_{n,R,\delta})\leq\sup_{n\geq 1}\frac{1}{\delta}\mathfrak{g}^{n}([0,t]\times G_{R}^{c}).

(A.10)

The right-hand side vanishes as
R
tends to infinity by the uniform
p
-integrability of
(\mathfrak{g}^{n})_{n}
. Now we write

\displaystyle\int_{[0,t]}y^{n}_{s}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(G_{R}^{c})\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle=\int_{A_{n,R,\delta}}y^{n}_{s}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(G_{R}^{c})\,\mathrm{d}s+\int_{A_{n,R,\delta}^{c}}y^{n}_{s}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(G_{R}^{c})\,\mathrm{d}s

\displaystyle\leq\int_{A_{n,R,\delta}}y^{n}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s+\delta\int_{[0,t]}y^{n}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s.

Now we first pick
\delta>0
large enough so that the second expression in the second line becomes smaller than
\frac{\epsilon}{12}
. Then, we enlarge
R
sufficiently so that
\int_{A_{n,R,\delta}}y^{n}_{s}\,\mathrm{d}s\leq\frac{\epsilon}{12}
. This is possible since the family
((y^{n}_{t})_{t})_{n}
is uniformly integrable and
\sup_{n\geq 1}\text{Leb}(A_{n,R,\delta})\to 0
as
R\to\infty
by (
A.10
). Putting all these estimates together yields
I_{2}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}
.

For the integrand in
I_{3}
we have

\biggl\lvert\int_{G_{R}}\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}^{n}_{s}(g)\biggr\rvert\leq\sup_{g\in G_{R}}\lvert\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\rvert.

The expression on the right-hand side converges to zero in measure as
n\to\infty
since
\Phi
is continuous in its last two arguments,
G_{R}
is compact, and
x^{n}\to x
in
L^{p}([0,T];E)
. Moreover, appealing to the estimate in Equation (
A.9
) shows that
\bigl(\bigl(\sup_{g\in G_{R}}\lvert\Phi(s,x^{n}_{s},g)-\Phi(s,x_{s},g)\rvert\bigr)_{s}\bigr)_{n}
is uniformly integrable, so that
I_{3}\to 0
as
n\to\infty
by Vitali’s convergence theorem. In particular, enlarging
N\geq 1
if necessary, we have
I_{3}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}
for all
n\geq N
.

For the last expression
I_{4}
, we note that
[0,T]\times G\ni(s,g)\mapsto\varphi(s,g)=\Phi(s,x_{s},g)
is measurable in the first component and continuous in the second. Thus if we define
\varphi_{k}(s,g)=-k\lor\varphi(s,g)\land k
for
k\geq 1
, it follows from
[
25
, Corollary 2.9]
that

\int_{[0,t]\times G}\varphi_{k}(s,g)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathfrak{g}^{n}-\mathfrak{g})(s,g)\to 0

(A.11)

as
n\to\infty
. On the other hand,

\biggl\lvert\int_{[0,t]\times G}\varphi_{k}(s,g)-\varphi(s,g)\,\mathrm{d}(\mathfrak{g}^{n}-\mathfrak{g})(s,g)\biggr\rvert\leq\int_{[0,t]\times G}\mathbf{1}_{\lvert\varphi(s,g)\rvert\geq k}\lvert\varphi(s,g)\rvert\,\mathrm{d}(\mathfrak{g}^{n}+\mathfrak{g})(s,g).

Using once more that
(\mathfrak{g}^{n})_{n}
is uniformly
p
-integrable and
\lvert\varphi(s,g)\rvert\leq C_{\Phi}(1+\rho^{p}(x_{s},x_{\ast})+\lvert g\rvert^{p})
, we conclude that the quantity on the right-hand side above vanishes uniformly in
n\geq 1
as
k\to\infty
. In view of Equation (
A.11
), increasing
N\geq 1
if necessary and choosing
k
large enough, we obtain
I_{4}\leq\frac{\epsilon}{4}
for
n\geq N
. Hence, it holds that
I_{1}+I_{2}+I_{3}+I_{4}\leq\epsilon
as required.
∎

Corollary A.8

(Continuity of integral operator)
.

Let
(E_{i},\rho_{i})
,
i=1
,…,
n
, be a finite collection of complete separable metric space and
\Phi\mathpunct{:}[0,T]\times E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{n}\times G\to\mathbb{R}
be a measurable function, such that
(x_{1},\dots,x_{n},g)\mapsto\Phi(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},g)
is continuous for every
t\in[0,T]
. Suppose that

\lvert\Phi(t,x_{1},\dots,x_{n},g)\rvert\leq C_{\Phi}\bigl(1+\rho_{1}^{p}(x_{1},x_{1,\ast})+\dots+\rho_{1}^{p}(x_{n},x_{n,\ast})+\lvert g\rvert^{p}\bigr)

for some
1\leq p\leq 2
and fixed
x_{i,\ast}\in E_{i}
. Then the map
[0,T]\times D_{E_{1}}[0,T]\times\dots\times D_{E_{n}}[0,T]\times\mathbb{M}_{T}^{2}(G)\to\mathbb{R}
,

(t,x^{1},\dots,x^{n},\mathfrak{g})\mapsto\int_{[0,t]\times G}\Phi(s,x^{1}_{s},\dots,x^{n}_{s},g)\,\mathrm{d}\mathfrak{g}(s,g)

is continuous.

Proof.

Simply note that the inclusion
D_{E_{1}}[0,T]\times\dots\times D_{E_{n}}[0,T]\to L^{2}([0,T];E_{1}\times\dots\times E_{n})
is continuous and apply Lemma
A.7
.
∎

A.4
Technical Results for Section
4

Lemma A.9

(Continuity of subprobability mapping)
.

Let
T>0
. Then the function
\Phi\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])\to L^{2}([0,T];\mathbf{M})
defined by

\langle\Phi(\mu)_{t},\varphi\rangle=\int_{D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]}y_{t}\varphi(x_{t})\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y)

for
\varphi\in C_{b}(\mathbb{R})
,
t\in[0,T]
, and
\mu\in\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
is continuous.

Here
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
denotes the space of probability distributions
\mu
on
D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]
for which

\int_{D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]}\lvert x\rvert^{\ast}_{T}+\lvert y\rvert^{\ast}_{T}\,\mathrm{d}\mu(x,y)<\infty,

where
\lvert\cdot\rvert^{\ast}_{T}
denotes the running supremum over
[0,T]
of the absolute value of a path
[0,T]\to\mathbb{R}
. We equip
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
with the
1
-Wasserstein distance, where
D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]
is endowed with the metric
d_{M1}
defined in Equation (3.7) in
[
43
, Chapter 12]
, which induces the topology of convergence in
M1
on
D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]
.
Note that if a sequence
(\mu^{n})_{n}
in
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
is convergent in
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
, then it is uniformly integrable in the sense that

\sup_{n\geq 1}\int_{D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T]}\mathbf{1}_{\lvert x\rvert^{\ast}_{T}\geq K}\lvert x\rvert^{\ast}_{T}\,\mathrm{d}\mu^{n}(x,y)\to 0

as
K\to\infty
.

Proof of Lemma
A.9
.

Assume that
(\mu^{n})_{n}
converges to
\mu
in
\mathcal{P}^{1}(D[0,T]\times D_{[0,1]}[0,T])
. Then by the Skorokhod representation theorem, we find random variables
(X^{n},I^{n})
,
n\geq 1
, and
(X,I)
such that
(X^{n},I^{n})\sim\mu^{n}
as well as
(X,I)\sim\mu
and
X^{n}\to X
as well as
I^{n}\to I
a.s. in
M1
. Now, by the definition of convergence in
M1
, both
X^{n}_{t}
and
I^{n}_{t}
converge a.s. to
X_{t}
and
I_{t}
for
t
in the cocountable set
\mathbb{T}
of a.s. continuity points of
X
and
I
. From the comment below the statement, we also know that
(\lvert X^{n}\rvert^{\ast}_{T})_{n}
is a uniformly integrable family. Thus, Vitali’s convergence theorem implies that
\mathbb{E}[\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert]\to 0
and
\mathbb{E}[(1+\lvert X_{t}\rvert)\lvert I^{n}_{t}-I_{t}\rvert]\to 0
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
. But, now we estimate

\displaystyle\int_{0}^{T}d_{1}^{2}(\Phi(\mu^{n})_{t},\Phi(\mu)_{t})\,\mathrm{d}t\leq\int_{0}^{T}\bigl(\mathbb{E}[\lvert X^{n}_{t}-X_{t}\rvert]+\mathbb{E}[(1+\lvert X_{t}\rvert)\lvert I^{n}_{t}-I_{t}\rvert]\bigr)^{2}\,\mathrm{d}t,

and the right-hand side vanishes as
n\to\infty
by our previous remarks.
∎

Lemma A.10

(Integral against nondecreasing processes)
.

Let
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]
denote the space of nondecreasing càdlàg functions
\ell\mathpunct{:}[-1,T]\to\mathbb{R}
with
\ell_{t}=0
for
t\in[-1,0)
and equip
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]
with the
M1
-topology. For nonnegative
\varphi\in C([0,T])
define
I^{\varphi}\mathpunct{:}\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]\to D[0,T]
by
I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell)=\int_{0}^{t}\varphi(s)\,\mathrm{d}\ell
. Then
I^{\varphi}
is continuous at any element
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]
, which is continuous at
t=T
.

Proof.

Let
(\ell^{n})_{n}
be a sequence in
\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]
that converges to
\ell\in\mathcal{I}_{0}[-1,T]
which is continuous at
t=T
. By Item (iv) of
[
43
, Theorem 12.5.1]
is is enough to show that
I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell^{n})\to I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell)
for each
t
in a dense subset of
[-1,T]
including
-1
and
T
. First note that we may assume that
\ell^{n}_{T}=\ell_{T}=1
, otherwise replace
\ell^{n}
by
t\mapsto(\ell^{n}_{t}+t_{+})/(\ell^{n}_{T}+T)
, where
t_{+}
denotes the positive part of
t
, and modify
\ell
in an analogous manner. Now, convergence at
t=-1
is obvious since
I^{\varphi}_{0}(\ell^{n})=0=I^{\varphi}_{0}(\ell)
. Next, let
\mathbb{T}
be the set of continuity points of
\ell
, which is dense in
[-1,T]
and contains
T
. We claim that
I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell^{n})\to I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell)
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
. Since
\ell^{n}\to\ell
in
D[-1,T]
, Item (iv)
[
43
, Theorem 12.5.1]
tells us that
\ell^{n}_{t}\to\ell_{t}
for each
t\in\mathbb{T}
. But we can view
\ell^{n}
and
\ell
as cumulative distribution functions of random variables
Y^{n}
and
Y
with values in
[-1,T+1]
. Then
\ell^{n}_{t}\to\ell_{t}
for all
t\in\mathbb{T}
is precisely equivalent to weak convergence of
Y^{n}
to
Y
by the Portmanteau theorem. Finally, we note that for any
t\in\mathbb{T}
, the map
s\mapsto\varphi(s)\mathbf{1}_{s<t}
is
\mathcal{L}(Y)
-a.s. continuous, so by the continuous mapping theorem it holds that
I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell^{n})=\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y^{n})\mathbf{1}_{Y^{n}<t}]\to\mathbb{E}[\varphi(Y)\mathbf{1}_{Y<t}]=I^{\varphi}_{t}(\ell)
.

∎
